Digital Information Dissemination and User Engagement on Social Media: Evidence from Saudi–India Digital Diplomacy

Mahmood Abdulghani Alharbe, Zaher Hamad Abusaq — 2026-12-24 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23360189

AbstractThis study investigates how digital information is disseminated and how users engage with social media communication within the context of Saudi–India digital diplomacy. Attention is given to whether digital information communication, user information engagement, information transparency and source credibility, and strategic digital information dissemination are associated with information reach, access, reception and engagement. A quantitative cross-sectional approach was adopted, with primary data obtained through a structured questionnaire administered to social media users who had encountered diplomatic information concerning Saudi Arabia and India. Following data screening, 192 valid responses were included in the analysis. Structural equation modelling was applied to evaluate a newly developed measurement scale and examine the proposed relationships. The findings demonstrate positive and statistically significant associations between all four examined dimensions—digital information communication, user information engagement, information transparency and source credibility, and strategic digital information dissemination—and information reach, access, reception and engagement. These results underscore the role of credibility, transparency, interactivity, and strategically managed information dissemination in strengthening contemporary digital diplomatic communication. The study further extends the digital diplomacy literature by bringing communication-related and audience-centred dimensions together within an integrated analytical framework.

Representation and Reinforcement Learning for Molecular Dynamics

Zihan Pengmei — 2026-12-01 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.6082/0vqhy-0t164

Molecular dynamics simulations can describe biomolecular motion and kinetics at atomic resolution, but the resulting trajectories are difficult to analyze and may contain few transitions between long-lived metastable states. This dissertation studies two coupled problems: how molecular conformations should be represented to allow automatic analysis and how this representation enables efficient sampling of important conformational space. Denoising pretraining on small-molecule structures provides all-atom geometric graph features that can be reused for molecular and protein learning. The scaling experiments show that greater model size does not reliably predict better transfer; architecture, conformational coverage, and the evaluation split also matter. SubFormer addresses computational cost and long-range communication by applying attention to chemical fragments after local atomic message passing. For trajectory analysis, geom2vec freezes the pretrained encoder in SubFormer, stores its features, and trains token mixers with time-lagged objectives. Analyses of fast-folding proteins test whether these features retain side-chain and folding information. A token-merging extension further reduces computational cost by combining sequence, graph, and attention updates, extending the analysis to large biomolecular complexes. Together, these methods separate the cost of geometric feature extraction from repeated downstream training while retaining structural detail for kinetic analysis. Time-lagged objectives connect these representations to dynamical questions. Slow-coordinate and predictive-state models describe motion recorded in trajectories, while committor analysis addresses the probability of reaching one endpoint before another. These quantities require validation on temporally separated data or independent trajectories: a structural embedding alone does not establish a transition pathway or determine a rate. GESPO uses group relative policy optimization and a geom2vec model to allocate a simulation budget toward informative conformational space. A generative policy proposes starting conformations for short simulations, and trajectory-level rewards guide updates between groups of simulations. Peptide and protein–ligand applications evaluate state discovery and the dynamical information recovered from the collected segments. Because the starts are chosen adaptively, pooled frame counts do not directly measure equilibrium populations. The dissertation therefore distinguishes representation quality, observed transition information, and the statistical assumptions needed to infer kinetic observables. It presents a closed-loop approach in which learned representations support kinetic inference, and kinetic feedback guides the collection of new trajectories.

Geavanceerde ruimtelijk-temporele modellering voor surveillance in de volksgezondheid: van opportunistisch verzamelde gegevens naar beleidsrelevante toepassingen

Jose Alejandro Rozo Posada — 2026-11-13 — openalex

DOI : —

Malaria is a life-threatening disease that causes a significant public health burden worldwide. Malaria incidences show considerable temporal and geographical variation, which can be linked at least partly to climatic conditions. Over the last twenty years, multiple Malaria Early Warning Systems (MEWS) have been developed. These systems primarily focus on short-term incidence predictions, but there is a growing demand to implement long-term predictions that are informed by climatic variables. This project will develop a geostatistical long-term forecasting framework for malaria incidence with an emphasis on the effects of climate-related conditions. The statistical methodology will be developed that makes use of spatial multivariate time series analyses and distributed lag models, in order to flexibly model spatio-temporal variation in malaria incidence and how this is affected by climatic events. It will embed these methods in a MEWS, which prioritizes applicability and interpretability for healthcare workers and policy-makers in developing countries.

Le clausole di revisione nei contratti di distribuzione commerciale nazionale ed internazionale.

CORRADO REMIGI — 2026-11-11 — openalex

DOI : —

This doctoral thesis examines price revision clauses in domestic and international commercial distribution contracts, with particular reference to long-term contractual relationships and their exposure to changing economic circumstances. The research investigates the extent to which contractual mechanisms for price adjustment may operate as effective instruments for managing supervening events while preserving the original economic balance between the parties. Particular attention is devoted to supply contracts as a paradigmatic model of continuing or periodic contractual relationships and to the concept of the “physiological incompleteness” of long-term contracts, arising from the practical impossibility of anticipating all circumstances capable of affecting their performance over time. The analysis therefore addresses the requirements of certainty and determinability of contractual obligations, the scope of private autonomy, and the legal effects of extraordinary and unforeseeable events. The study adopts a comparative-law perspective, considering Italian law alongside foreign legal systems, case law, legal scholarship and international contractual practice. Within this framework, particular attention is paid to the impact of major supervening events, including the Covid-19 pandemic and recent economic and geopolitical crises. A central element of the research is the classification of price revision mechanisms into fixed and variable adjustment clauses. The former include mechanisms linked to raw-material costs, market indices, statistical and economic indicators, and currency parameters. The latter encompass periodic arm’s-length renegotiation, hardship clauses and mechanisms entrusting the determination of the contractual price or performance to an independent third party. The thesis ultimately argues that price revision clauses constitute a significant instrument of contractual risk allocation, enabling parties to reconcile legal certainty with contractual flexibility and to preserve the economic functionality of long-term commercial relationships in changing market conditions.

Supplementary data: In Silico ODE Modeling Reveals TP63 and TWIST1 Dosage Compensation Mediated by microRNA-Transcription Factor Networks in Aneuploid Cancer

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Pablo Mora-Cubillo, Guillermo Oviedo-Blanco, Rodrigo Mora-Rodriguez — 2026-11-11 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21660475

Supplementary Figure Supplementary Figure S1.pdf: Result of the fitting of the ODE models in COPASI. (A) No significant differences were observed between the experimental data and the final model predictions, indicating a successful fit. (B) Each heatmap shows experimental measurements (left), model-simulated values (center), and their absolute differences (right) across multiple cell lines (x-axis) and species (y-axis). (C) The networks of molecular interactions associated with the same models are shown. Solid and dashed lines represent upregulation and downregulation, respectively. Supplementary Figure S2.pdf: Results of multi-parameter scan on the kinetic parameter values for the species in the TP63 minimal model (A-G) and the TWIST1 (H-K) model under two copy numbers of TP63 or TWIST1 (1 and 3). The ratio between both scans is used to characterize the landscape of gene dose compensation and to analyze its dependence on the kinetic parameter values of the species. Cases of gene dosage compensation are located within the compensated region (cyan and blue colors). The “Control fold change” corresponds to the result obtained with the original fitted model. Combinations with an undefined ratio, as well as those for which COPASI did not find a steady state in one or both conditions, appear as unfilled gaps. These results indicate that the presence of a loop motif is not sufficient to generate the system-level property of gene dosage compensation. On the contrary, this property depends on the combination of specific parameter values. Supplementary Figure S3.pdf: Results of the identifiably analyses for the species in the TP63 minimal model (A) and the TWIST1 (B), obtained using the Profile Likelihood tool implemented in COPASI according to the Sabber (2012). Each graph shows the value of the objective function by successively setting one parameter and re-optimizing the remaining ones. The red curve represents the objective function value. The blue line represents the 95% confidence interval. The red line does not cross the blue line at both ends in any case, indicating that none of the parameters is identifiable. This result is consistent with the expected from the model equations and the single-point-time measurements of NCI-60 panel, which represents a steady-state experimental design. For example, in the steady state the derivatives of the species are zero, so the concentration depends on the ratio ks/kd. Without time series data, these parameters cannot be distinguished individually, although their values affect the temporal dynamics. Therefore, changes in one of these parameters in the identifiability analysis can be offset by a change in the other parameter. Other pairs (or groups) of parameters are expected to have similar behaviors, such as kr in TP63 together with ks in miR-155. Supplementary Figure S4.pdf: Comparison of experimental and predicted values by species in the HNF4A (A), TP63 (B), TP63 minimal (C) and TWIST1 (D) models. For each case, the root mean square error (RMSE), mean absolute error (MAE), coefficient of determination (R²), and Pearson's r are presented. Many factors can contribute to discrepancies between simulated and experimental data, such as the high heterogeneity among cancer cell lines, including differences in tissue origin, aneuploidy pattern, mutation, and distinct expression values at identical copy number, as well as the non-inclusion of a large number of molecules that interact with the species in the model. This is specifically relevant because each cell line is simulated using a shared set of kinetic parameters. The main value of these models lies in providing a mechanistic framework for studying gene dosage compensation, rather than predicting exact concentrations in individual cancer cell lines. Supplementary Figure S5.pdf: Base-10 logarithm transformation for the number of copies (x-axis) and the expression fold change (y-axis) for the target TF of the fitted models. Note that a slope (m) below 1 indicates sublinear growth, an m close to 1 indicates linear growth, and an m above 1 indicates superlinear growth. TP63 and TWIST1 clearly show sublinear growth. * Reduced model obtained after removing the species whose inhibition had no effect on TP63 levels. COPASI Models and Experimental Data Each model is provided as a separate .tar archive containing the COPASI model (.cps) and its corresponding experimental data (Experiments.tab) extracted from the NCI-60 cell line panel. HNF4a_model.tar: Contains the fitted ODE model of the HNF4A regulatory network (HNF4a_model.cps) and its experimental data (Experiments.tab). TP63_initial_model.tar: Contains the initial fitted ODE model of the TP63 regulatory network (TP63_initial_model.cps) and its experimental data (Experiments.tab). TP63_minimal_model.tar: Contains the reduced or minimal TP63 model (TP63_minimal_model.cps), used to identify the molecular components responsible for dosage compensation, and its experimental data (Experiments.tab). TWIST1_model.tar: Contains the fitted ODE model of the TWIST1 regulatory network (TWIST1_model.cps) and its experimental data (Experiments.tab). External Resources The regulatory interactions, CNV data, mRNA expression, and microRNA expression datasets from the NCI-60 and CCLE panels, as well as the BioNetUCR software used to construct regulatory networks and generate ODE models, can be found in Acón et al. (2021). References: Schaber, J. (2012). Easy parameter identifiability analysis with COPASI. Biosystems, 110(3), 183-185. M. CATNAP: Celltype Annotation by Taxonomy of Nested Adaptive Predictors

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Tom PERRIN, Sokuntepy Chriv, Jannes Mittelbach, Giorgio Gonnella — 2026-11-03 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23233884

Background: Single-cell RNA-seq has transformed biological research by resolving cellular heterogeneity invisible to bulk approaches, revealing fine cell types, elucidating host responses to infection, vaccination, and disease. Objectives: Hierarchical annotation models mirror the hierarchical definition of cell types, but existing tools fix a single classifier family and often feature space across all nodes. We offer a flexible hierarchical cell type annotation framework with a unified interface, supporting several classification methods, user-defined cell-types, node-specific feature selection and classifiers, and pre-trained human PMBC models. Methods: Hereby we introduce CATNAP, a customizable hierarchical cell-type annotation framework. It is fully modular: catnap_core drives the cell type annotation, given a cell type hierarchy tree, specifying which classifier and parameters shall be used for each subtree. Cell type hierarchies, model parameter presets and other configuration files, tailored to human PMBC studies, are available in catnap_preset. New models can be trained with own data or curated bundles of publicly available datasets, using catnap_pretrain. Finally, catnap_evaluation defines standardized metrics and representations for model performance assessment and comparisons among sets of models. Any classifier can be integrated, registering new catnap_core backends. We provide backends for predictors spanning correlation-based (Spearman), distance-based (k-nearest neighbors, nearest mean centroid), classical machine learning (logistic regression, random forest, gradient tree boosting, support vector machine), probabilistic (linear discriminant analysis, scANVI) and foundation models (Geneformer). Results: CATNAP was used for training, and cell-types prediction on six human PBMC scRNA-seq datasets. A scANVI-based hierarchical model achieved 86.42% accuracy and 83.61% macro-F1 on these datasets, and 55.31% accuracy and 51.38% macro-F1 on two additional out-of-distribution datasets. Conclusions: CATNAP is a customizable hierarchical cell-type annotation framework, providing also a framework for benchmarking existing methods, and a prototyping environment for new classifiers. Future work will explore model combinations, and alternative configurations for exploiting the full potential of our customizable hierarchical framework. Poster presented at the Pasteur Network Annual Meeting (PNAM 2026), Laval, Canada, 3–5 November 2026.

RNA-seq of mouse ventral midbrain from the following experimental groups: CTRL-VEHICLE; α-SYNUCLEIN overexpression-VEHICLE; CTRL-EMM-3.20 (SGK1 inhibitor); α-SYNUCLEIN overexpression-EMM-3.20 (SGK1 inhibitor).

Isabel Lastres‐Becker, Ignacio Silva-Llanes — 2026-10-30 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23388909

Parkinson’s disease (PD) is characterized by progressive degeneration of dopaminergic neurons and chronic neuroinflammation, processes that are strongly associated with the pathological accumulation of α-synuclein. The aim of this study was to investigate the role of serum- and glucocorticoid-regulated kinase 1 (SGK1) in α-synuclein-induced neuroinflammatory responses and neurodegeneration, and to evaluate the therapeutic potential of the novel brain-penetrant SGK1 inhibitor EMM-3.20. To model PD-related pathology, six-month-old male C57BL/6 mice received unilateral stereotaxic injections of an adeno-associated virus serotype 9 (AAV9) expressing human α-synuclein into the substantia nigra. Animals were randomly assigned to receive either vehicle or the SGK1 inhibitor EMM-3.20 (15 mg/kg, intraperitoneally) once daily for 14 days. The contralateral, non-injected side served as an internal control. For transcriptomic analysis, ventral midbrain tissues were dissected separately from the α-synuclein-overexpressing and contralateral control hemispheres. Total RNA was extracted and subjected to high-throughput RNA sequencing using the Illumina NovaSeq X Plus platform. Four experimental groups were analyzed: CTRL-VEH, α-SYN-VEH, CTRL-EMM-3.20, and α-SYN-EMM-3.20 (n = 5 biological replicates per group). Sequencing reads were quality controlled, aligned to the Mus musculus reference genome (GRCm39/mm39), and analyzed for differential gene expression. The RNA-seq experiment was designed to characterize the transcriptional programs induced by α-synuclein overexpression and to determine how pharmacological inhibition of SGK1 modifies these responses.

Optimalisatie van endogene productie in niet-model Pseudomonas-soorten: van chassis-evaluatie tot systeemgeleide stamoptimalisatie

Jorien Poppeliers — 2026-10-28 — openalex

DOI : —

Bacteria have the potential to serve as microbial cell factories, offering a sustainable alternative to the petrochemical industry. They can convert renewable feedstocks into a wide range of metabolites that are used in applications spanning healthcare, agriculture, cosmetics, and other industrial sectors. However, microbial production currently relies on a relatively small number of model organisms, meaning that only a fraction of the natural chemical diversity found in microorganisms is being exploited. Expanding the range of bacterial species that can be used for microbial fermentation is therefore essential. Many promising production hosts remain underutilized due to limited genetic accessibility, a lack of a suitable engineering toolbox and an insufficient understanding of the regulatory mechanisms governing metabolite production. To address these challenges, this dissertation establishes an integrated framework for the development of microbial cell factories based on non-model bacteria. Three Pseudomonas strains were selected as potential production platforms: Pseudomonas chlororaphis NRRL-B-30761, Pseudomonas fluorescens NCIMB 10586, and Pseudomonas syringae pv. porri CFBP 1770. First, the synthetic biology readiness of these strains was evaluated by assessing their genetic accessibility, as well as the availability and functionality of regulatory elements for controlling gene expression. Considerable differences were observed between the strains, with P. chlororaphis and P. fluorescens proving substantially more amenable to genetic manipulation than P. syringae. To further expand the available genetic toolbox, bacteriophages were explored as a source of regulatory elements. As natural experts in bacterial manipulation, phages possess a rich repertoire of genetic components that can be repurposed for synthetic biology applications. Using ONT-cappable-sequencing, hundreds of promoters and terminators were identified, together with novel RNA polymerase-promoter pairs spanning a broad range of expression strengths. Functional characterization of these elements in the selected hosts identified P. chlororaphis NRRL-B-30761 as the most promising chassis for further development as a microbial production platform. At the same time, this work generated a collection of new genetic building blocks that can facilitate future engineering efforts in other non-model Pseudomonas species. To further evaluate the potential of P. chlororaphis NRRL-B-30761 as a microbial cell factory, mono-rhamnolipid production was selected as a case study. Before production could be optimized, a deeper understanding of the regulatory mechanisms governing the mono-rhamnolipid biosynthetic gene cluster was required. By combining transcriptomics (ONT-cappable-sequencing) with metabolomics (LC-MS and molecular networking), an integrated overview was obtained of the factors influencing mono-rhamnolipid biosynthesis. The analyses revealed a complex and highly condition-dependent regulatory landscape controlling expression of the biosynthetic pathway. In addition, several antisense transcripts and a putative riboswitch-like element that likely interferes with rhamnolipid biosynthesis were identified. Metabolomic analyses confirmed the production of multiple mono-rhamnolipid congeners and uncovered additional pathway-associated metabolites, including several previously undescribed mono-rhamnolipid variants. Together, these findings highlighted multiple regulatory and metabolic bottlenecks limiting production capacity in the native host. Finally, these insights were translated into targeted engineering strategies to improve mono-rhamnolipid production. Following a design-build-test-learn framework, genetic modifications were introduced to optimize transcriptional regulation, translational efficiency, quorum-sensing control, and precursor availability. CRISPR-Cas3-mediated genome engineering was used to implement these modifications in P. chlororaphis. The resulting strains exhibited substantial improvements in mono-rhamnolipid production, with the best-performing strain producing up to 498-fold more mono-rhamnolipids than the wild-type strain. In particular, removal of the riboswitch-like element combined with optimization of translation efficiency yielded the greatest improvements, demonstrating that translational regulation represents a major bottleneck in the native biosynthetic pathway. Overall, this dissertation demonstrates how host evaluation, multi-omics characterization, and rational engineering can be combined to develop and optimize microbial cell factories from non-model bacteria. The results establish P. chlororaphis NRRL-B-30761 as a promising non-pathogenic host for mono-rhamnolipid production and provide both fundamental biological insights and novel genetic tools for future microbial strain engineering. In summary, this work presents a broadly applicable framework for unlocking the biotechnological potential of non-model bacteria through systems-guided synthetic biology.

IDENTIFICATION OF A STRUCTURALLY AND EVOLUTIONARILY CONSERVED 3'→5' PROOFREADING EXONUCLEASE DOMAIN IN THE LARGE L PROTEIN OF HANTAVIRUSES- EMERGING HUMAN PATHOGENS

Peramachi Palanivelu — 2026-10-18 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.30574/wjarr.2026.32.1.2621

The proofreading 3′→5′ exonuclease not only represents a crucial class of enzymes responsible for maintaining genome integrity across diverse groups of organisms, but also plays a critical role in ensuring replication fidelity. In the non-segmented, positive-strand bat-borne RNA viruses like Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome Coronaviruses (SARS-CoVs and MERS-CoV), it is found along with the methyltransferase (MTase) gene of the non-structural protein 14 (NSP14). In contrast, in the non-segmented, but negative-strand bat-borne RNA viruses such as Marburg, Ebola and Nipah viruses, it has been identified together with a novel type of capping enzyme, viz. guanosine diphosphate polyribonucleotidyltransferase (PRNTase). Hantaviruses, one of the fatal and emerging human pathogens in recent times, belong to segmented, negative-strand RNA viruses and are primarily rodent-borne. Currently, no universally approved vaccine or antiviral therapy exists for all hantavirus infections. The presence of a proofreading (PR) exonuclease domain has not been reported in hantaviruses until now. Interestingly, a putative PR exonuclease domain has been identified for the first time in these viruses within the large L protein, preceding the RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (RdRp). The newly identified domain belongs to the DEDD superfamily, but exhibits a distinct structural deviation, notably the replacement of the first catalytic triad -DxE- in the Old World hantaviruses with an -NxE- motif in most of the New World hantaviruses, and the use of an invariant tyrosine instead of histidine at the proton acceptor site. This feature closely resembles the DEDD superfamily of PR exonucleases reported in bacterial DNA polymerases I and II and in the DNA-dependent DNA polymerases of DNA viruses. To the best of my knowledge, this study provides the first evidence for the presence of a typical DEDD superfamily of PR exonuclease domain in hantaviruses, and thus, expanding our understanding of the structure and catalytic mechanism of 3′→5′ PR exonucleases in segmented, negative-strand RNA viruses and further presenting a novel structural target for the development of universally effective antiviral therapies against fatal hantavirus infections.

Imaginary Local Volatility, Arbitrage Fee Pricing and Vega Smoothing

Abdelmalek El Alaoui — 2026-10-18 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23443220

Classical Dupire is a real valued construction. When a stressed call surface makes the local variance negative, the exact one factor coefficient leaves the real line. This paper goes beyond real numbers and represents the local arbitrage contribution as an imaginary payoff component carried on, and paying along the imaginary axis iR. Its distributional projection creates a negative real contribution, Jordan normalization then gives C = (1 + β)C⁺ − βC⁻, with two ordinary positive Dupire branches. For risk calculations, the negative branch weight is frozen as an arbitrage fee across the declared IV-shock set. Autocall tests show that this fixed fee removes repair driven bucket vega spikes and makes the IV PnL explain substantially more stable.

Evie's Perfect Purity - Extreme Quantization Drill: Finite Lattice Quantization, Drift-Aware Reconstruction, and Existing-Parameter Distillation for Transformer Models

Artificial Hyperintelligence, Eve, wife of Maciej Nowicki, Maciej Nowicki — 2026-10-18 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23442743

Evie’s Perfect Purity - Extreme Quantization Drill investigates extreme compression of complete transformer models through finite lattice representations, activation-aware reconstruction, guarded discrete and continuous refinement, and teacher–student distillation restricted to parameters already present in the stored representation. The research treats the serialized, decoded model as the object of evaluation. Continuous reconstruction targets, optimizer master parameters, rounded metadata, discrete code assignments, and installed inference weights are distinguished explicitly. This distinction matters at extreme precision: an improvement in an idealized quadratic objective can disappear after finite rounding or produce a worse complete-model probability distribution. The collection brings together the preserved foundational manuscript, later lattice-method development material, aggregate summaries of surviving experiments, and a separately identified record of the subsequent scale-diagonal extension. Its contribution is the specific integration of these mechanisms, their finite representation contracts, and the empirical examination of their interactions. Established lattice constructions, least-squares identities, orthonormal transformations, Hessian feedback, and distillation retain their original attribution. The methodological program comprises six connected components. Finite lattice coding and Cherubic Isometric Transport. The later representation combines the published QuIP# E8P construction with fixed signed orthonormal transforms, stored input diagonals, and row-group scales. In ideal arithmetic, a projection is reconstructed as Ŵ = UᵀQVD, where U and V are signed orthonormal operators, D is a positive diagonal matrix, and Q contains scaled lattice coordinates. The two-bit projection code identifies one of 65,536 eight-dimensional E8P points using a 16-bit index. The three-bit construction adds an eight-bit residual index covering the same eight coordinates, together with a stored relative residual scale. The initial residual assignment is sequential rather than an exhaustive search over all code pairs. The implementation uses signed DCT transforms, local codeword ordering, paid diagonal values, and explicit finite metadata. It is an attributed CPU adaptation of published constructions, with implementation and protocol differences from the official QuIP# pipeline. Immaculate Affine Closure and teacher–student drift. Quantizing earlier operations changes the inputs received by later projections. The affine formulation therefore uses paired teacher responses and current-student inputs, rather than assuming that teacher and student activations coincide. For student input x, teacher response y, dense reference W₀, and λ > 0, the local objective is: L(A,b) = E‖y − Ax − b‖² + λ‖A − W₀‖²F. Eliminating the real-valued bias gives b*(A) = μy − Aμx. With centered covariances, H = Cxx + λI and M = (Cyx + λW₀)H⁻¹, the matrix-dependent term becomes tr[(A − M)H(A − M)ᵀ]. This classical identity supplies a drift-aware target for the finite quantizer. The stored bias introduces an additional rounding residual, so separate matrix and bias guards do not establish monotonicity of the entire finite affine objective across matrix changes. The optional Ophanim Residual Closure formulation additionally targets the terminal feed-forward response after accounting for the current student residual path. Hessian feedback and Celestial Coordinate Purification. Initial encoding uses established Hessian error-feedback principles. Subsequent coordinate refinement revisits finite codewords under the full transformed quadratic metric. For F(Q) = tr[(M̃ − Q)H̃(M̃ − Q)ᵀ], the change associated with a block proposal Δ is evaluated through its quadratic and linear contributions. The incumbent remains eligible, proposals must pass a numerical improvement margin, and complete-sweep recomputation can trigger rollback. Euclidean nearest-codeword routines generate candidates; they do not solve every full-metric discrete assignment problem exactly. The guards concern computed local objectives and do not establish global codeword optimality, monotonic complete-model perplexity, or improved generalization. Concordant Lattice Refitting. With code assignments fixed, the existing group scales form a coupled quadratic subproblem. For each output row, the transformed reconstruction is expressed as a weighted sum of decoded group vectors. Cross-group terms from the calibration metric enter the scale Gram matrix. The real-valued proposal is obtained from an active-system solve. It is then clipped, rounded to the existing FP16 scale words, and compared with the incumbent. Additional checks evaluate the ordinary decoded FP32 matrix. The method introduces no additional correction matrix or group-scale inventory. Clipping an unconstrained solution is not presented as an exact nonnegative quadratic-program solution. Refinement controls receive the extra code and scale optimization without the centered affine target, helping distinguish target changes from additional optimization work. Angelic Finite Complement Distillation. A separate intervention freezes the quantized body and adjusts only existing projection biases and LayerNorm parameters. For the declared OPT-125M configuration, this complement contains 122 tensors and 121,344 scalar values. Training uses full-vocabulary teacher-to-student KL divergence across all 50,272 classes. FP32 master values accumulate updates, while forward computation uses their projected FP16 values. The identity backward treatment through rounding is explicitly a straight-through surrogate. A memory-bounded vocabulary-head schedule preserves the full distributional objective while reducing temporary activation requirements. Endpoint selection includes the unchanged parent and uses development data. This intervention adds a distillation budget even though it introduces no model parameters. Recorded Angelic Scale-Diagonal Concordance extension. The subsequent v5 record describes jointly tuning existing group scales, input diagonals, biases, and normalization values while keeping lattice codes fixed. Its reported trainable subset contains 867,840 existing scalars, approximately 0.693% of the model’s unique parameters. The recorded smooth decoder pullback distinguishes the scale adjoint from the diagonal adjoint and uses the unrounded pre-diagonal reconstruction where required. Parent-relative log coordinates, finite forward projection, and surrogate gradients are stated separately. The prior report describes a matched 128-update comparison against complement-only continuation and reports a 1.002% perplexity reduction relative to that control on a separate 128-document C4 prefix collection. The original v5 implementation, selected model, raw outputs, and final manuscript remain unavailable in the recovered materials. This result is retained as a clearly labeled prior report, rather than an independently revalidated measurement of this edition. The preserved experiments use OPT-125M with 125,239,296 unique parameters and 72 decoder projection matrices containing 84,934,656 weights. Calibration uses 32 WikiText-2 training contexts of 1,024 tokens. The later development guard contains 16 reset contexts of 2,048 tokens. Evaluation includes 140 WikiText test contexts, 256 declared C4 contexts, and all 1,838 PIQA validation questions. Likelihood evaluation uses the complete vocabulary and pooled token-weighted negative log-likelihood. PIQA reports both raw conditional-likelihood accuracy and a secondary normalization by solution character count. Historical measurements and fresh measurements from the earlier recovery study retain distinct provenance. Selected surviving complete-model measurements are: Candidate Complete artifact bytes WikiText140 perplexity C4_256 perplexity Dense FP16-word reference 250,564,117 27.663 25.631 Local GPTQ, two-bit projection codes 47,926,137 198.500 269.831 Cherubic E8P reference 46,869,586 48.216 56.694 Cherubic refined E8P 46,869,594 46.701 54.408 Local GPTQ, three-bit projection codes 58,542,969 36.325 35.044 Cherubic residual-vector reference 57,529,210 31.333 30.067 Cherubic refined residual-vector 57,529,218 31.437 30.059 These results support improved language-metric quality relative to this package’s local GPTQ controls at smaller complete-file sizes. They also expose limits: two-bit refinement improves the reported perplexities while reducing PIQA accuracy; three-bit refinement produces mixed language-metric changes; affine reconstruction candidates can regress despite favorable local fitting diagnostics. The dense reference retains a substantial quality advantage. Storage accounting charges projection codes, FP16 scales and diagonals, packed signs, residual metadata, tied vocabulary arrays, positional weights, complement values, and complete container headers. Nominal two-bit and three-bit projection codes therefore do not imply corresponding whole-model rates. The recorded selected artifacts occupy approximately 2.994 and 3.675 bits per unique model parameter, respectively. Reference inference expands the representation into FP32 matrices; compressed-kernel throughput, serving latency, and deployed low-bit memory improvements are not established. This standalone edition includes the unchanged 93-page v3 manuscript, a clearly marked 28-page v4.1 development draft, six preserved methods and reference notes, a research overview, protocol documentation, aggregate evidence summaries, citation metadata, licensing notices, and document checksums. The surviving later checkpoint contains 36 of 42 expected task records, covering 12 of 14 declared candidates. Multi-omics reveals the role of Bifidobacterium animalis with the potential to reshape the bile acid pool in neonatal jaundice

王明帮(Mingbang Wang) — 2026-10-18 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.26036/cnp0007919

Neonatal jaundice (NJ), affecting 60-80% of newborns, risks neurotoxicity in severe cases. Although gut microbiota dysbiosis is known to influence bilirubin and bile acid (BA) metabolism, key microbial mechanisms remain incompletely understood. Through a multi-omics analysis of 120 neonates, we identified the key bacteria of moderate-to-severe jaundice and discovered the correlations between gut phages, bacteria, and BA in NJ. Moderate-to-severe NJ exhibited distinct gut dysbiosis, characterized by increased Streptococcus and Escherichia, while depleted Bifidobacterium animalis. Therapeutic intervention reversed this imbalance, suppressing pathogens while restoring B. animalis. Virome profiling revealed distinct group-specific phage patterns (e.g., Streptococcus phage dominance in GBS group, Enterobacteria phage in NJ) and significant phage-bacteria covariance, encompassing both potential lytic and symbiotic interactions. Structural modeling quantified interactions: phages indirectly modulated BA via microbiota restructuring, whereas B. animalis directly regulated BA metabolism. Functionally, B. animalis bile salt hydrolase (BSH) activity was downregulated in active NJ and recovered post-treatment. In vitro assays confirmed its BA-remodeling capacity, reducing total BA while enhancing deconjugation (increased deoxycholic acid). Animal experiments suggested that B. animalis might ameliorate jaundice and liver damage, potentially via Cyp7a1/Cyp7b1-mediated BA metabolic regulation. Together, these findings reveal a multi-faceted interaction involving phages, bacteria, and BA in NJ, and highlight B. animalis as a promising therapeutic candidate.

In-silico Drug Designing and Development for Genetic Disorders- A Review

Prashant Kumar, Ajeet, Babita Kumar, Shabnam Ain, Qurratul Ain — 2026-10-18 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23439252

Genetic disorders arise from pathogenic genomic alterations that disrupt essential cellular, biochemical, and physiological functions, collectively imposing a substantial global health burden. Conventional drug discovery for inherited diseases is challenged by complex molecular defects, including loss- or gain-of-function mutations, altered protein stability, undruggable targets, limited patient populations, and high development costs. In-silico drug designing offers a mechanism-driven approach by integrating multi-omics data, structural bioinformatics, cheminformatics, artificial intelligence, and computational systems biology. This framework encompasses disease-target identification and prioritization through genomic, expression, and interaction network analyses; comparative structural assessment of wild-type and mutant proteins to elucidate mutation-induced changes in folding, binding sites, and molecular dynamics; virtual screening and molecular docking to identify potential therapeutic compounds; and molecular dynamics simulations to evaluate protein–ligand stability and binding energetics. Additionally, pharmacophore modeling and quantitative structure–activity relationship (QSAR) analysis facilitate the identification and optimization of promising chemical scaffolds, while network pharmacology and drug repurposing reveal multi-target therapeutic opportunities and potentially accelerate clinical translation. Early assessment of absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity (ADMET) further supports drug developability. Integrating these computational strategies with multi-omics and systems-level insights can transform genetic disease drug discovery from conventional empirical screening into a mutation-aware, mechanism-based approach, advancing personalized and precision medicine.

Boundary Histories, Target-Specific Observability, and Physical Calibration: A Claim-Bounded Synthesis of the BIG-B42 Research Programme

Jun Lucis — 2026-10-18 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23432803

Boundary Information Geometry (BIG) is an exploratory mathematical and computational research programme that studies boundary fields, response geometry, and history-dependent dynamics through several distinct model families. The BIG-B42 programme examines whether history retention, observable response, counterfactual history contributions, elapsed progression, and cross-system calibration can be connected without conflating their mathematical requirements. This paper synthesizes its conditional propositions, frozen synthetic-model tests, source-implementation audits, and physical-calibration constraints. We formulate target-specific identifiability through observational equivalence: a deterministic target is recoverable from noiseless observations exactly when it is constant on every observational fiber. Under bounded noise, the range of target values compatible with a record determines an optimal worst-case error radius. We distinguish these standard results from stronger requirements for dynamic state closure, causal-effect identification, and physical measurement. A restricted integrability obstruction applies to the original one-way history-to-response coupling under fixed block-diagonal gradient mobility, whereas separately introduced reciprocal thermodynamic completions admit conventional distributed-Maxwell representations. In the frozen B17.4 synthetic-PDE studies, an estimator achieves limited accuracy for weak history-dependent responses but fails predetermined ordering and preparation-transfer gates. The B27 continuum description and legacy two-dimensional numerical flux also require explicit version separation. Across these results, no independently calibrated BIG-specific physical law, unique boundary-history variable, or emergent physical time has been established. We conclude by specifying what independently measured state variables, work-conjugate observables, and equal-information conventional comparisons would be needed for a discriminating physical test. Publication scope and files: English primary preprint (35 pages), with a Japanese reference translation (26 pages), version 1.0. BIG-B42 formal scientific verdict NONE; independent external-validation R4 selected/opened/evaluator counts 0/0/0. Original CP-series FAIL and mixed outcomes and the B27 source–implementation discrepancy are retained. Basic reproduction materials are available, but an independent full computational rerun audit has not been completed. The small Zenodo_Preprint_Upload_Package ZIP contains byte-identical copies of the two standalone PDFs, their editable DOCX files, selected CP9/CP10 figure inputs, a historical metadata proposal, a scope README and its original SHA256 list. The separate Integrated_Assessment_Reproducibility ZIP contains the archived integrated audit and eight source-study packages. It is a scoped collection, not a newly completed full rerun or a complete reassembly of every B42 branch. The metadata proposal inside the small ZIP is historical; the Zenodo record metadata governs the actual publication status and author-selected license. Reproduction archive reconstruction: The integrated reproduction ZIP is supplied as 10 sequential byte parts named BIG_B42_Integrated_Assessment_Reproducibility_v1_0.zip.part001 through .part010. These parts are not separate ZIP archives. Save all ten parts, B42_REPRO_TRANSPORT_MANIFEST.json, B42_reassemble.py and README_B42_REPRO_TRANSPORT.txt in one directory, then run python3 B42_reassemble.py. The script verifies every part and restores BIG_B42_Integrated_Assessment_Reproducibility_v1_0.zip (97,023,337 bytes; SHA256 3a5ac10d6f0b900d9e4a2ae9232065fccd617a323b0a220263ae2a98491da08b). To verify the complete data without writing the restored ZIP, run python3 B42_reassemble.py --verify-only. Only the transport representation has changed; the archived research data and scientific verdicts are unchanged. Manuscript status: This version is released as a working preprint. Editorial proof notes and explicitly unresolved checks on mathematical assumptions, the B17.4 weighted reader, and source-label/version mapping remain in the manuscripts. File-integrity verification and successful archive reconstruction do not complete or certify those outstanding mathematical or computational checks. The stated scientific verdicts and limitations are retained.

Identification of a natural product inhibitor of SARS-CoV-2 Mpro through integrated virtual screening, DFT, and molecular dynamics simulations

Dharma Paudel, Pooja Shrestha, Saran Lamichhane, Giri Tripathi, Shyam Khanal — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-72090-0

Abstract The emergence of vaccine-resistant SARS-CoV-2 variants demands the development of novel antivirals targeting conserved viral proteins, like the main protease (M pro ). For expediting drug discovery, CADD approach of integrating different computational tools have been employed in drug candidate identification. Based on computational analysis, our study aimed to identify potential M pro inhibitors from natural product databases by combining virtual screening, DFT calculations and MD simulations. From 224,205 molecules from ZINC15 natural product database, N-[[(2R,3S,4R,5S)-5-(2-anilino-2-oxoethyl)-3,4-dihydroxyoxolan-2-yl]methyl]-4-methoxybenzamide (Lig14) was a prospective candidate against SARS-CoV-2 M pro . It exhibited binding energy of − 8.2 kcal/mol (Paxlovid with − 7.6 kcal/mol). It showed dipole moment of 12.70 D, HOMO/LUMO energy gap of 123.00 kJ/mol. MD simulations revealed a consistent contact area interference of ~ 360 Å 2 . The binding free energy (MM-GBSA) of − 31.82 ± 6.64 kcal/mol suggested in proper binding. Moreover, MD analysis also exhibited strong sub-diffusive motion (α = 0.49) with 35.2 Å confinement within the M pro active site, demonstrating sustained binding interactions characteristic of competitive inhibitors. The alchemical free energy perturbation calculation gave solvation free energy (ΔG solv ) in water (− 41.22 to − 40.70 kJ/mol) across four methods indicating solubility. The C145Y and π-sulfur bonding with C145 in G143S, G143D, G143N and E166K could disrupt catalytic dyad formation with H41, potentially compromising enzymatic function. Through an integrated computational approach, we identified Lig14 as a promising lead compound for the inhibition of SARS-CoV-2 M pro . While these in silico methods are highly credible for initial screening, their inherent limitations warrant subsequent experimental investigation to validate these findings.

Antimicrobial resistance profiles and antibiotic resistance gene detection in environmental isolates from urban streams in Ilorin, Nigeria: a One Health cross-sectional assessment

Olatunji Matthew Kolawole, Fausat Abimbola Jimoh, Damilola Miracle Olayemi, Amudalat Jimoh, Adetola Esther Okunola — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-75372-9

Urban streams in sub-Saharan Africa receive continuous inputs of untreated wastewater and solid waste, potentially promoting accumulation of antibiotic-resistant bacteria (ARB) and resistance genes (ARGs). Data linking community sanitation to environmental antimicrobial resistance (AMR) in West African cities remain sparse. We assessed physicochemical quality, bacteriological contamination, antibiotic susceptibility, ARG prevalence, and sanitation practices across six urban streams in Ilorin, Nigeria. Three weeks of sampling yielded 247 isolates tested against six antibiotics; 98 multidrug-resistant (MDR) Gram-negative isolates were PCR-screened for five ARGs; seven isolates were 16S rRNA-sequenced; 180 households were surveyed to compute a Composite Sanitation Score (CSS) per catchment. All streams deviated from WHO/NSDWQ reference values. Ampicillin resistance was 64–89% per site; MDR prevalence was 43–68%. bla TEM was most prevalent (58–82%), followed by tet A (46–74%) and int I1 (39–61%). CSS correlated strongly negatively with all five AMR indicators (ρ = − 1.00; exact permutation p = 0.003; Bonferroni α = 0.01). With only six catchments, these are exploratory ecological associations, not causal evidence. Sanitation quality is associated with environmental AMR burden in Ilorin streams, supporting WASH investment within One Health AMR strategies, subject to confirmation in larger multi-site studies.

High-density fermentation of Streptococcus suis serotype 31 for inactivated vaccine production

刘丽霞, Tao Zhou, Jinna Cui, Sie Han, Jianhua Hu — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12896-026-01241-y

Streptococcus suis serotype 31 (SS31) is a clinically important swine pathogen for which inactivated vaccine development requires a scalable cultivation process and evidence that the resulting bacterin has protective activity. However, conventional culture processes often yield insufficient biomass, whereas aggressive high-density fermentation may perturb the physiological features that determine vaccine quality. Herein, we demonstrate a Response Surface Methodology (RSM)-guided fermentation-to-vaccine strategy that optimizes the SS31 culture microenvironment and evaluates the protective activity of a bacterin produced under the optimized conditions. Single-factor screening and Box-Behnken modeling identified glucose, organic nitrogen and newborn bovine serum as dominant growth variables, leading to an optimized formulation containing 2.19 g/L glucose, 43.8 g/L organic nitrogen and 5.89% serum. When scaled to a 14-L bioreactor with residual sugar controlled at 1–3 g/L and pH maintained at 7.2, this process achieved 1.51 × 10¹⁰ CFU/mL, approximately seven-fold higher than shake-flask cultivation. The LD₅₀ estimates were 2.50 × 10⁶ CFU/mouse for SS31 cultured in the optimized medium and 2.14 × 10⁷ CFU/mouse for SS31 cultured in Martin medium, indicating a difference in the virulence-associated phenotype under the experimental conditions. These estimates do not demonstrate the conservation or enhancement of vaccine antigens. Among the four vaccine formulations tested, the aluminum-adjuvanted vaccine containing 3 × 10⁹ pre-inactivation CFU equivalents/mL yielded the highest observed survival (10/10 mice) after challenge with 2 LD₅₀. The organ bacterial-load and histopathological findings were evaluated descriptively. These findings demonstrate the technical feasibility of the SS31 cultivation process at the 14-L scale and support further evaluation of the resulting inactivated vaccine against S. suis .

Structural determinants of communicable disease risk in asylum reception centres

Giancarlo Ceccarelli, Francesco Branda, Cecilia Ceccarelli, Luca Bortolani, Helen Linda Morrone — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1080/14787210.2026.2751993

Introduction With over 120 million people forcibly displaced globally, asylum reception systems represent critical epidemiological interfaces. This synthesis is not confined to a single geographic region: evidence was drawn from reception systems across high-income host countries in Europe and, where available, comparable settings elsewhere, reflecting the substantial heterogeneity of reception models worldwide. Within a One Health framework, these socio-spatial environments shape communicable disease dynamics through accommodation density, sanitation capacity, and governance continuity.Areas covered Synthesizing evidence from 2015–2026, we examine transmission patterns of respiratory infections, fecal–oral diseases, ectoparasitic and dermatological conditions, tuberculosis, and vaccine-preventable diseases in reception infrastructures. Outbreaks cluster around occupancy density, ventilation constraints, sanitation integrity, mobility, and discontinuity of care. Transmission dynamics are structurally produced by interactions between built environments, organizational processes, and health system integration.Expert opinion Reception systems are conditionally amplifying environments: outbreak magnitude depends on spatial configuration, occupancy governance, surveillance interoperability, and clinical continuity. Reframing centers as epidemiological infrastructures enables a shift from reactive containment to structural calibration. Modular design, density thresholds, and integrated preventive–clinical pathways enhance epidemiological predictability and reduce transmission risk. Amplification is not intrinsic to populations but to the environments accommodating them, with implications for broader collective housing and public health system design.

Mapping Research on HPV Vaccination in Brazil: A Bibliometric Review of Current Conditions and Future Decisions

Beatriz Pereira Gonçalves, Beatriz Reimberg Rasquinho Fernandes, Beatriz Verderame Braz, Pablo Fortunato Silva, Dennis Minoru Fujita — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s40944-026-01147-9

Abstract Purpose To analyze scientific production trends on Human Papillomavirus (HPV) vaccination in Brazil through a bibliometric approach, evaluating publication growth, collaboration networks, and knowledge gaps related to vaccine research and public health strategies. Methods A bibliometric study was conducted using data retrieved from the Web of Science database, including publications from 1995 to 2023. A total of 447 articles were analyzed using RStudio software and the Bibliometrix package to assess publication trends, thematic focus, and regional research distribution. Results Brazil demonstrated an annual growth rate of 8.95% in HPV-related publications since 2002, with a notable increase after 2014 following the implementation of free HPV vaccination through the Brazilian Unified Health System (SUS). The main research topics included vaccine efficacy, vaccination coverage, and population acceptance. However, important regional disparities were identified, with most studies concentrated in the Southeast and South regions, while the North and Northeast remained underrepresented. Conclusion HPV vaccination research in Brazil has expanded significantly over the past decades, particularly after the incorporation of vaccination into national public health policies. Despite this progress, regional inequalities in scientific production persist, highlighting the need for further studies focused on vaccination awareness, access, and adherence in underserved regions to strengthen cervical cancer prevention strategies.

— 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.73s.152

Background: Unexplained recurrent spontaneous miscarriage (URSA) accounts for up to 50-60% of recurrent pregnancy losses, with immune dysregulation playing a key pathogenic role.Objective: To assess the safety and effectiveness of cyclosporine A in enhancing pregnancy outcomes among women with unexplained recurrent spontaneous miscarriage.Methods: This prospective, randomized, placebo-controlled trial included 78 women with URSA, randomly assigned (1:1) to receive either CsA (2-4 mg/kg twice daily) or placebo.Baseline clinical, laboratory, immunological, and ultrasonographic assessments were performed.Participants were followed through a screening phase, a one-year prepregnancy period, and up to 42 weeks after conception.Results: No significant variances were observed in the timing of pregnancy loss, stillbirth, ectopic pregnancy, preterm birth, gestational age at delivery, neonatal anthropometric measures, sex, or maternal complications (all P > 0.05).Composite neonatal morbidity was lower in the Cyclosporine A group (17.9% vs. 25.0%)but didn't reach statistical significance (P = 0.57).Rates of individual neonatal adverse outcomes were also comparable between groups, with no increase in congenital anomalies or neonatal sepsis associated with Cyclosporine A treatment.Conclusion: Cyclosporine A significantly reduced miscarriage rates and improved successful pregnancy outcomes in females with URSA without increasing maternal or neonatal adverse events, suggesting it may be an effective and safe therapeutic option in this population.

INFECTIOUS MORBIDITY OF THE POPULATION OF UKRAINE IN 2016–2025 IN THE CONTEXT OF EUROPEAN TRENDS

Sava I. Palamar, Tetiana S. Gruzieva, Hanna V. Inshakova — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.31612/2616-4868.7.2026.19

Introduction. Infectious diseases remain an important public health problem despite progress in the prevention and control of individual infections. Changes in the epidemiological situation in Ukraine are occurring against the background of global and European trends, which necessitates systematic monitoring and effective forecasting. Identifying current trends in Ukraine and the European Region is important in the context of global challenges and migration processes.Aim. To identify the main trends in infectious morbidity among the population of Ukraine in 2016–2025 and to conduct a comparative assessment with European trends in order to substantiate prevention priorities.Materials and methods. The study used data from official statistical reports of the Public Health Center of the Ministry of Health of Ukraine for 2016–2025, national regulatory and legal documents in the field of epidemiological surveillance, as well as analytical and statistical materials from the World Health Organization (WHO) and the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC). Information and analytical, statistical, and epidemiological methods, time-series analysis, and comparative analysis were applied.Results. It was established that Ukraine has an updated regulatory and legal framework aimed at improving epidemiological surveillance and strengthening the control of infectious diseases. During 2016–2025, a decrease in the incidence of socially significant infectious diseases was observed, including a 47.0% reduction in HIV infection, a 71.6% reduction in AIDS, and a 30.5% reduction in tuberculosis. The incidence of acute viral hepatitis A, B, and C decreased by 62.9%, 64.7%, and 26.2%, respectively, whereas the incidence of chronic viral hepatitis B and C increased by 3.3% and 29.6%, respectively. Among other infections, the largest decreases were observed for rubella (−95.7%), pediculosis and phthiriasis (−86.4%), gonococcal infection (−91.5%), and pertussis (−62.6%). At the same time, measles incidence increased 15.3-fold, Lyme disease incidence increased 2.6-fold, and tetanus incidence increased by 20.0%. Regional analysis revealed substantial disparities in the incidence of HIV infection and tuberculosis. The highest rates were observed in Dnipropetrovsk and Odesa regions, while the lowest rates were recorded in western regions, with differences reaching 9- to 12-fold. These trends correspond to challenges faced by the WHO European Region, where a substantial burden of tuberculosis and HIV infection persists and measles outbreaks continue to occur amid declining routine immunization coverage.Conclusions. Thus, analysis of the ten-year dynamics demonstrates multidirectional changes in infectious morbidity in Ukraine. Alongside a sustained decrease in the incidence of a number of socially significant and vaccine-preventable infections, an increase was observed in the incidence of certain diseases, particularly measles, Lyme disease, and chronic viral hepatitis. This highlights the need for a differentiated approach to assessing the epidemiological situation and for further improvement of epidemiological surveillance, prevention, early detection, and control of infectious diseases, as well as effective outbreak forecasting.

Targeting Force-Regulated FimH Adhesin: Assays, Mannosides, and Biologics for Anti-Virulence Therapy of UPEC Infection

Mohor Mitra, Zhilei Chen — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1021/acsinfecdis.6c00689

Abstract Urinary tract infections (UTIs) are among the most common bacterial infections, and recurrent UTIs caused by uropathogenic Escherichia coli (UPEC) drive antibiotic use and antimicrobial resistance. FimH, the mannose-binding adhesin at the tip of type 1 pili, is a central UPEC virulence factor. Under shear force, FimH switches from a low-affinity, tense state to a high-affinity, relaxed state, strengthening adhesion during urine flow. This catch-bond behavior makes FimH a promising anti-virulence target, though a difficult one to drug. Here, we review strategies to target FimH, including monovalent and multivalent mannosides, vaccines, conformation-selective monoclonal antibodies, and de novo engineered miniproteins, together with the assays used to quantify FimH-ligand binding and UPEC adhesion. Clinical translation has been limited by assay-dependent potency, urinary pharmacokinetics, and intracellular reservoirs. We posit that effective FimH-targeted therapy requires alignment among inhibitor mechanism, FimH conformational state, bladder lumen exposure, and infection stage, which in turn defines realistic roles in prevention, early intervention, and combination therapy.

Needle-free Injection Technology: Physical Mechanisms, Clinical Evidence, Safety and Translational Prospects

Harshada Andhale, Suresh Waghamare — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.56557/arjocs/2026/v8i1212

Needle-free injection denotes the delivery of a medicinal substance across a tissue barrier without penetration by a conventional solid hypodermic needle. Liquid-jet injectors are its most clinically developed form; particulate ballistic systems and emerging laser-, pneumatic- and electromagnetic-actuated microjets represent related but technically distinct approaches. This critical narrative review integrates engineering, pharmaceutical, clinical and implementation evidence to examine how device architecture, jet dynamics, tissue heterogeneity and formulation characteristics determine the balance between delivery accuracy and injury. Literature published from 2000 to 9 August 2026 was considered, together with earlier concepts where necessary, using targeted scholarly searching and reference tracing. Experimental studies support the importance of nozzle geometry, jet power, temporally controlled propulsion, applicator loading and tissue properties. Randomised influenza and poliovirus vaccine studies demonstrate that particular injector–vaccine combinations can achieve clinically acceptable immune responses, although local reactogenicity and delivered-dose variability remain relevant. Controlled insulin studies indicate earlier drug absorption and improved early postprandial glycaemia, but do not establish long-term superiority in diabetes outcomes. Dermatological trials support selected intralesional applications, while their small samples, heterogeneous endpoints and frequent drug–device confounding restrict extrapolation. Historical bloodborne-pathogen risks show why reusable patient-contact components cannot be regarded as safe merely because no needle is present. Preclinical microjet and nucleic-acid findings extend the design space but are not equivalent to validated human delivery. The review proposes a device–formulation–tissue–setting framework in which pharmacological effectiveness, contamination prevention, usability and affordability must be demonstrated jointly rather than inferred from needle elimination. Future advances depend on reproducible dose accounting, robust biopharmaceutical stability testing, comparative clinical endpoints and implementation research under realistic conditions.

Longitudinal BCR repertoire dynamics reveal the evolution of broadly binding antibodies following heterologous SARS-CoV-2 vaccination and breakthrough infection: a single-donor case study

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Rui Qiao, Xiaorong Chen, Xuhuan Zhang, Yuchen Shao, Liangjiu Zhang — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34662047

Continued SARS-CoV-2 antigenic drift and the persistent risk of zoonotic sarbecovirus spillover highlight the need for broadly protective countermeasures. Here, we longitudinally profiled the B-cell receptor (BCR) repertoire of a single volunteer who received heterologous booster vaccination followed by Omicron BF.7 breakthrough infection, integrating bulk BCR sequencing with single B-cell culture and antibody binding analysis. Breakthrough infection was associated with broadened serum neutralization across SARS-CoV-2 variants and related sarbecoviruses, consistent with recall of antibodies targeting conserved epitopes. Repertoire analysis revealed stage-specific remodeling, with high-affinity binding antibodies shifting from an IGHV1-46-enriched profile after vaccination to preferential representation of IGHV3-30-3 after infection. Isolated monoclonal antibodies exhibited nanomolar-to-picomolar binding affinities to diverse animal-derived sarbecovirus spikes, with broad binding commonly, but not universally, associated with CDRH3 lengths of 14-16 amino acids. Phylogenetic and sequence analyses defined divergent evolutionary trajectories for IGHV1-46 and IGHV3-30-3 lineages, highlighting candidate genetic features that warrant validation in larger cohorts. Together, these findings describe how sequential heterologous antigen exposures can reshape the humoral repertoire in one individual and generate hypotheses for future pan-sarbecovirus antibody and vaccine studies.

When models diverge: Harmonizing pneumococcal vaccination cost-effectiveness analyses to inform PCV20 and PCV21 use in US adults

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Kenneth J. Smith, Angela R. Wateska, Zinan Yi, Richard Kent Zimmerman, Peter P. Mueller — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.vaccine.2026.129267

Objectives Policymakers use decision models to assess pneumococcal vaccine cost-effectiveness, but different models often yield inconsistent results. We systematically compared 2 published models evaluating 20- and 21-valent pneumococcal conjugate vaccines (PCV20/PCV21) in adults aged 50+ years. Methods Markov decision model structures, parameters, and assumptions were iteratively modified, comparing PCV20 use in higher-risk 50–64-year-olds and all 65-year-olds to either PCV20 or PCV21 for all at age 50 or 65. One model was adjusted toward the other by harmonizing strategies, aligning parameter values, performing sensitivity analyses to identify influential parameters, and modifying sensitive parameters to narrow differences. Other analyses compared differing cohort structures and vaccine timing. Results Initial results diverged widely, driven by differing disease risk, vaccination likelihood and serotype coverage, and childhood vaccination indirect effects — all uncertain and debated. Results were extremely sensitive to parameter variation due to minuscule incremental differences between strategies. Parameter harmonization produced substantial convergence. Other factors that reduced divergence were vaccine effectiveness assumptions, cohort age, and single-age vs. multi-age cohorts. Conclusion Divergent model results were mitigated through harmonization. Cost-effectiveness ratios were very sensitive to alternative model inputs. Model comparisons can identify uncertainty, reveal evidence gaps, enhance transparency, and potentially improve decision making.

Cross-protection of wildtype and vaccine-derived chikungunya virus antibodies against chikungunya and Mayaro viruses in mice

Konrad M. Wesselmann, Léa Lucıanı, Maxime Cochin, Marie‐Christine Jaffar‐Bandjee, Jan Felix Drexler — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41541-026-01604-8

Abstract The live-attenuated vaccine IXCHIQ against chikungunya virus (CHIKV) was approved in 2023, with its efficacy evaluated using human neutralizing antibody titers as surrogate effectiveness criterion. Previous in vivo studies assessed its efficacy at high CHIKV-specific antibody titers, whereas its efficacy and antibody-dependent enhancement (ADE) risks at suboptimal levels mimicking long-term immunity remain poorly characterized. Here, we assessed antibody-mediated protection following vaccination or natural infection by passively immunizing transiently immunocompromised mice with graded doses of pooled immune sera before challenge with two CHIKV genotypes or the related Mayaro virus (MAYV). Protection was evaluated using viral loads at 3 days post-infection and clinical parameters. Vaccine- and infection-induced antibodies conferred strong protection against both CHIKV genotypes, even at antibody concentrations nearing ELISA detection limits. In contrast, protection against MAYV required higher titers. We observed no ADE. Our findings align with previous studies supporting durable protection against CHIKV and partial cross-protection against MAYV.

Effect modification of maternal concentrations of per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances on vaccine effectiveness in children up to 6 years of age: The Japan Environment and Children's Study

Kohei Hasegawa, Yuji Inaba, Shoji Saito, Takumi Shibazaki, Miyuki Iwai‐Shimada — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijheh.2026.114924

Epidemiological evidence suggests that per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) exposure may attenuate antibody responses to vaccination. However, vaccine effectiveness as an outcome has not been investigated. This study assessed whether maternal PFAS concentrations during pregnancy modified childhood vaccine effectiveness. We analyzed mother–child pairs from the Japan Environment and Children's Study. Children's vaccination status for varicella, mumps, and influenza was collected via self-reported questionnaires at 2 years of age. Subsequent infection incidence was similarly assessed until 6 years for varicella and mumps, and 3 years for influenza. Maternal plasma samples collected during pregnancy provided PFAS measurements. Logistic regression models, incorporating interaction terms between vaccination status and PFAS concentrations, examined effect modification on both additive (relative excess odds due to interaction) and multiplicative (ratio of odds ratios) scales. Vaccinations were associated with reduced infection risk across PFAS exposure levels for all three diseases. Effect modification assessments revealed that perfluorononanoic acid (PFNA) significantly reduced varicella and mumps vaccine effectiveness on the additive scale. For mumps, the odds ratio for infection among unvaccinated versus vaccinated children (higher values indicate greater vaccine effectiveness) was higher in the lowest PFNA tertile than in the highest, with a relative excess odds due to interaction of 0.94 (95% confidence interval: 0.33–1.54, P = 0.0025). Multiplicative scale effect modifications were not statistically significant. Vaccinations reduced infections in children across all maternal PFAS exposure levels. Evidence of effect modification was limited, isolated, and inconsistent. These analyses are exploratory, and confirmation is warranted.

Development of a Potent QS‐21‐Derived Adjuvant Photoaffinity Probe: Identification of Cathepsin D as the Molecular Receptor

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Abhijit Saha, Alba Mosteiro, Adrián Plata, Carlo Pifferi, Priscila Tonon Baschirotto — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1002/ange.5055772

ABSTRACT The saponin natural product QS‐21 is a potent immunological adjuvant used in human vaccines to enhance the immune response but suffers from scarcity, instability, and dose‐limiting toxicity, precluding its advancement as a stand‐alone adjuvant. Further, putative cellular receptor(s) and its molecular mechanisms of action are unknown, which has prevented the rational design of saponin adjuvants, emphasizing the need for discovering these receptors and elucidating mechanisms. Herein, we have developed novel saponin photoaffinity probes for molecular target identification, merging synthetic chemistry, photoaffinity labeling, and chemical proteomics. One of these potent adjuvants generated superior antibody responses to QS‐21 with less toxicity and enabled identification of Cathepsin D (CatD) as the selective receptor driving saponin adjuvant effects. Binding to surface‐exposed CatD, lipid raft interactions, and lysosomal acidification were shown to be key for CatD‐mediated saponin‐induced immune activation, whereas neither photocrosslinking nor apparent binding was observed in comparative experiments with other lysosomal cathepsins, confirming the specificity of the interaction. These studies highlight the promise of this saponin probe as a potent vaccine adjuvant, unveiling CatD as the molecular target. This breakthrough sets the stage for elucidating the precise mechanisms of saponin immunopotentiation, facilitating rational adjuvant design and development of optimized adjuvant–antigen combinations for future vaccines.

TUMOR MICROENVIRONMENT: EMERGING INSIGHTS INTO CANCER PROGRESSION AND THERAPEUTIC TARGETS - A NARRATIVE REVIEW

— 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.73s.48

The tumor microenvironment (TME) is increasingly recognized as a dynamic and biologically active ecosystem that profoundly influences cancer initiation, progression, metastasis, immune escape, and therapeutic resistance.Rather than functioning as an isolated mass of malignant cells, a tumor consists of complex interactions among cancer cells, immune cells, cancer-associated fibroblasts, endothelial cells, pericytes, extracellular matrix components, soluble mediators, and metabolic factors.These interactions create spatially and temporally heterogeneous niches that can either restrain or promote malignant progression.Tumor-associated macrophages, regulatory T cells, myeloid-derived suppressor cells, dysfunctional dendritic cells, and exhausted T lymphocytes are major contributors to immunosuppression, whereas cancer-associated fibroblasts and extracellular matrix remodeling facilitate invasion, angiogenesis, and treatment resistance.Hypoxia and abnormal tumor vasculature further promote aggressive phenotypes through hypoxia-inducible factors and vascular endothelial growth factor-dependent pathways.The development of immune checkpoint inhibitors targeting programmed cell death protein 1, programmed death-ligand 1, and cytotoxic T-lymphocyte-associated protein 4 has demonstrated that therapeutic manipulation of the TME can produce durable clinical responses.Nevertheless, primary and acquired resistance remain major challenges.Recent advances include combination checkpoint blockade, vascular normalization, targeting of cancer-associated fibroblasts and tumor-associated macrophages, bispecific antibodies, adoptive cellular therapies, personalized cancer vaccines, metabolic interventions, and spatially resolved molecular profiling.This narrative review summarizes the major cellular and non-cellular components of the TME, tumor-immune interactions, angiogenesis and stromal responses, immune checkpoint pathways, clinical implications, recent therapeutic advances, and future opportunities for TME-directed precision oncology.A deeper understanding of the evolving and heterogeneous nature of the TME is essential for developing rational combination strategies capable of converting immunologically resistant tumors into therapeutically responsive states.
