Causal analysis of Alzheimer’s disease molecular data

Rebecca Ting Jiin Loo, Enrico Glaab — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s13195-026-02235-w

Abstract Background Genome-wide studies have revealed many molecular associations with Alzheimer’s disease (AD), but association alone does not identify cause, mechanism, or treatable target. This review surveys methods used to infer causal relationships from AD molecular data and assesses their core findings. Main body We reviewed causal-inference work applied to AD genetics, transcriptomics, epigenomics, proteomics, and metabolomics, covering Mendelian randomization, probabilistic graphical and Bayesian network models, causal reasoning over knowledge graphs, time-resolved methods including Granger causality, and mediation analysis. Genetic-instrument and network methods have identified candidate causal molecules, mapped pathways, and supported some drug targets. However, several associations reported in observational studies have not been supported by genetic analyses, and apparent disagreements between studies often reflect differences in the estimand, the tissue from which genetic instruments are derived, or assumptions of the methods used. Conclusion Brain-relevant genetic instruments, longitudinal multi-omics, single-cell resolution, and triangulation across methods are needed to separate cause from consequence and to prioritize intervention targets for experimental validation.

Rethinking selection criteria for liver transplantation in hepatocellular carcinoma: from morphology to artificial intelligence

Quirino Lai, Silvia Quaresima, Chiara Matrascia, Fabio Melandro — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1080/17474124.2026.2750985

Introduction Liver transplantation (LT) is a uniquely curative treatment for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC), as it removes both the tumor and the underlying cirrhotic liver. However, organ scarcity makes candidate selection a central clinical and ethical issue. Over the past three decades, selection has evolved from simple morphologic thresholds toward increasingly multidimensional models of oncologic risk.Areas covered This review examines the evolution of selection criteria for LT in HCC, from the Milan criteria and subsequent morphology-based expansions to models incorporating tumor biology, serum biomarkers, treatment response, waiting-list dynamics, and artificial intelligence. The literature discussed includes landmark morphologic criteria, AFP-based models, PIVKA-II and AFP-L3 integration, downstaging strategies, dynamic scores such as TRAIN and NYCA, and machine-learning models including MoRAL-AI, TRIUMPH, and TRAIN-AI.Expert opinion The field is moving from categorical eligibility rules toward individualized, continuous risk estimation. Future selection should not aim merely to expand transplant access, but to identify patients most likely to achieve durable oncologic benefit. Artificial intelligence, radiomics, liquid biopsy, and multi-omic profiling may refine risk prediction, but their clinical adoption will require prospective validation, transparency, calibration, transportability, and integration into allocation systems.

Cross-Omics correlation and genomic pattern recognition for accurate tumor detection in oncological surgery decision-making

N Naveenkumar, Dr.DileepKumar Padidem, Nitisha Shrivastava, Abhishek Shrivastava — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s10791-026-10626-8

Abstract A genomic sequence analysis is necessary to determine the cancer-specific genomic patterns for oncological surgery decision-making. However, the existing works overlooked the cross-omics interaction regarding Genomic Pattern Recognition (GPR). Therefore, Chebyshev-Excoffier Heun Density-Based Spatial Clustering of Application with Noise (CEHDBSCAN)-based GPR and Cross-Omics Correlation Analysis (COCA) using Heaviside Function-based Pearson Correlation Coefficient (HeFPCC) is proposed. Primarily, the patient registers on the hospital’s server. Then, the multi-omics data is collected and then encrypted using Legendre Chi Function-based Advanced Encryption Standard (LCFAES). Further, in the hospital server, the secured multi-omics data is decrypted and pre-processed. Afterward, the CEHDBSCAN-based GPR is performed. Then, the features are extracted, and the hidden mutation progress is analyzed using Jacobi Elliptic Function-Hidden Markov Model (JEFHMM). Also, the HeFPCC-based COCA is carried out. Meanwhile, the extracted features are selected using Hawk Fish Walsh Function Optimization Algorithm (HFWaFOA). Finally, the gradients are secured using Differential Privacy (DP); then, the tumor is detected using Deep Shifted and Scaled Sigmoid Alpha Dropout Learning Neural Network (DSSSADLNN). The classified output is further given for decision-making regarding oncological surgery. Thus, the proposed framework effectively recognized the genomic pattern and classified the tumor with an accuracy of 99.05%, showing better performance than existing works.

Integrated microbiomic and metabolomic analysis of irritable bowel syndrome comorbid with psychiatric symptoms

Jiahui Fan, Peiwei Xie, Mei Luo, Lishou Xiong — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12866-026-05801-2

Irritable bowel syndrome (IBS), a chronic functional gastrointestinal disorder, exhibits high comorbidity with psychiatric symptoms. However, a comprehensive evaluation of psychiatric symptoms in IBS and their interactions with the gut microbiota and metabolic disorders is lacking. This study was aimed at investigating the associations among the gut microbiota, serum amino acid metabolites, and different psychiatric comorbidities. Clinical data from 267 patients with IBS and 91 healthy controls (HCs) were collected using validated questionnaires: the IBS Severity Scoring System, Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale, Patient Health Questionnaire-15, Somatic Symptom Disorder-12, and Symptom Checklist-90 (SCL-90). Patients with IBS were stratified into psychiatric comorbidity and non-comorbidity subgroups based on SCL-90 scores. Serum amino acid metabolites and gut microbiota were analyzed using liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry targeted metabolomics and 16 S rRNA gene sequencing, respectively, followed by integrative correlation analyses to explore associations among microbiota, metabolites, and psychological comorbidities. Patients with IBS showed significantly higher prevalence and severity of psychiatric symptoms than HCs, with obsessive-compulsive symptoms, anxiety, and depression being the most prevalent. Preliminary exploratory multi-omics analyses identified symptom-specific microbial associations: increased Eubacterium ramulus in somatization, decreased Agathobacter and increased Tyzzerella in depression, decreased Peptostreptococcaceae in anxiety, and increased Lactobacillus in hostility. Metabolomic profiling identified 10 differential serum metabolites between IBS and HCs, including elevated L-ornithine, urea, and creatine and reduced arginine, homo-arginine, and S-(5-adenosyl)-L-homocysteine; within the IBS cohort, psychiatric comorbidity subgroups showed elevated arginine-pathway metabolites (L-arginine, Nα-acetyl-L-arginine, and argininosuccinic acid) and reduced aromatic (L-tryptophan and L-phenylalanine), branched-chain (L-valine), and neuroactive (L-glutamate and L-aspartate) amino acids. Several psychiatric symptom-related genera, including Ruminococcus , Agathobacter , and Prevotella , showed associations with various amino acid metabolites. Multi-omic integration identified co-occurrence patterns involving Clostridia and Coriobacteriia with somatization and anxiety symptoms, alongside alterations in ornithine-cycle and glutamatergic metabolites. Because the HC omics group was small, between-group findings have limited power and generalizability and should be interpreted as exploratory. Patients with IBS exhibited multidimensional psychiatric symptoms accompanied by symptom-specific shifts in gut microbiota and serum amino acid profiles. Integrated analyses identified candidate microbiota–metabolite–symptom associations. These exploratory findings provide candidate targets for future longitudinal and mechanistic studies.

Occupational environments are associated with distinct oral and gut microbiome–metabolome profiles

Shujie Dou, Guangping Fu, Saili Liu, Jing Han, Qingxiang Mu — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12934-026-03158-9

The profound health impacts of distinct long-term work environments, such as indoor sedentary versus outdoor physical labor, remain poorly understood at the microbial and metabolic levels. To address this, we used an integrated metagenomic and metabolomic approach to systematically compare the oral and gut ecosystems of indoor sedentary workers (university teachers) and outdoor physical laborers (construction and transportation workers). Our analysis revealed stark, niche-specific differences. Outdoor laborers showed higher salivary microbial diversity, whereas indoor workers had higher gut microbial diversity coupled with a marked reduction in microbial resistance genes. Crucially, statistical mediation analysis of the ‘environment-microbiome-metabolite’ axis uncovered fundamentally different regulatory logics in the two niches. The oral ecosystem operated on a ‘push-and-pull’ model of mixed mediation; notably, the indoor sedentary lifestyle was positively associated with the key metabolic risk marker, uric acid, statistically mediated by a reduced abundance of purine-competing Prevotella species. In contrast, the gut ecosystem displayed a highly uniform ‘many-to-one’ architecture, where diverse microbial shifts converged to statistically mediate a reduction in a small set of metabolites, primarily a single exogenous steroid derivative. Furthermore, these multi-omics signatures were highly predictive, with salivary metabolome data distinguishing the two groups with up to 98.04% accuracy. This study suggests that multi-omics profiles may serve as a sensitive ‘biological archive’ of an individual’s work environment. These highly predictive signatures are promising candidates for biomarker development in health monitoring and forensic inference, and highlight potential microbe-metabolite targets for future intervention studies tailored to lifestyle-specific risks.

Mechanistic integration of genetic alterations, PTM signaling, and tumor microenvironment remodeling in pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Santhosh Suresh, Sundaresan Sivapatham, Magesh Ramasamy — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s12672-026-05927-9

Pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) is one of the deadliest malignancies, with late diagnosis, rapid progression, considerable therapy resistance, and low overall survival. Growing evidence suggests that PDAC progression is not determined by simple genetic events but rather depends on a multitude of genetic susceptibilities, post-translational modifications (PTMs), metabolic dysregulation, chronic inflammation, and dynamic remodeling of the tumor microenvironment (TME). Germline and somatic alterations, including KRAS, TP53, BRCA1/2, ATM, and SMAD4, impact genomic instability and oncogenic signaling, whereas inflammatory and metabolic pathologies like chronic pancreatitis, obesity, and type 2 diabetes mellitus promote acinar-to-ductal metaplasia (ADM) and tumor initiation. PTMs such as phosphorylation, ubiquitination, acetylation, methylation, and glycosylation are among the key regulators of signaling pathways including MAPK, PI3K/AKT, NF-κB, and STAT3, affecting the plasticity of cellular organization, epithelial–mesenchymal transition (EMT), immune evasion, and therapeutic resistance. In parallel, the desmoplastic TME, composed of cancer-associated fibroblasts (CAFs), extracellular matrix (ECM), immune suppressor cells, and hypoxic metabolic conditions, potentiates tumor advancement through co-signaling cascades with tumor cells. The objective of this review is to provide a comprehensive, systems-based analysis of PDAC by correlating the changes at the genomic level, PTM-mediated signaling, and microenvironmental factors, along with mapping how genomic, PTM, and microenvironmental changes interact to drive cancer, progression, and treatment resistance. In addition, the importance of multi-omics analysis, which includes genomics, proteomics, and metabolomics, for biomarker identification and precision medicine is emphasized. Whereas existing research have tended to study either genetics, PTMs, or TMEs separately, this review instead traces the mechanistic thread that connects them into a single integrated framework. This allows the researcher not only to develop a deeper understanding of the biology of diseases but also to discover new opportunities for therapy as well.

Liquid-liquid phase separation in cancer radiotherapy and immunotherapy: emerging roles and translational opportunities

Chenchen Dong, Cong Shang, Bing Han, Ziyi Liu, Jiaoyang Wang — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12943-026-02813-4

Abstract Liquid-liquid phase separation (LLPS) is a physicochemical process through which biomolecules assemble into membraneless condensates via weak, multivalent interactions. LLPS has emerged as a central biological principle that helps explain intracellular spatiotemporal compartmentalization, signal transduction, and stress responses. Dysregulated LLPS contributes to tumor initiation, progression, immune evasion, and treatment resistance; however, its cell-type-specific functions and relevance to radiotherapy and immunotherapy remain incompletely integrated. In this review, we critically synthesize recent mechanistic and translational advances linking LLPS to cancer radiotherapy and immunotherapy. Condensates may either support therapeutic resistance and immune evasion or enhance radiosensitivity and antitumor immunity, depending on their molecular composition, subcellular localization, cell type, and treatment context. We examine how condensate regulation connects radiation-induced DNA damage repair with innate immune activation and evaluate emerging therapeutic strategies that target disease-associated condensates. We also assess LLPS-associated abnormal condensates as candidate biomarkers for prognosis and prediction of tumor response to radiotherapy and immunotherapy. Tissue-specific LLPS regulation across tumor types, dynamic condensate remodeling under therapeutic stress, and rational strategies combining phase-separation modulation with immune-checkpoint inhibitors and radiotherapy require further investigation. Integrating multi-omics data with artificial intelligence-based algorithms may help construct systematic LLPS-related therapeutic-event maps and predictive models, thereby prioritizing experimentally testable hypotheses for personalized cancer management. With rigorous mechanistic validation and prospective clinical evaluation, selective modulation of disease-associated condensates may enhance radiosensitivity and strengthen antitumor immunity.

Universal Safety Kernel v4: AI-Driven Rescue and Recovery of the Chernobyl RBMK Reactor

This report presents Version 4 of the Universal Safety Kernel, applying the proprietary Hybrid Agent-Based (ABM) and Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE) engine to the most infamous safety-critical failure in history: the Chernobyl RBMK-1000 reactor (April 1986). This simulation demonstrates how an AI-driven safety controller can prevent the catastrophic "prompt criticality" explosion by overriding human error, injecting neutron-absorbing Boron, and executing a controlled recovery. Key Innovations in Version 4: - Overriding the Human Factor: The AI detects the operator's attempt to insert the graphite-tipped control rods (AZ-5 button) and instantly overrides the command to prevent the positive void reactivity spike. - Emergency Boron Injection: Instead of relying on flawed mechanical rods, the AI injects Boron to rapidly halt the chain reaction. - Boron Flush & Auto-Restart: The system demonstrates a complete recovery protocol. Once the core temperature stabilizes, the AI flushes the Boron, carefully withdraws the control rods, and restarts the reactor to a safe, steady-state power level (~37%). Simulation Results: The engine proves that the Universal Safety Kernel can detect the error in real-time, neutralize the threat without structural damage, and restore the reactor to safe operation. This cross-disciplinary case study serves as a definitive proof-of-concept for AI-controlled safety systems in critical energy infrastructure.

Statistical Model Checking in 2026: Simulation, Learning, and Exhaustive Verification

Arnd Hartmanns, Jan Křetínský, Kim G. Larsen, Sabine Rieder — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-40124-3_10

Abstract Statistical model checking (SMC) analyses stochastic models by simulation: it samples many runs, evaluates the property of interest on each of them, and returns an estimate together with a statistical guarantee. It only needs the ability to draw runs from a model or system, which makes it widely applicable—at the cost of statistical instead of hard guarantees. Yet rare events, unbounded properties, and nondeterminism remain hard for simulation alone. In 2026, SMC is therefore practised as simulation, learning, and exhaustive verification working together. We recall the essence of SMC, and survey the work of the past few years on these three problems and SMC’s statistical methods themselves. We then examine how SMC combines with reinforcement learning and with probabilistic model checking, and introduce the contributions to the SMC track at ISoLA 2026.

Files in this record: the full Dataset Passport (PDF), the technical description with the complete data dictionary, the audit of all 202 model constants (CSV), the register of 43 literature sources (CSV), the validation report (PDF) and the free sample archive. About the full dataset The drug that corrupts the number it is dosed from Piperacillin-tazobactam is dosed from the creatinine clearance that the clinician estimates from serum creatinine. The drug itself raises serum creatinine in many patients without reducing filtration, because it suppresses tubular secretion of creatinine; in a minority it also injures the kidney. A raised creatinine lowers the estimated clearance, the clinician cuts the dose, exposure falls below what the true filtration requires — and the susceptible bacteria are killed less while the resistant ones are never killed at all. The loop works in the opposite direction to the vancomycin loop: it lowers exposure and favours resistance. This dataset closes that loop inside the equations. Antibiotic Resistance Feedback 100k is 100,000 virtual hospitalised adults treated for seven days (168 h), simulated under two calibrated scenarios, with the observed hospital record and the ground truth stored side by side for every patient. Both sides are generated by one mechanistic model for the same patient and linked by patient_id. Every patient is recorded twice Observed (what a hospital archive holds)Truth (what no archive holds) Noisy serum creatinine at eight daily draws (0–168 h), with biological (4.5 %) and analytical (1.4–3.5 %) variationTrue filtration rate and true creatinine, hourly for 5,000 patients and daily for all The dose chosen after each draw from the creatinine-based clearance estimateTrue free piperacillin concentration, true daily exposure and the dose the true filtration would have required — dosing error patient by patient Pathogen named 24 h after the culture; susceptibility (MIC on the twofold grid, EUCAST category) reported at 48 hTrue MIC, true bacterial burden of the susceptible and the resistant subpopulation, hourly for 5,000 patients KDIGO acute-kidney-injury label computed from the noisy draws; number and timing of dose reductionsTrue AKI stage, share of every creatinine rise caused by secretion suppression versus true injury, cause of each rise The observed tables hold no truth column — checked mechanically. The truth exists because every value was computed from a model with 202 audited constants, each traced to a published source. What it lets you do Train and test AKI models against a known cause. The true AKI stage, the true injury share of the creatinine rise and a flag for rises explained by secretion alone sit next to the observed label. Measure how much observed AKI is real. The observed label is about twice the true incidence; models and protocols can be scored against both. Measure dosing error patient by patient. Dose chosen on the estimated clearance against the dose the true filtration would have given, at each of seven dosing draws; exposure deficit, unwarranted cuts and excess doses. Isolate the effect of the feedback loop. Every patient is re-simulated without the renal layer (truth_noloop), with a fixed full dose and with dosing from the true filtration (truth_counterfactual). Compare dosing regimes on the same patients with the same noise. Label dosing by indication, deferred renal adjustment, two extended-infusion regimens and susceptibility-guided switching (regime_arms). Work under two assumptions about true kidney injury. Scenario A and scenario B bracket the share of patients susceptible to true injury (6.2 % and 12.9 %). Teach and demonstrate. A complete, documented closed loop with known truth, small enough to run on a laptop. Nine tables per scenario, 18 Parquet files 100,000 patients per scenario. Scenario B has the same patients, admission doses, noise and pathogens as scenario A and differs only in the renal-loop parameters carried by the patients. The cohort Admission covariates follow a published cohort treated with piperacillin-tazobactam (Kadomura et al. 2019): median age 76 years (range 21–96), 37.4 % female, median body weight 76 kg, median true filtration rate 62.5 mL/min (interquartile range 40.2–80.8), median baseline creatinine 0.92 mg/dL. The model Seven days of therapy (168 h), vectorised fourth-order Runge–Kutta scheme over the whole cohort, step 0.1 h (results unchanged at 0.05 h and 0.025 h). The simulated clinician doses from the label dosing table of piperacillin-tazobactam applied to the creatinine clearance estimated at each daily draw; the first dose comes from the admission draw. Pharmacological Evaluation of the Analgesic Activity of Zingiber officinale Rhizome Extract in Experimental Rats

Zaid Shamshee, Mehta Parulben Durgashankar, Shweta Gogate, Rita Mourya, Vansh Barapatre — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.78s.31

Background: Pain is a common disease-associated symptom of tissue damage or localized inflammation, which greatly contributes both qualitatively and quantitatively to the health burden and to the quality of life of patients all over the world. While non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) and opioid analgesics are still considered the standard treatments for pain, these drugs are often linked to gastrointestinal toxicity, renal issues, cardiovascular side effects, tolerance, and dependence. As such, it has been interested in the identification of plantbased, more effective, and safer analgesics to improve the profile of the analgesics. Zingiber officinale Roscoe (ginger), a medicinal plant with extensive application in traditional medicine, is known to possess many bioactive phytoconstituents, such as gingerols, shogaols, paradols, and zingerone, that are said to have anti-inflammatory, antioxidant, and analgesic properties. Methodology: The objective of the present research was to study the peripheral and central analgesic activity of hydroalcoholic extract of Zingiber officinale rhizome in a set of validated models for peripheral nociception and central effect in experimental Wistar rats. Materials and Methods: The rhizomes of Zingiber officinale were authenticated, shade-dried, powdered, and extracted with a hydroalcoholic solvent. Preliminary phytochemical screening was conducted to find the different major classes of bioactive compounds. Before efficacy studies, acute oral toxicity was assessed based on OECD guideline 423. All the rats were randomly divided into 4 groups with 6 rats in each group: Vehicle control, Standard dose of drug, Low dose of extract, and High dose of extract. This peripheral analgesic activity was measured by investigating writhing induced by the administration of acetic acid, while the central analgesic effect was determined by the hot plate and tail flick test methods. Diclofenac sodium and Morphine were used as positive reference drugs in the peripheral and central analgesic models, respectively. All the experimental results were presented as Mean ± SEM and analyzed by one-way ANOVA and Tukey's multiple comparison test, with a pvalue of < 0.05 considered statistically significant. Result: The hydroalcoholic extract showed the presence of phenolics, flavonoids, tannins, terpenoids, and other bioactive phytoconstituents. The acute toxicity test results also revealed that the extract did not cause any deaths or behavioral changes at the dosages tested. The results of the acetic acid-induced writhing model showed that the extract could significantly reduce the number of writhes, in a dose-dependent manner, as compared to the control group (p < 0.001). Likewise, significant prolongation of reaction latency was observed in both the hot plate and tail flick tests, attributes of central analgesic activity. The higher dose demonstrated higher levels of analgesic activity and effects similar to those of the standard analgesics. Pharmacological activity observed is probably due to its inhibitory effect on prostaglandin synthesis, its central and peripheral anti-nociceptive effect, its antiinflammatory effect, and its antioxidant effect. Conclusion: The following results showed that the hydroalcoholic rhizome extract of Zingiber officinale exerts considerable peripheral and central analgesic (anti-inflammatory activity) in experimental rats. The dosedependent effect on the analgesic modulation seems to be due to the synergistic effect of the presence of gingerols, shogaols, and related phytoconstituents. The findings were suggestive of traditional roles of ginger in pain management and indicated that Zingiber officinale can be a potential source of plant-based alternative pain management agents without any side effects. Hence, there is a need for further pharmacological investigations, mechanistic studies on a molecular basis, and clinical trials that are well-designed to validate its therapeutic potential.

— 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.73s.76

Chronic wound management represents one of the most significant unmet clinical and economic challenges in contemporary healthcare, with an annual global burden exceeding USD 22 billion and disproportionately poor clinical translation rates for advanced wound therapeutics despite substantial research investment.Quality by Design (QbD), formalised through ICH Q8 (R2), Q9, and Q10, offers a rigorous, prospective framework for wound formulation development by systematically linking material attributes and process parameters to patient-relevant quality outcomes.This narrative review critically evaluates the application of QbD principles encompassing the Quality Target Product Profile (QTPP), Critical Quality Attributes (CQAs), Critical Material Attributes (CMAs), Critical Process Parameters (CPPs), risk assessment methodologies, and Design of Experiments (DoE) strategies across the principal advanced wound therapeutic platforms: lipid nanocarriers, polymeric nanoparticles, hydrogels, electrospun scaffolds, biopolymeric films, and stimulus-responsive smart dressings.Published evidence demonstrates that DoE-based Response Surface Methodology (RSM) consistently delivers 20-40% improvements in key CQAs and produces statistically validated design spaces aligned with regulatory expectations.The review further examines translational barriers including scale-up variability, in vitro-in vivo correlation (IVIVC) gaps, and regulatory classification challenges, and identifies artificial intelligence-assisted QbD, digital twin manufacturing, and organ-on-chip wound models as high-priority emerging tools for bridging these gaps.A structured perspective on pre-submission requirements and regulatory alignment with ICH Q8-Q10 is provided for researchers targeting Scopus-indexed pharmaceutical journals.

Clinical Profile of Echocardiographically Estimated Pulmonary Hypertension in Patients with Non Dialysis-dependent Chronic Kidney Disease Stages 3-5: A Cross-sectional Study

Gunishetti Adithya, RM Anitha, Buchipudi Sandeep Kumar Reddy, Bhargav Kiran Gaddam, Hemachandar Radhakrishnan — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.7860/jcdr/2026/91602.24619

Introduction: Pulmonary Hypertension (PHTN) is increasingly recognised as a clinically significant complication of Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD), even before initiation of dialysis. However, the clinical profile, echocardiographic pulmonary pressure estimates, and biochemical correlates of PHTN in Non Dialysis-Dependent CKD (NDD-CKD) stages 3-5 remain incompletely characterised. Aim: To describe the clinical profile of elevated pulmonary pressures in NDD-CKD stage 3-5, estimate Pulmonary Artery Systolic Pressure (PASP) by echocardiography and to determine PHTN prevalence and severity, and explore correlations between biochemical parameters and PHTN severity. Materials and Methods: The present hospital-based crosssectional study enrolled 84 NDD-CKD stage 3-5 patients at a tertiary care centre, Mahatma Gandhi Medical College and Research Institute, Sri Balaji Vidyapeeth (Deemed to be University), Puducherry, India. PASP was estimated by transthoracic echocardiography and mean Pulmonary Artery Pressure (mPAP) was derived using the Chemla formula (mPAP=0.61×PASP+2). Elevated pulmonary pressure was defined as mPAP ≥20 mmHg and categorised as mild (≥20- 40 mmHg), moderate (>40-55 mmHg), or severe (>55 mmHg); values <20 mmHg were considered normal. Demographic data, co-morbidities, CKD stage, and laboratory investigations were measured. Non parametric stage-wise comparisons with Bonferroni correction, Spearman correlations, ordered-stage trend analyses, and binary logistic regression were performed using Statistical Package for the Social Sciences (SPSS) version 24.0. p-value of <0.05 was considered statistically significant. Results: Mean age was 48.18±14.07; 49 (58.3%) were male. Hypertension (HTN) was present in 70 (83.3%) participants, Diabetes Mellitus (DM) in 37 (44.0%), and smoking in 45 (53.6%). Mean mPAP was 36.29±16.68 mmHg; Elevated pulmonary pressure was present in 59 (70.2%) participants, comprising mild elevation in 20 (23.8%), moderate elevation in 24 (28.6%), and severe elevation in 15 (17.9%). mPAP increased significantly across CKD stages (p=0.006), with an increasing ordered-stage trend (p=0.0006) and rising PHTN severity (rs=0.288; p=0.007). mPAP correlated positively with serum urea (rs=0.335; p=0.001) and creatinine (rs =0.332; p=0.002), and negatively with estimated Glomerular Filtration Rate (eGFR) (rs=−0.339; p=0.001)-all surviving Bonferroni correction. HTN was an independent predictor of PHTN on both univariate (OR 4.157; 95% CI 1.263- 13.680; p=0.019) and multivariable analysis (adjusted Odds Ratio (aOR)=5.425; 95% CI=1.416-20.783; p=0.014). Conclusion: PHTN burden is high in NDD-CKD stage 3-5 and increases with declining renal function. HTN independently predicts PHTN, highlighting the importance of left-heartmediated mechanisms. Structured cardiopulmonary evaluation is warranted in advanced pre-dialysis CKD.

An asteroseismic age estimate for the Pleiades using a neural-network emulator of rotating δ Scuti pulsation models

Anuj Gautam, Simon J. Murphy — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.66977/xsci.2610.001n

Asteroseismology of δ Sct stars has strong potential for determining the ages of young stars through individual mode fitting. We present a neural-network emulator trained on approximately 46 million rotating pulsation models. From stellar mass, metallicity, age, and equatorial rotation velocity, it predicts 12 classical observables, two asteroseismic scalars, and the p mode, f mode and low-order g mode frequencies for degrees ℓ=0-3, including the individual m components of rotationally split multiplets. We couple the emulator to nested sampling through a multivariate likelihood that propagates correlated emulator residuals, making full Bayesian posterior exploration practical. Applied to five δ Sct stars in the Pleiades observed by TESS, the framework gives mutually consistent individual asteroseismic ages of 107-117 Myr. Combining their age posteriors gives a cluster age of 113.3±4.5 Myr (statistical), consistent with the 112±5 Myr lithium-depletion-boundary age and the 110-160 Myr range from rotating-isochrone fitting. Together with spectroscopic vsini, the inferred equatorial velocities also provide post-fit inclination estimates. Separate perturbed-grid tests using 100 matched synthetic pairs per input-physics variation give median absolute age shifts of 2.1, 2.3, and 5.2 Myr for |Δf_ov|=0.005, |Δα_MLT|=0.1, and |ΔY|=0.02, respectively; the corresponding 90th percentile shifts are 6.5, 7.6, and 16.2 Myr. These tests describe conditional population sensitivities, not a systematic uncertainty on the Pleiades age. Together, the cluster-age agreement and the scale of these input-physics sensitivities support δ Sct asteroseismology as a complementary age estimation method for young stars.

Ektropic Immanence: Cosmological Recurrence and the Conditions for Physical Memory - Observable Recurrence, Retained Physical State, and the Conditions for Cosmological Renewal

Riaan De Beer — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23439687

Abstract A recurrent observable universe need not return to its complete earlier physical state. Ektropic immanence asks whether recurring macroscopic regimes can coexist with accumulating recoverable history, later causal re-access, and renewed work-producing gradients under one consistent dynamics. We specify these obligations through operational boundaries, record ensembles, admissible decoders, and resource accounting. Standard capacity and initial-information bounds constrain strong retention. Finite reversible, oscillator, and quantum examples demonstrate selected obligations; none identifies the historical origin of its inputs. A sourced canonical two-field gravitational analogue yields conditional signed field response but obstructs the positive-Ḣ intervals of an illustrative growing-cycle geometry. That geometry also exhibits discrete metric symmetry, bounded horizon access, and past null-incompleteness. An identifiability analysis distinguishes hidden-state influence from evidence for a prior cosmic epoch. Comparisons with cyclic, quantum-bounce, conformal-crossover, and quantum-memory work delimit substantial overlap; originality remains unestablished. A multi-domain computational specification, candidate registry, and evidence map organize tests across the broad carrier catalogue. This is a foundational disclosure of conditional results and open physical hypotheses. No stable recurrent cosmological completion, uniquely identifying cosmological prediction, empirical detection, or proof of indefinite renewal is presented. Keywords cyclic cosmology; information retention; non-Markovian dynamics; generalized entropy; recurrence; cosmological inference.

A Quantified Open Region of Carrier Non-Identifiability in Fragment-Averaged Pointer Information: A Parameterized Interval Certificate for Four Qubits

Yoichi Tsujisawa — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23438613

This unrefereed research preprint establishes a computer-assisted, quantitatively certified non-identifiability result for fragment-size-averaged Holevo information in an N=4 environment with two equiprobable pure pointer-conditioned branches. For every point in an explicitly specified closed rational rectangle of coordinate area 4 × 10^-14, two realizations of the complete averaged Holevo information curve exist: one with minimum pure-common-rest carrier size two and another with carrier size four. The proof combines a uniform parameterized contraction mapping, directed interval arithmetic with a second interval implementation, and an intermediate-value construction. Reproducibility materials include the certification programs, frozen numerical certificates, SHA-256 checksums, and a standalone 200-state numerical comparison against direct 16-dimensional partial traces. Both interval implementations share the underlying analytic Holevo reduction and do not constitute independent symbolic proofs. The conclusions are restricted to N=4, equiprobable pure branches, fragment-size-averaged Holevo information, and the specified carrier definition. They do not establish non-identifiability for arbitrary N, mixed states, unequal priors, or individual labeled-fragment information. This is an unrefereed preprint, not a formally verified or peer-reviewed mathematical theorem. Comprehensive novelty priority is not claimed. Licensing: The manuscript and original documentation/data are provided under CC BY 4.0 where applicable. Original Python research code is provided under the MIT License. The accompanying reproducibility archive specifies licenses on a per-file basis. AI assistance disclosure: OpenAI ChatGPT assisted with mathematical analysis, Python implementation, numerical and interval verification, manuscript preparation, and quality assurance. Claude contributed critical assessment and adversarial review assistance. Neither AI system is a coauthor or an external scientific referee. The human author, Yoichi Tsujisawa, assumes responsibility for the research and its publication.

SELF-CALIBRATING NANO-NEURAL INTERFACES AND REFERENCE LINEAGE AT THE LIMIT Built-In References × Drift Attribution × Independent Functional Requalification STARLI / Institute XXIII / Research Flagship 17 PUBLICATION DESCRIPTION When a long-running neural interface changes, is the cause the sensor, the surrounding tissue, or the decoder? An adaptive algorithm can restore predictive accuracy while obscuring the physical origin of its own correction. This book proposes a rigorous, falsifiable architecture for self-calibrating nano-neural interfaces that must retain the scientific history of every reference and every update. Its central axiom is that self-calibration does not imply self-certification: measurement claims, permission to change, electrode safety and functional benefit demand independent evidence. The first coupling is the existence of physically traceable internal references that can be challenged against electrically and temporally independent standards. The second is a source-aware drift-diagnostic process that distinguishes electronics gain, electrode-tissue transfer, tissue-like surrogate changes, changing source signals and model/feature drift, explicitly recording instances of nonidentifiability. The third is external functional requalification, which can reject an apparently successful update after hidden-domain testing, material or reference replacement, loss of selectivity, missing raw observations, or inability to reproduce the frozen predecessor. This research monograph is divided into seven causal layers and 82 chapters. Five major subsections of each chapter are assigned independent pages, yielding 410 separately paginated technical essays. Thirty gold mathematical derivations are linked from chapter-opening equations to discriminating experiments. The mathematics includes augmented state-space observability, nonlinear observation functions, source-separation Jacobians, Kalman innovations, impedance spectroscopy, covariance propagation, change-point statistics, conformal coverage, selective risk, hard-veto constrained update eligibility, reference-bridge covariance, exact rollback and cross-laboratory requalification. A displayed formula is a candidate analytical instrument with stated dimensions and assumptions, not a discovered biological law. Two hundred detailed Research Gates are preserved at the rear: 82 interrogate source identifiability, another 82 challenge independent physical and evidential hard vetoes, and 36 require third-party reconstruction of a frozen reference history. Thirty-three Phoenix Answer Embryos propose revisable architectures, beginning with the Neural Interface Calibration Passport. The Passport ties an interface's physical identity to the raw analog and digitized record, calibrated unit chain, hardware reference ancestors, model and feature checksums, reasons for changing any reference, failed updates, revoked permissions, blinded requalification and rollback. These are proposed evidence structures, not commercially validated devices. The DOI bibliography contains 18 unique external research records covering chronic intracortical signal instability, low-dimensional BCI stabilization, decoder adaptation, long-term electrode-tissue behavior, complex impedance metrology, electrochemical reference limits, neural interface materials and conformal uncertainty under covariate shift. Selected publisher and PubMed pages were consulted for source metadata. Their findings cannot establish efficacy of the new self-calibration architecture; source-specific claims and independent replication would be necessary.

Knowing the Collective: Temporal Cohort Membership and Small-Cell Governance as Foundations for Cohort Negentropic Assessment

Lu-An Chiu — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23430722

Part IV of Volume II (the outward extensions) of the H-DKPS series on physician-governed clinical AI (知衡八法). The redacted code package is included as a separate file. Clinical problem. Patients who share a workplace, a night-shift rota or a neighbourhood share more than an address. They share exposures — noise, dust, heat, disrupted sleep, the same air — that leave traces in many individual records before any single record looks unusual. A clinic that sees several workers from one factory, or several residents of one district, holds information about that group that no single encounter reveals. It also holds a responsibility: group-level statements about a small number of identifiable people can disclose what each individual's record would have kept private. Engineering problem. A clinical decision system built to reason about one patient at a time — as the system examined in this series is — has no concept of a group. Adding one raises two problems that must be solved before any group analysis is attempted. The first is membership: who belonged to which group, and when — because people change jobs and move house, and a group defined only by the present misattributes the past. The second is disclosure: in a single clinic, most groups are small, and small counts identify people, directly or by subtraction. Approach. We specify and implement the foundation for cohort assessment as two parts. A cohort registry records group membership with periods: workplace groups (with shift pattern, exposures and occupation as membership attributes) and community groups at two levels (district and the village beneath it); leaving a group records a date and never deletes the membership; addresses are reduced to city, district and village, with city names matched against the official list so that recognition errors cannot split one place into several; and every write is hash-chained. A small-cell governance gate, following the published rule of Taiwan's health-data science centre for the release of statistical results, suppresses any cell of one or two people, applies complementary suppression so that no suppressed group can be recovered by subtraction, rolls small villages up into their district, exports suppressed cells only as a merged category without special symbols, outputs aggregates only, and records every export with its stated purpose. Population analysis itself, and any use of group membership in prescribing, are deliberately not built. Evidence. This is an architecture study; all evidence is from guard suites on synthetic data. The registry guard (29 checks) verifies data minimisation, type rules, temporal membership, idempotent joins, hash-chain integrity and intake wiring; six mutations — among them returning the street address, deleting on leave and skipping city-name matching — are each caught. The governance guard (24 checks) verifies primary and complementary suppression, roll-up, symbol-free merged export and identifier screening; an exhaustive property check over 3,000 random tables confirms that no visible cell contains one or two people and that every suppressed set is either empty or at least two cells totalling at least three; six mutations are each caught, and one candidate mutation was found to be equivalent and is reported as such. Contribution. Population-level negentropic assessment in a small clinic is first a problem of governance, not of analysis. Temporal membership and small-cell suppression with complementary protection can be specified as checkable properties, implemented before any analysis exists, and verified to fail when weakened. Not peer reviewed.

BZ Unified Theory and the BZ Particle: Effective Models, Mathematical Foundations, and Experimental Tests

Fangcun Zhang — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34651965

This monograph examines the proposed BZ unified theory and BZ particle through effective models, mathematical analysis, measurement, and experimental testing. Starting from Belousov–Zhabotinsky chemical systems, it develops a framework for distinguishing established mechanisms, mathematical results, modeling assumptions, and hypotheses requiring empirical support.Topics include reaction kinetics, reaction–diffusion equations, stability and pattern formation, image-based measurements, statistical inference, information measures, energy balances, and entropy production. The analysis addresses model identifiability, uncertainty, calibration, reproducibility, and the conditions under which theoretical predictions can be compared with observations.The proposed BZ particle is examined as a hypothesis concerning localized dynamical structures, with attention to observable signatures, competing explanations, and falsifiable tests. Broader claims concerning unification, agency, consciousness, and cosmology are assessed in relation to their assumptions and evidential requirements.The monograph presents a research framework and proposed tests rather than treating its central hypotheses as experimentally established discoveries.

TRUST-BASED PSYCHOSOCIAL STABILISATION OF FAMILIES IN CRISIS: A PREVENTIVE HEALTH AND SOCIAL CARE APPROACH IN CHILD PROTECTION SERVICES IN SLOVAKIA

Soňa Šrobárová, Filip Gerec, Janka Bursová — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.31612/2616-4868.7.2026.18

Aim. To explore the psychosocial needs of families in crisis involved in child protection and social guardianship services in Slovakia and to identify how parents perceived the role of social workers during crisis stabilisation. The study also examined the public health relevance of trust-based support for psychosocial and mental health, prevention of crisis escalation and access to health and social care.Materials and methods. A qualitative exploratory study was conducted with adult parents or legal guardians from ten families with minor children and recent or current contact with child protection and social guardianship services. Semi-structured interviews were anonymised and analysed using open coding, axial coding and thematic analysis. Selected codes were descriptively quantified only to increase transparency of qualitative interpretation and not to estimate statistical prevalence.Results. Eight interrelated themes were identified: accumulation of crisis factors, emotional destabilisation, threat to child well-being, practical service navigation, trust-based psychosocial support, barriers to help-seeking, coordinated field intervention and partial stabilisation. Child-related difficulties appeared in all ten interviews, practical help with institutions or documents in eight interviews and shame, fear or mistrust in seven interviews. Families described service navigation, accompaniment, counselling referrals, repeated contact and interinstitutional coordination as mechanisms that reduced fear and improved access to support.Conclusions. Families valued support that combined child protection responsibility with practical guidance, repeated contact, non-judgemental communication and coordination between services. Trust-based psychosocial stabilisation is proposed as a preventive health and social care model linking emotional containment, service navigation and coordinated field intervention. In public health terms, this approach may help reduce crisis overload, protect child and parental mental health and strengthen timely access of vulnerable families to integrated support.

Evaluating Efficiency Differences in Supply Chain Practices: Evidence from Indian SMEs Using the Mann–Whitney U Test

— 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.73s.134

Small and medium enterprises (SMEs) supply the majority of India's manufacturing employment, yet the efficiency consequences of how they organise their supply chains remain thinly documented relative to large-firm evidence.This study compares supply chain efficiency between SMEs operating integrated supply chain practices and those operating traditional, fragmented practices, using a cross-sectional survey of 400 registered Indian SMEs and the Mann-Whitney U test.Efficiency is measured across six domains procurement and sourcing, inventory management, logistics and distribution, information integration, supplier relationships, and demand planning plus a composite index.Because the response distributions are ordinal, non-normal in every domain, and heteroscedastic across arms, a rank-based test is the appropriate inferential instrument; the analysis reports tie-corrected z statistics, Cliff's δ with bootstrap confidence intervals, and Hodges-Lehmann shift estimates rather than p-values alone.Integrated-practice firms rank significantly higher on the composite index (U = 24,416.0,z = 5.90, p < .001,δ = 0.360) and on five of the six domains after Holm correction.The advantage is largest for information integration (δ = 0.416) and demand planning (δ = 0.307), and statistically indistinguishable from zero for supplier relationships (δ = 0.063, p = .299).The composite result survives rank analysis of covariance on firm size, enterprise resource planning presence, export orientation and age, and an exactly matched 113-pair subsample.The pattern suggests that practice integration in Indian SMEs delivers its returns through informational and planning coordination rather than through relational quality, which appears to be achieved by traditional firms through other means.Implications for SME managers, cluster development agencies and non-parametric practice in operations research are discussed.

Quantum Information and the Fundamental Phenomenality Hypothesis: A Representation Theorem, Non-Implication Results, and Witness Constraints on Constitutive Quantum Claims

Caesar Martinez — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.34625610

The Fundamental Phenomenality Hypothesis (FPH) identifies phenomenal consciousness with states of a hierarchical generative model, and defines its self-reference component through conditional mutual information, which admits a natural quantum lift. We separate a representational extension, in which classical information measures are replaced by quantum ones, from a constitutive extension, in which a certified nonclassical resource makes a nonredundant causal difference to an FPH variable. For the first we prove a representation theorem: with independently anchored roles in finite dimensions, the lifted functional is well defined, recovers the parent theory exactly on role-compatible product-basis-diagonal states, is invariant under an explicit gauge group, and carries bounded role ambiguity and open-system error through explicit uncertainty bounds, requiring no auxiliary coupling constant and no privileged classicalization map; among lifts linear in the marginal entropies it is the unique one with the correct classical limit. For the second we prove non-implication results: entangled and classical realizations can share every quantity the functional uses, every finite causal experiment admits an exact classical stochastic model, and a scalar measurement cannot separate a small quantum perturbation from a tuned ordinary one to first order. Radical-pair spin chemistry then supplies exact coherence witnesses together with control-theoretic and phase-versus-loss obstructions to implementing them, each stated within its hypotheses. An engineered construction separates a quantum implementation from every matched separable one, and the separation can be certified from three correlators. We close with identifiability results for the parent framework.

Replication package for "Evaluating models of human choice when feedback format changes: Input-representation controls, identifiability checks and second-task tests"

Yunan Zhang, Jingjing Fan, Yanxiao Liu, Wenna Zhang, Chunjie Yan — 2026-10-17 — openalex

Identifiability of Gravitational Field Equations from Restricted Physical Probes

Yan Godin — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23431804

Restricted physical probes — null-horizon thermodynamics, cosmological observations — do not determine gravitational field equations uniquely. They determine equivalence classes of field equations under a probe-induced equivalence relation. We formalize gravitational reconstruction as theories → field equations → observables, define the probe class [E] in the quotient space, and study reconstruction as intersection of the probe class with covariantly admissible field equations (characterized in a companion paper via Noether identities). Covariance can eliminate the class, select a representative, or reduce it to finite- or infinite-dimensional moduli. We prove the null-kernel lemma, quantify the collapse of functional degeneracy to a one-parameter residual (the cosmological constant) under Einstein conservation, exhibit operator-level degeneracy in scalar-tensor gravity, and distinguish probe blindness of field equations from non-identifiability of theories as different quotients. The inverse problem is layered: probe blindness → covariant completion → dynamical closure → theory identifiability.

TESTING THE IMPACT OF REFUGEE IMMIGRATION ON INFLATION SUB-GROUPS IN THE WORLD’S LEADING REFUGEE HOST COUNTRY: FOURIER CAUSALITY APPROACH

Merve Kocaman — 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.14780/muiibd.1674861

This study investigates the causal relationship between the refugee population in Türkiye and sub-categories of inflation. Employing econometric analysis on data spanning the period from 2003 to 2024, the research first utilizes the Fourier ADF unit root test to determine the stationarity properties of the variables. Subsequently, the direction of causality is examined through the application of both the conventional Toda-Yamamoto test (1995) and the more recently developed Fourier Toda-Yamamoto test (Nazlioglu et al. 2016), which accommodates potential structural breaks within the time series. The econometric analysis demonstrated a causal nexus originating from the refugee population and impacting inflationary pressures within specific sub-indices: food, alcoholic beverages, clothing, furnishings, healthcare, education, and the hotel/restaurants. The results obtained from the standard Toda-Yamamoto test and the Fourier Toda-Yamamoto test exhibited substantial consistency, with the primary discrepancy arising in the context of housing inflation. Specifically, the Fourier Toda-Yamamoto methodology identified a statistically significant causal flow from the refugee population to housing inflation. Overall evaluation of the results reveals a convergence between the findings of the Fourier Toda-Yamamoto causality test and the standard Toda-Yamamoto causality test. This consistency suggests that the presence of structural breaks does not fundamentally alter the general equilibrium relationships identified. The findings obtained underscore the critical need for the formulation of targeted policies that explicitly consider the occupational profiles, age distribution, and educational attainment of the refugee population to effectively mitigate inflationary pressures within the identified sub-sectors.

Biofilm Inhibition of Cinnamaldehyde Against A Group of Intracanal Pathogens Compared to Chlorhexidine. An In Vitro Study

— 2026-10-17 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.72s.190

Aim: Evaluation of the antibiofilm effectiveness of cinnamaldehyde against three principal endodontic pathogens-Staphylococcus aureus, Enterococcus faecalis, and Candida albicans-and to compare its efficacy with that of chlorhexidine utilizing a microtiter plate biofilm model. Materials and Methods:After being extracted, cinnamon oil was analyzed using gas chromatographymass spectrometry (GC-MS).Broth dilution was used to calculate its minimal inhibitory concentration (MIC).Biofilm production was quantified via the crystal violet technique, with absorbance measured at 550 nm.One-way ANOVA with post hoc multiple comparisons was used for statistical analysis of the data; p ≤ 0.05 was deemed statistically significant.Results: Cinnamaldehyde produced a significant reduction in biofilm formation compared with the negative control for all tested microorganisms.Chlorhexidine also reduced biofilm formation; however, its effect against S. aureus did not reach statistical significance.No significant differences were detected between cinnamon oil and chlorhexidine in their activity against E. faecalis and S. aureus, whereas cinnamon oil showed better results than chlorhexidine against C. albicans.Conclusions: Cinnamon oil effectively inhibits biofilms of common intracanal pathogens and may serve as a natural alternative to chlorhexidine as an intracanal irrigant.To verify its effectiveness, more clinical research is required.

A COMPREHENSIVE REVIEW ON NIOSOMES IN MODERN PHARMACEUTICS AS ADVANCED VESICULAR CARRIERS: FROM MOLECULAR DESIGN TO CLINICAL POTENTIAL

Lattupally Prashanthi Reddy1, Syeda Sana Ilsa2, P. M. Sameera*3 — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23366829

Niosomes are advanced non-ionic surfactant-based vesicular nanocarriers that have gained significant attention in modern pharmaceutics due to their ability to improve drug solubility, stability, bioavailability, and therapeutic efficiency. These vesicles consist mainly of non-ionic surfactants and cholesterol, forming a bilayer structure capable of encapsulating hydrophilic, hydrophobic, and amphiphilic drugs. Their advantages, including improved chemical stability, low cost, ease of preparation, and controlled drug release, make them attractive alternatives to conventional drug delivery systems. Recent advances in molecular design, formulation optimization, and surface modification have expanded their applications in ocular, transdermal, topical, oral, anticancer, vaccine, and targeted drug delivery. Different preparation approaches allow control over vesicle size, drug loading, and release behaviour, while advanced characterization techniques ensure evaluation of formulation quality and stability. Despite promising pharmaceutical applications, challenges related to large-scale manufacturing, stability, regulatory approval, and clinical translation remain. This review highlights recent developments in niosomal technology, focusing on molecular design, formulation components, preparation methods, characterization approaches, therapeutic applications, and future perspectives in modern drug delivery.

LUMPY SKIN DISEASE: A COMPREHENSIVE REVIEW

Aryan Shahi — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23384446

Lumpy skin disease (LSD) is an economically devastating viral disease of cattle caused by Lumpy Skin Disease Virus (LSDV), a member of the genus Capripoxvirus within the family Poxviridae. Historically confined to sub-Saharan Africa, the disease has undergone dramatic geographical expansion over the past two decades, spreading through the Middle East, Eastern Europe, Central Asia, and South and Southeast Asia, culminating in catastrophic outbreaks across the Indian subcontinent since 2019. Development of a chimeric multi-epitope vaccine targeting surface-exposed proteins of Chlamydia psittaci through immunoinformatics and structural vaccinology

Ramy Sayed Yehia, Muhammad Asim, Hotaf Hassan Makki, Hibah AlMustafa — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.humimm.2026.112107

Chlamydia psittaci is an obligate intracellular zoonotic pathogen that causes psittacosis and severe respiratory illness in humans. Despite its clinical importance, no licensed vaccine is currently available, and antibiotic treatment may not provide long-term protection or prevent recurrent infection. To address this challenge, a computational strategy integrating structural vaccinology and immunoinformatics was used to design a multi-epitope vaccine targeting the major outer membrane porin and the virulence-associated pGP3-D protein of C. psittaci . Cytotoxic T-cell and helper T-cell epitopes were identified and screened to select antigenic candidates. The selected epitopes were joined using linker sequences together with β-defensin-3 and PADRE. The vaccine construct was evaluated using physicochemical analysis, three-dimensional structural modeling, interaction analysis with TLR2, a 500 ns molecular dynamics simulation, MM-GBSA binding free-energy analysis, immune simulation, and in silico cloning. The designed vaccine was predicted to possess favorable antigenicity, a non-allergenic profile, suitable physicochemical properties, and a global population coverage of 92.15%. Computational analyses suggested stable interaction with TLR2 and indicated structural stability of the vaccine–receptor complex during the simulation. Immune simulation predicted increased IgG levels, memory B-cell formation, activation of CD8 + T lymphocytes, and a cytokine profile consistent with Th1-type immune responses. These findings suggest that the proposed construct represents a computationally designed multi-epitope vaccine candidate that requires further evaluation through experimental studies to determine its safety, immunogenicity, and protective efficacy against C. psittaci .
