Disruption of the polyuracil tract can greatly enhance immunostimulatory properties of influenza delVGs

Sydney NZ Jordan, Marisa Mendes, Alistair B. Russell — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.10.13.758203

Non-standard viral genomes (nsVGs) are replication-incompetent genomes within viral populations whose presence has been associated with an enhanced immune response. This association has garnered interest in their use as potential antivirals, vaccine adjuvants, or general immune-modulators. In influenza A virus (IAV), the predominant nsVG is a large deletion in one of the three polymerase segments, which results in deletion-containing viral genomes (delVGs). Work from our group has found that individual delVGs can be recovered that are capable of stimulating high levels of interferon, however we and others have not previously found any consistent means of predicting or modifying determinants of this stimulation. Using high-throughput genetic approaches, we find that specific mutations disrupting the polyuracil tract in IAV delVGs can enhance interferon production by up to two orders of magnitude. Such variants can also associate with improved rescue in artificial systems engineered to exclude infectious virus, and can greatly suppress the growth of replication-competent virus. These findings indicate the polyuracil tract is a key, and unexpected, target for improvement of immunostimulatory delVGs derived from IAV.

Turkey haemorrhagic enteritis live vaccination controls disease and field virus circulation: molecular, clinical and pathological evidence in field longitudinal studies

Caterina Lupini, Giulia Quaglia, Valentina Benedetti, Chiara Giudice, Giulia Mescolini — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1080/03079457.2026.2718853

Haemorrhagic enteritis (HE) is a viral disease affecting turkeys. Live haemorrhagic enteritis virus (HEV)attenuated vaccines have recently been introduced in Italy. In this study, longitudinal field trials were conducted to assess the efficacy and safety of the live HE vaccine. The study was carried out on two turkey farms. Birds housed in sheds 1 and 4 were assigned to the control and vaccinated groups, respectively. Birds in shed 4 were vaccinated at 28 days with a live attenuated vaccine. From 3 weeks of age until the end of the production cycle, five birds per group were selected weekly for clinical evaluation, euthanasia, necropsy, and sampling. Clinical and post-mortem scores assessed health status and duodenal and splenic lesions, respectively. Cloacal swabs and spleen were collected for HEV detection by PCR, allowing differentiation between vaccine and field strains. Selected samples were analysed by shotgun next-generation sequencing (NGS). Spleens were histologically examined. Clinical and post-mortem scores were significantly higher in control groups at 49, 56, and 63 days of age. Molecular analyses showed persistent detection of vaccine-like strains in vaccinated groups from 35-118 days, whereas field strains were sporadically detected. NGS analysis did not reveal co-infections. In unvaccinated groups, field strains were consistently detected. Histological examination revealed a marked reduction in splenic follicles. Follicle numbers subsequently recovered in vaccinated groups. These results demonstrate that the live HE vaccine effectively protects turkeys from infection until slaughter age. Vaccination limits circulation of field strains, reduces environmental infectious pressure, and minimizes the risk of clinical disease.RESEARCH HIGHLIGHTS Live haemorrhagic enteritis virus (HEV) vaccination reduced clinical signs and lesions under field conditions.Vaccination limited field strain circulation and environmental infection pressure.Vaccinated turkey flocks remained protected from HEV until slaughter age.

N1-Methylpseudouridine-Modified mRNA-Transfected Dendritic Cells Enable Efficient Generation of Functional Tumor Antigen-Specific Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes

Tiffany Chor Kiu Ngan, Mouldy Sioud — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3390/ijms27209191

Efficient generation of tumor-specific cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) remains challenging. Although mRNA-based approaches enable the delivery and expression of diverse tumor-specific antigens, unmodified mRNA can trigger innate immune responses that suppress translation. Here, we investigated whether 1-methyl-pseudouridine (m1Ψ)-modified mRNA enhances antigen expression in human dendritic cells (DCs) and promotes the induction of antigen-specific CTLs, using survivin and MART-1 as model tumor-associated antigens. Remarkably, m1Ψ modification increased protein production in DCs by up to 20-fold compared with unmodified mRNA. DCs transfected with m1Ψ-modified mRNAs efficiently primed survivin- and MART-1-specific CD8+ T cells from autologous peripheral blood mononuclear cells of healthy donors, as demonstrated by HLA class I peptide–MHC dextramer staining. Expanded CTL lines exhibited potent effector functions, including IFN-γ production, CD107a mobilization, and robust cytotoxicity against peptide-pulsed T2 cells and tumor cell lines expressing survivin or MART-1. Translation of the engineered mRNA variants in human HeLa cells and DCs is strongly dependent on the poly(A) tail. Together, these findings demonstrate that m1Ψ modification substantially enhances antigen expression and supports the efficient generation of functional, antigen-specific CTLs, providing a promising platform for next-generation mRNA-based cancer vaccines and adoptive T-cell therapies.

Hantaviral therapeutics: life cycle targets and chemotherapeutic interventions

Deren Zehra Kocabiyik, Jansen de Araújo, Edison Luíz Durigon, Carsten Wrenger — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fcimb.2026.1972830

Orthohantaviruses represent a persistent global health threat, being responsible for life-threatening diseases such as Hemorrhagic Fever with Renal Syndrome (HFRS) and Hantavirus Cardiopulmonary Syndrome (HCPS). Despite high case fatality rates and recent outbreaks, there are currently no universally licensed antivirals or vaccines. Clinical management remains heavily reliant on supportive care and repurposed drugs, which often exhibit limited efficacy and dose-limiting toxicities. This review examines rational drug design strategies targeting critical steps in the viral life cycle, specifically Gn/Gc-mediated cellular entry and RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (RdRp)-driven genome replication. We evaluate the current landscape of viral inhibitors, including peptidomimetics, nucleoside analogues, and naturally derived compounds. To accelerate the discovery of novel treatments, we advocate for the integration of modern in silico screening to evaluate extensive chemical and natural product libraries. These broad compound collections offer a structurally diverse and largely untapped reservoir for antiviral development. Ultimately, expanding preclinical testing to include globally neglected variants will be essential to develop broad-spectrum hantaviral therapeutics capable of mitigating future epidemic threats.

Seroprevalence of Peste des Petits Ruminants Virus Antibodies and Associated Factors in Small Ruminants Sampled Around Protected Wildlife Areas in Pakistan

Abdul Kabir, Muhammad Abubakar, Asghar Ali Kamboh, Shumaila Manzoor, Muhammad Farooq — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3390/vetsci13101105

Peste des Petits Ruminants (PPR) threatens small-ruminant production in Pakistan, particularly where livestock grazing overlaps with wildlife habitats. In this exploratory cross-sectional study (January–June 2024), 1939 sera from goats (n = 1327) and sheep (n = 612) in 56 herds, purposively selected near eight protected wildlife areas, were tested for antibodies against PPR virus (PPRV) using a competitive ELISA. Vaccinated and unvaccinated animals were included, and the assay cannot distinguish vaccine-induced from infection-induced antibodies. The overall apparent antibody seroprevalence was 38.5% (747/1939; 95% CI: 36.4–40.7%) (naive 95% CI; it ignores clustering and understates uncertainty, and between-site heterogeneity was very high, I2 = 94%), ranging from 16.7% in Peshawar to 73.0% at Kirthar National Park, Sindh. Crude odds of seropositivity were higher in goats than in sheep (44.8% vs. 25.0%; OR = 2.43, 95% CI: 1.97–3.01), in animals older than three years than in those aged > 1–3 years (52.0% vs. 33.8%; OR = 2.13, 95% CI: 1.69–2.67), and in herds with more than 100 animals than in herds with 50–100 animals (43.3% vs. 35.7%; OR = 1.38, 95% CI: 1.11–1.72). After adjustment for site, goats remained more likely to be seropositive than sheep (OR = 2.19, 95% CI: 1.76–2.74), although the species difference varied between sites (heterogeneity p = 0.005). Herd-level clustering could not be modelled; associations with herd-level factors (herd size, vaccination status) were sensitive to plausible within-herd correlation and are exploratory. Vaccination categories are reported descriptively only. PPRV antibodies were common in small ruminants sampled near the selected protected areas. However, wildlife were not sampled, distance categories were confounded with site, and results are not nationally representative; transmission at the livestock–wildlife interface was not demonstrated. Targeted surveillance and integrated livestock–wildlife studies are warranted.

THE SCIENTIFIC DISCOURSE ON PHARMACOLOGY AND BIOINFORMATIC ANALYSIS IN MODERN DRUG DESIGN: FROM TARGET DISCOVERY TO TRANSLATIONAL OPTIMIZATION THROUGH INTEGRATED MOLECULAR, COMPUTATIONAL, STRUCTURAL, SYSTEMS AND PRECISION MEDICINE APPROACHES

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Nodar Sulashvili, Margarita Beglaryan, Nana Gorgaslidze, Naira Chichoyan, Vira Kravchenko — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.52340/spectri.2026.13.01.06

Drug discovery has evolved from a largely empirical discipline into a knowledge-intensive enterprise that depends on the integration of pharmacology, structural biology, computational chemistry, and bioinformatics. Modern pharmacotherapeutic design is no longer satisfied by the demonstration that a molecule binds a macromolecular target. Instead, a candidate must be considered within a broader context that includes disease mechanism, network position of the target, tissue expression, structural tractability, off-target liabilities, pharmacokinetic behavior, and the probability of translation from preclinical systems to humans. Bioinformatic analysis has become central to this process because it enables the organization and interpretation of genomic, transcriptomic, proteomic, metabolomic, phenotypic, and chemical data at a scale that cannot be managed by traditional reductionist workflows alone. Pharmacology remains equally indispensable because the relevance of computational outputs depends on mechanistic interpretation, exposure-response understanding, and a realistic view of efficacy and safety in complex biological systems. This review examines how pharmacology and bioinformatic analysis jointly inform contemporary drug design across the discovery pipeline. It discusses target identification and validation, structural bioinformatics, structure-based and ligand-based design, network pharmacology, multi-omics analysis, ADMET prediction, and pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic modeling. It also addresses the increasing role of machine learning, not as a replacement for experimental science, but as a tool for prioritization, pattern recognition, and iterative decision support. Particular emphasis is placed on the limitations of current approaches, including database incompleteness, bias in training sets, uncertain biological annotation, and the common error of overinterpreting docking scores or predictive probabilities as proof of therapeutic success. The central argument is that the most reliable drug design strategies emerge when computational breadth is combined with pharmacological depth. In that integrative framework, bioinformatics helps define what should be tested, while pharmacology determines what the resulting signals actually mean for drug action, safety, and translation.

Community barriers, perceptions, and health-seeking behaviors within the one health approach in six priority districts of Rwanda: a descriptive qualitative study

Marie-Michèle Umulisa, Leandre Ishema, Edson Rwagasore, Patrick Kayinamura Muhirwa, Odree Mugwaneza — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12889-026-29896-4

Rwanda’s health security can only be strengthened through integrated One Health approaches that link human, animal, and environmental health. Emerging health threats such as the Marburg Virus highlight the urgency, yet fragmented systems, weak coordination, and behavioral barriers continue to constrain resilience. Thus, we assessed behavioral, social, and systemic determinants influencing community resilience, outbreak preparedness, and response, using the Health Belief Model (HBM) and the Social Ecological Model (SEM) to generate actionable evidence for strengthening One Health integration, health system performance, and risk communication strategies for effective disease prevention and outbreak mitigation in Rwanda. Our descriptive qualitative assessment included participants from Rulindo, Kayonza, Nyanza, Rusizi, Rubavu, and Nyaruguru districts of Rwanda. We had six In-depth Interviews (IDIs) per district and two Focus Group Discussions (FGDs) per district. Participants were selected from different socioeconomic class, occupation selected rotated across districts, ensuring inclusivity, while data collection was carried out in October 2025. We conducted weekly debriefing meetings, which enabled us to familiarize ourselves with the data. Using Dedoose for data coding, results were presented according to themes, while indicating areas of convergence. We received ethical approval for this study from the Rwanda National Ethics Committee (IRB00001497 of IORG0001100). A total of 143 participants took part in the FGDs; the majority were male ( n = 82, 57.34%) and were aged between 31 and 40. Most participants were working as the animal health personnel, n = 19 (13.29%). Of the 36 participants in the IDIs, almost half ( n = 15, 41.7%) were aged 31–40 years. The most commonly reported occupation was being health personnel, n = 12 (33.32%). Several themes emerged; however, using the joint display method of comparing findings from IDIs and FGDs, we identified data convergent points to include factors influencing healthcare-seeking behavior, economic constraints and the deprioritization of health interventions, perceived importance of One Health and weak intersectoral collaboration, community health information dissemination through multiple communication channels and myths and misinformation leads to vaccine rejection, as well as maintaining proper handwashing is a challenge. Findings from this assessment provide a critical foundation for identifying behavioral and systemic gaps and facilitators towards the One Health approach. Bottleneck challenges on resources and limited engagement of community members continue to promote health insecurity. Therefore, district-level coordination and accountability, as well as training of community health workers to listen and engage community members, will enhance the One Health sector of Rwanda.

Immunogenicity and safety of trivalent inactivated influenza vaccine in adults living with HIV compared with HIV-uninfected adults: a study from China

Jinfeng Su, Kai Gao, Shasha Han, Xuewen Tang, Xue Guo — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2026.1945123

Objective To evaluate the immunogenicity and safety of trivalent inactivated influenza vaccine (IIV3) in adults aged ≥18 years living with HIV and to provide evidence supporting influenza vaccination in this population in China. Methods This single-center, open-label comparative analysis was derived from a larger three-arm clinical study. For the present comparative analysis, 472 vaccinated participants were planned, including 236 adults living with HIV (Group 1) and 236 age- and sex-comparable HIV-uninfected adults (Group 2). Participants in both groups received one dose of IIV3 (0.5 mL) on Day 0. Blood samples were collected before and 28 days after vaccination for hemagglutination inhibition (HI) antibody testing against A(H1N1), A(H3N2), and B/Victoria strains. Immunogenicity endpoints included seroconversion rate, seroprotection rate, geometric mean titer (GMT), and geometric mean increase (GMI). Safety was assessed by monitoring solicited and unsolicited adverse events (AEs) and serious adverse events (SAEs) during 6 months of follow-up. An unvaccinated cohort of adults living with HIV from the original trial was included only for an exploratory, descriptive analysis of influenza-like illness (ILI), without assessment of vaccine efficacy. Results A total of 489 participants were enrolled, including 237 in Group 1 and 252 in Group 2. At 28 days post-vaccination, seroconversion rates did not differ significantly between Group 1 and Group 2 for A(H1N1) (91.00% vs. 86.69%, P > 0.05), but significantly lower for A(H3N2) (49.50% vs. 89.52%) and B/Victoria (40.00% vs. 64.52%) (both P < 0.001). Seroprotection rates were also lower in Group 1 than in Group 2 for A(H1N1), A(H3N2), and B/Victoria (94.00% vs. 97.98%, 63.50% vs. 97.18%, and 40.50% vs. 68.55%, respectively). Post-vaccination GMTs were significantly lower in Group 1 for all three strains (all P < 0.001). GMI was higher for A(H1N1) but lower for A(H3N2) and B/Victoria in Group 1 than in Group 2 (all P < 0.001). Multivariable analyses showed that HIV infection remained independently associated with several measures of vaccine-induced immune responses after adjustment for demographic and clinical factors, particularly for A(H3N2). IIV3 was generally safe and well tolerated. Most AEs were mild in severity, and no Grade 4 adverse reactions or vaccine-related SAEs were reported. During the period from 29 days to 6 months after vaccination, few influenza-like illness events were reported among vaccinated and unvaccinated participants living with HIV; these findings were descriptive and were not used to assess vaccine efficacy against influenza. Conclusion Adults living with HIV developed substantial antibody responses following IIV3 vaccination, although the magnitude of response varied across influenza strains. A(H1N1) seroconversion was high in adults living with HIV, whereas responses to A(H3N2) and B/Victoria were generally lower than those in HIV-uninfected adults. HIV infection remained associated with reduced vaccine-induced immune responses for several strain-specific outcomes after multivariable adjustment, with associations particularly evident for A(H3N2). IIV3 was safe and well tolerated, supporting current recommendations for influenza vaccination in adults living with HIV. Mesoscopic learning dynamics of extensive-width neural networks

Shu Zhou, Jean Barbier, Sebastian Goldt — 2026-10-16 — arxiv

DOI : —

Understanding how stochastic gradient descent learns internal representations in wide neural networks trained on rich target functions is a central problem in machine learning. We study this question in two-layer neural networks learning target functions represented by networks of the same architecture, with arbitrary nonlinearity, and with network width and input dimension growing proportionally. In this regime, standard approaches for describing the learning dynamics require tracking a diverging number of overlaps, which measure correlations between learned and target features, leaving no obvious description in terms of a few order parameters. Here, we develop a mesoscopic description of learning by characterizing the distribution of overlaps. In the early stages of learning, we find that a Gaussian description of overlap statistics reduces the learning dynamics to a few collective variables that track learning of the target function's linear and quadratic components. We then estimate the timescale of specialization, when Gaussian closure breaks down and individual learned features begin to strongly correlate with specific target features. We find that specialization in extensive-rank networks is accelerated via a quadratic shielding mechanism, whereby learning the linear and quadratic components of the target function filters gradient fluctuations. We finally describe how the activation function determines the pattern of specialization and characterize the subsequent refinement of learned features. Together, these results show how collective learning gives way to individual feature discovery and clarify when learning in large neural networks admits a simple statistical description.

On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management

Marco Boresta, Tommaso Giovannelli, Sepehr Namirad, Giulia Riccardi — 2026-10-16 — arxiv

DOI : —

Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.

Causal Discovery beyond Conditional Independence: Identifiability, Algorithms, and Statistical Inference for Directed Acyclic and Cyclic Graphs

Yang Ni — 2026-10-16 — arxiv

DOI : —

Learning causal structure from data requires assumptions about mechanisms, noises, observation schemes, and interventions. Historically, causal discovery has relied primarily on conditional independence. This review focuses on modern causal discovery methods that exploit properties of distributions beyond conditional independence. Our organizing principle separates the source of identification from the statistical estimator and its computational implementation. This perspective connects conditional-independence methods with distributional asymmetries, conditional-moment restrictions, and variation across environments or individuals. We examine discovery under latent confounding and feedback, distinguishing causal information about observed variables from reconstruction of latent structure and emphasizing the additional semantics required by cyclic models. We also distinguish population identification, frequentist and Bayesian consistency, uncertainty quantification, and algorithmic guarantees. Throughout, examples show why the same observational distribution can support different conclusions under different model classes. We conclude with guidance for comparing methods at their identifiable targets and a research agenda for uncertainty, heterogeneous populations, non-scalar variables, and the intersection of discrete data, feedback, and hidden confounding.

Optimal subsampling approach for Cox models under large-scale length-biased right-censored data

Zhicheng Feng, Panpan Ba, Jianhua Shi, Lin Ouyang — 2026-10-16 — arxiv

DOI : —

With the rapid advancement of data acquisition technologies, massive survival data have become increasingly prevalent, posing substantial computational challenges for the standard Cox proportional hazards model under complex censoring and truncation mechanisms. This paper addresses the analysis of large-scale length-biased right-censored data within the Cox model framework. We propose a novel subsampling approach to enhance computational efficiency for large samples and establish theoretically that the resulting subsample estimator provides an efficient approximation to the full-data estimator, while preserving consistency and asymptotic normality. Explicit optimal subsampling probabilities are derived under A- and L-optimality criteria. To facilitate practical implementation, we develop an efficient two-step estimation algorithm. Extensive numerical simulations and an empirical analysis of U.S. airline flight operation data evaluate the estimation performance and computational gains under various censoring rates and sample sizes. The results demonstrate that the proposed method substantially reduces computational cost while preserving statistical accuracy, offering a practical and scalable solution for analyzing large-scale length-biased right-censored survival data.

Exact Tail Laws for Nonlinear SGD with Infinite-Variance Gradients

Xiaoli Li, Qianqian Lei, Ziyang Wei, Wei Biao Wu — 2026-10-16 — arxiv

DOI : —

With infinite-variance gradient noise, SGD can have infinite expected excess objective even when most iterates are close to the optimum. We characterize its accuracy through exact first-order tail probabilities for the last iterate of nonlinear, constant-stepsize SGD. For regularly varying innovations of index $t\in(1,2)$, the leading measure transports one large gradient shock through the local Hessian flow. This is the same measure that governs the central stable Ornstein--Uhlenbeck limit; we prove its relative accuracy at shrinking tolerances above the central scale, with state-dependent noise and a growing observation horizon. The admissible window is nonempty for every available gradient moment $p>1$. The main technical step is a moment bound at the central fluctuation scale and a lower-moment control of the nonlinear error. The result yields explicit projection and excess-objective tails, extreme quantiles, and a factor $b^{1-t}$ for fixed batch size $b$. For affine population gradients, a sharper comparison gives the same law at fixed tolerances, including random sample Hessians. Coupled simulations in dimensions one to one hundred examine relative calibration, objective quantiles, batch effects, and the error due to random curvature.

Non-asymptotic oracle inequalities for the Lasso in high-dimensional mixture of experts

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

TrungTin Nguyen, Hien D. Nguyen, Faïcel Chamroukhi, Geoffrey John McLachlan — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s10463-026-01007-5

Abstract We study Lasso estimation for high-dimensional softmax-gated Gaussian mixture-of-experts (SGMoE) regression models, where the predictor dimension P may exceed the sample size N . Under bounded parameters, we establish a non-asymptotic Kullback–Leibler (KL) oracle inequality with an explicit sufficient regularization level and explicit dependence on N , P , response dimension Q , number of experts K , and boundedness constants. We derive sparse well-specified and approximation-space rates, showing how deterministic SGMoE approximation error transfers into statistical KL-risk bounds. The theory extends to random covariates and, under misspecification, controls the best Gaussian SGMoE approximation in the candidate class. Reproducible experiments validate KL decay and density recovery, compare Lasso with SCAD when $$P>N$$ P > N , evaluate model-selection criteria, handle multivariate covariance structures, and assess high-dimensional biological benchmarks relevant to heterogeneous genotype–phenotype mapping, omics-to-trait prediction, and AI-enabled virtual-cell modelling in systems biology.

Saddlepoint approximations for plug-in resampling

Ziang Niu, Zhihan Huang, Jyotishka Ray Choudhury, Eugene Katsevich — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/asag063

Abstract Resampling-based procedures can improve on normal approximations in sparse, large-scale testing problems, but their computational cost can be prohibitive. We recognize that several existing procedures belong to a faster plug-in resampling subclass, fixing fitted nuisance parameters during resampling. When the resampled statistic is a sum of conditionally independent terms, the saddlepoint approximation for the resampling p -value offers further acceleration, replacing resampling with an analytical tail approximation. However, standard Edgeworth-based approximation-error bounds impose regularity conditions that are hard to verify for plug-in resampling laws. We use an alternative approach to establish a finite-sample relative-error bound for the Lugannani–Rice approximation under more tractable conditions, which we apply in two contexts. In statistical genetics, we identify response resampling procedures as the targets of existing saddlepoint approximations and establish guarantees in a representative setting. In conditional independence testing, we introduce a saddlepoint approximation for the distilled conditional randomization test, which has been applied successfully in biology. Our rates quantify the effects of sparsity and signal strength, with matching lower bounds in special cases. We additionally establish asymptotic Type-I error control of the corresponding plug-in resampling procedures under growing sparsity. In simulations and single-cell CRISPR data analysis, our new test closely approximates distilled conditional randomization test p -values and preserves its statistical performance while accelerating computation by up to 250-fold.

RNA sequencing significantly improves HYDIN diagnosis and defines the effects of common deletions in primary ciliary dyskinesia

Carolina Jaramillo Oquendo, Janice Coles, Lee Baker, David J. Bunyan, N. Simon Thomas — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41431-026-02243-2

Primary Ciliary Dyskinesia (PCD) is a rare genetic disorder affecting ~1:7500 individuals characterised by recurrent chronic respiratory infections, bronchiectasis, laterality defects, and infertility. While at least 60 PCD-causing genes are reported, approximately 30% of patients remain without a confirmed molecular diagnosis. PCD caused by biallelic HYDIN variants is difficult to diagnose due to a 98% homologous pseudogene (HYDIN2). Additionally, functional ciliary diagnostic testing can be inconclusive due to the lack of discernible HYDIN ciliary ultrastructure defects by electron microscopy. Our aim was to diagnose genetically unresolved PCD patients with strong clinical suspicion of HYDIN defects using RNA sequencing (RNA-seq). RNA-seq was performed on total RNA extracted from patients' nasal epithelium cultured for 21 days at the air-liquid interface. Splicing abnormalities were detected using rMATS and genome-aligned reads were manually inspected using Integrative Genome Viewer. Thirteen individuals had strong clinical suspicion of PCD, normal TEM and inconclusive genetics: 11/13 had a single pathogenic or likely pathogenic variant in HYDIN, 1/13 had no variants, and 1/13 required clarification of variant origin (HYDIN vs HYDIN2). RNA-seq resolved 11/13 (85%) of cases. The same complex pathogenic aberrant splicing events were seen in seven unrelated patients. This abnormal splice pattern was caused by a large 10-kilobase deletion spanning intron 17 to intron 18. RNA-seq increases the diagnostic yield for patients with suspected PCD and has exposed a HYDIN 'hotspot' variant, increasing the observed prevalence of HYDIN-related cases within our genetically diagnosed cohort. Routine PCD genomics testing will now incorporate investigation of this genetic region of HYDIN.

Claudio Cucini — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.33416206

This repository hosts a modified version of the genome assembly of Bilobella aurantiaca (Zhang, F. and Ding, Y., unpublished), originally available in the NCBI Genome database under accession number GCA_021029175.1.The genome assembly was manually modified at contigs VYYI01002198.1 and VYYI01000826.1 to correct a putative frameshift (identified at position 12,535 of the original assembly) and some repetitive sequences. This was carried out through Sanger sequencing of the two different loci. Individually revised sequences were submitted to GenBank.The modified genome assembly was subsequently used for genome annotation in combination with publicly available RNA-seq data deposited in the NCBI Sequence Read Archive (SRA) under accession number SRR1653175.In addition to the modified genome assembly, this repository provides the resulting structural and functional annotations. The structural annotation is provided in GFF3 format (braker.gff3) and was generated using BRAKER. NEW INSIGHTS INTO PAUSING AND TERMINATION BY T7 RNA POLYMERASE

Mohammad Amin Abek Azerbaijani — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.7275/58209

T7 RNA polymerase is the most widely used enzyme for RNA synthesis in both research and industrial settings. Despite its widespread use, most end users disregard the enzyme’s intrinsic regulatory features during transcription. Although T7 RNA polymerase possesses fewer regulatory elements than those of higher organisms, the limited regulatory mechanisms it does possess have been significantly overlooked. In this work, we investigate the mechanism of the pause-and-termination sequence known as the class II signal. The means by which the conserved DNA sequence ATCTGTT exerts its regulatory effect has remained unresolved for decades. We test the hypothesis that termination at this site occurs via hyper-forward translocation. Our results indicate that forward translocation is unlikely to make a significant contribution to termination at the class II signal. Instead, using computational modeling and building on previous work, we propose a model of signal recognition in which the polymerase binds the conserved sequence on the nontemplate strand of DNA, promoting formation of a noncanonical DNA structure. Termination (release of RNA) could occur, perhaps due to structural stress induced by RNA growth within a static (at least upstream) structure. RNA-seq characterization of transcription products generated from templates containing the class II sequence revealed a surprising and unprecedented phenomenon: a family of short RNAs corresponding to sequences downstream of the consensus element. Mass spectrometry analysis demonstrated that these RNAs carry 5’ triphosphates, indicating that they arise from a promoter-independent de novo initiation mechanism. These findings imply that, after release of the RNA, T7 RNA polymerase remains bound to the DNA and competent to reinitiate RNA synthesis — a mode of initiation not previously observed in any RNA polymerase. Finally, this work addresses a practical challenge in large-scale mRNA synthesis: the generation of undesired truncated RNA products. We show that many such fragments arise not from terminator sequences, but from collision-induced bumping events during transcription of long templates. Reducing the polymerase-to-DNA ratio decreases truncation, and use of a co-tethered transcription system that enforces strict 1:1 DNA–polymerase stoichiometry nearly eliminates these artifacts, substantially improving long RNA yield and functional performance.

BIRC3 facilitates osteogenic differentiation of bone marrow mesenchymal stem cells via NGFR/β-catenin signalling axis

Jun Tang, Ming Li, Yuanquan Chen, Yuwei Liang, Wenbin Yan — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1302/2046-3758.1510.bjr-2025-0372.r2

Aims: Osteoporosis primarily results from age-related declines in bone marrow mesenchymal stem cell (BMSC) differentiation into osteoblasts; however, the regulatory mechanisms remain unclear. Methods: Integrated analysis of Gene Expression Omnibus (GEO) and RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) data identified a key osteogenic regulator. Western blot, reverse transcription quantitative polymerase chain reaction (RT-qPCR), RNA-seq, immunohistochemistry (IHC), alkaline phosphatase (ALP), and alizarin red S (ARS) staining were employed to elucidate the functional and mechanistic aspects of modulating osteogenic differentiation. A C57 mouse tibial drill-hole model was used to evaluate therapeutic potential. Results: RNA-seq identified baculoviral inhibitor of apoptosis (IAP) repeat-containing protein 3 (BIRC3) as a potential regulator of BMSC osteogenesis, showing significant upregulation during BMSC osteogenic differentiation and upon monocyte stimulation. BIRC3 positively correlated with ALP and ARS staining intensity and significantly enhanced the expression of (runt-related transcription factor 2) RUNX2 and ALP proteins. RNA-seq, RT-qPCR, and western blot data indicated that BIRC3 positively regulates the expression of nerve growth factor receptor (NGFR). NGFR also promoted the osteogenic differentiation of BMSCs. Further experiments demonstrated that BIRC3 regulates BMSC osteogenesis through NGFR. Through XAV939 treatment, we demonstrated that both BIRC3 and NGFR promoted BMSC osteogenesis by enhancing β-catenin expression and nuclear translocation, thereby defining the functional role of the BIRC3/NGFR/β-catenin axis in BMSCs. In C57 mouse tibial bone defect models, BIRC3 overexpression group exhibited significantly enhanced callus formation and ossification compared to the control group. Conclusion: BIRC3 regulates BMSC osteogenic differentiation via NGFR/β-catenin signalling in vitro and promotes bone healing in vivo, suggesting therapeutic potential for osteoporosis and bone defects.

A Computational Pipeline for Patient-Level Gene-Expression Analysis in Colorectal Cancer

Mohammad Yamaan Iqbal — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23406274

Group-level differential expression does not show whether gene-expression changes are consistent across individual patients. I developed a Python-based computational pipeline to evaluate patient-level consistency using a publicly available colorectal cancer RNA-sequencing dataset containing 20 matched tumor-normal pairs. Paired differential expression analysis identified 8,300 of 14,911 genes as significant at FDR < 0.05. For each gene, I calculated a direction-agnostic patient-consistency score based on the proportion of non-tied patients showing the same direction of change and tested this against chance using an exact binomial test. Consistency varied independently of fold-change magnitude: 1,927 genes showed modest average changes but high patient-level consistency, whereas 567 showed large average changes with lower consistency. For 1,261 genes (8.5%), the majority patient direction differed from the group-level average. Results were reproduced in a held-out 14-patient cohort, with strong fold-change correlation (r = 0.858) and 95.7% directional replication among high-consistency genes. Hierarchical clustering and pathway-enrichment analyses further supported the distinction between consistent and heterogeneous expression patterns. The pipeline demonstrates a reproducible computational approach for analyzing patient-level variability in matched gene-expression data. Keywords computational pipeline; colorectal cancer; RNA sequencing; differential gene expression; patient-level consistency; tumor-normal pairs; cross-cohort validation; gene set enrichment; Python; bioinformatics

Damascus goat mammary gland transcriptome under mixed and artificial rearing systems: 3’ mRNA-seq gene count matrix, sample metadata, and differential expression results

Eleni Sfakianaki, Despoina Miltiadou, Constantina Constantinou, Simoni Symeou, Georgios C. Stefos — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.17632/s6nd6hhjt6

This dataset contains processed 3’ mRNA sequencing data generated from mammary gland epithelial biopsies of second-lactation Damascus goats under two rearing systems: artificial rearing (AR) and mixed rearing (MR). Kids in the AR group were weaned 48h post partum while in the MR group were allowed to suckle on their mother for seven weeks. In both groups does were machine milked twice daily. Mammary gland tissue biopsies were collected at 42 days postpartum (n = 6 per treatment group). Libraries were prepared from total RNA using the QuantSeq 3′ mRNA-Seq Library Prep Kit (Lexogen) and sequenced on an Illumina MiSeq platform (150-cycle kit). Raw reads were quality-checked using FastQC (v0.12.1) and processed with Cutadapt (v4.4) to remove adapters, poly(A) tails, and low-quality bases. Reads were aligned to the Capra hircus reference genome assembly ARS1.2 using STAR aligner (v2.7.10b). Gene-level counts were generated using the quantMode option in STAR. Because libraries were sequenced across multiple runs, batch effects were corrected using ComBat-seq. Differential expression analysis was performed using DESeq2 (v1.40.2). Genes with adjusted P-value < 0.05 were considered differentially expressed; adjusted P-value < 0.1 indicated a trend for differential expression. The Excel file includes five sheets: -Supplemental Table 1: Transcriptomic analysis pipeline output in number of reads per library. -Supplemental Table 2: Gene Ontology Terms' attributes of the differentially expressed genes for GO domains Molecular Function, Biological process and Cellular Component. -gene_count_table: raw gene counts per library, prior to differential expression analysis. -sample_metadata: library identifiers, treatment group (AR or MR) and sequencing batch. -Full_DE_results: complete DESeq2 output including baseMean, log2FoldChange (lfc), lfc Standard Error (lfcSE), Wald statistic (stat), P-value, and adjusted P-value. These data support the findings reported in Sfakianaki et al., Journal of Dairy Science, 2026, Kid suckling versus artificial rearing: Effects in Damascus goat milk production parameters and the mammary gland transcriptome. https://doi.org/10.3168/jds.2026-28481

Insufficient BMP8B–SULF2 signaling may contribute to preeclampsia by impairing extravillous trophoblast function

Yue Liang, Zirui Peng, Zhuoqun Ren, Kejie Wu, Yan Li — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s13062-026-01013-w

Extravillous trophoblast (EVT) invasion is essential for placental development, and their dysregulation is a hallmark pathological feature of preeclampsia (PE). However, the underlying molecular mechanisms remain incompletely understood, limiting the development of clinically reliable biomarkers and therapeutic targets. Although bone morphogenetic protein 8B (BMP8B) has been reported to be highly expressed in placental trophoblasts, its potential role in early pregnancy placental development—particularly in EVT invasion—and in PE pathogenesis remains to be defined. Using RNA-seq analysis, RT–qPCR, Western blot, and immunostaining, we characterized the cell type-specific localization of BMP8B at the maternal–fetal interface and assessed its expression levels in preeclamptic placentas. Functional studies were performed using the immortalized EVT cell line HTR8/SVneo, primary human EVTs, and first-trimester villous explants to evaluate the role of BMP8B and its downstream target SULF2 in regulating EVT function. BMP8B is broadly expressed across multiple trophoblast subtypes, with its expression levels significantly downregulated in preeclamptic placentas. Recombinant BMP8B activates the PI3K-AKT pathway through ALK4/ACVR2B receptors and upregulates sulfatase 2 (SULF2) expression, thereby promoting EVT migration, invasion, and tube formation. Knockdown of endogenous BMP8B suppresses these EVT functional phenotypes, further supporting the pro-EVT functional role of BMP8B. Our findings establish the BMP8B–SULF2 signaling axis as a pivotal regulatory pathway governing EVT function. Disruption of BMP8B–SULF2 signaling in placental tissues may contribute to defective placentation and PE pathogenesis. Targeting BMP8B–SULF2 signaling axis may therefore offer new clinical avenues for early prediction and targeted intervention in PE.

Meta-Analysis of ALS Post-Mortem Cerebral Cortex Transcriptome Studies Identifies Robust Shifts in Gene Expression Despite Significant Between-Study Heterogeneity

William R. Swindell, Aminah Tasnim Ali, Alfredo Iacoangeli, Salman F Bhai — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3390/brainsci16101120

Background: ALS is characterized by motor neuron failure with potential neurocognitive comorbidity. Cortical neurons, and their support cells, exhibit pathologic features, some of which have been identified in post-mortem transcriptome studies of the cortex from ALS patients and control subjects. These studies, however, have utilized varying technologies with few attempts to reconcile findings. Methods: This study used meta-analysis to evaluate differential gene expression (ALS vs. control) from 16 prior transcriptome studies utilizing multiple technologies (microarray, bulk RNA-seq or snRNA-seq). Normalization with GC content correction was used to mitigate downstream biases impacting differential expression. Results: Approximately one-third of protein-coding genes (5930 of 16,579) exhibited significant between-study effect size heterogeneity (FDR < 0.05), which was frequently associated with variation in average post-mortem interval. Nonetheless, we identified 269 and 273 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) with relatively consistent increased or decreased expression in the ALS cortex, respectively (FDR < 0.05 with SMD ≥ 0.20 or SMD ≤ −0.20). ALS-increased DEGs were associated with transcription (RNA polymerase), cell signaling (TGF-beta, PI3K), interneurons, microglia, endothelial cells, and deep laminar layers (L5, L6). ALS-decreased DEGs were associated with transcription (RNA polymerase), glial cells (astrocytes, oligodendrocytes) and the L1 layer. There was significant overlap between DEGs from the ALS cortex and those previously identified from ALS spinal cord and blood. Conclusions: These findings highlight signaling pathways, cell types and cortical layers associated with robust changes in ALS cortex gene expression. Our results illustrate the importance of meta-analysis as a tool for understanding between-study heterogeneity and identifying repeatable findings across technologies.

6PPD-quinone exposure impairs erectile function through dysregulation of the HIF-1α/eNOS/NO axis: multi-omics and in vivo evidence

Chunyang Meng, Yuqi Li, Xinyao Zhu, Yifan Li, Yuelin Du — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fendo.2026.1912092

Background 6PPD-quinone (6PPD-Q), a transformation product of the tire antioxidant 6PPD, has emerged as an environmental contaminant with potential reproductive toxicity. However, its role in erectile dysfunction (ED) and the underlying mechanisms remain unclear. Methods This study integrated network toxicology, transcriptomics, single-cell RNA sequencing, machine learning, Mendelian randomization, molecular docking, molecular dynamics simulation, and animal experiments to investigate the potential mechanisms linking 6PPD-Q exposure to ED. Results A total of 134 genes were identified at the intersection of the 6PPD-Q- and ED-related gene sets. Integration of bulk and single-cell transcriptomic data, followed by LASSO and SVM-RFE analyses, prioritized ACO1, HIF1A, PDK4, and PTPN11. Among these genes, only HIF1A showed a significant association with ED risk in the Mendelian randomization analysis. Molecular docking and a 100-ns molecular dynamics simulation supported the structural plausibility of an interaction between 6PPD-Q and HIF-1α. In rats, 6PPD-Q exposure reduced the ICPmax/MAP ratio and cavernosal NO levels without affecting testosterone. These changes were accompanied by increased HIF-1α and decreased p-eNOS, eNOS, and p-eNOS/eNOS levels in the corpus cavernosum. PX-478 partially restored eNOS/NO signaling and erectile function. Conclusion These findings suggest that 6PPD-Q may contribute to ED by activating HIF1A-related stress responses and impairing eNOS/NO-mediated endothelial relaxation.

Computational deconvolution of drug sensitivity via single-cell–to–bulk transcriptome mapping

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Alexandre Coudray, Romain Forey, Benjamı́n Béjar, Filipe Martins, Joana Carlevaro-Fita — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pcbi.1014862

Analyses routinely conducted in both fundamental research and biomedical studies often report aggregate signals derived from heterogeneous mixtures of cells. These mixtures reflect the intrinsic anatomical and functional complexity of the tissues and organs from which they are derived. Such bulk measurements obscure cell-specific signals, thereby hindering accurate interpretation of the underlying biological processes. Here, we introduce CLIMB and CLIFF, a two-step machine-learning framework that deconvolves bulk RNA-seq into cell-type composition and expression, and then resolves bulk drug sensitivity measurements into cell subtype- and single-cell-specific profiles. We first validate CLIMB using in-vitro mixtures of leukemic cell lines with experimentally known cell-type proportions, then extend this validation to large-scale, cross-dataset pseudo-bulk analyses built from independent scRNA-seq references across two disease contexts and five cancer types - mimicking the reference-to-target mismatches encountered in real applications - and show that CLIMB accurately infers the cellular composition of bulk samples, outperforming state-of-the-art bulk deconvolution methods. We emphasize CLIMB's effectiveness in acute myeloid leukemia, where it offers highly accurate estimation of leukemic stem cell fractions across three test datasets and three patient cohorts. We then validate CLIFF, our algorithm for estimating cell subtype- and single-cell-specific drug sensitivity, first on the same in-vitro cell line mixtures using experimentally measured drug responses, and then through in-depth, realistic-scale pseudo-bulk simulations. We applied our deconvolution strategy to the beatAML cohort dataset, currently the most extensive database of ex-vivo drug screening data. This analysis generated a cell subtype-resolved drug sensitivity landscape for 121 compounds, revealing multiple compounds putatively active against leukemic stem cells, including midostaurin, A-674563, SNS-032, and foretinib. Collectively, our work introduces a general computational framework for resolving bulk functional measurements at cell-type and single-cell resolution, providing an attractive new computational tool for drug development and precision medicine.

MCT4-Associated Lactate Export Defines Myeloid Metabolic Reprogramming during Head and Neck Cancer Progression

Thomas Tilton, Young Min Park, Haiqi Zhu, Chi Zhang, De‐Chen Lin — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.10.13.758108

Head and neck squamous cell carcinoma (HNSCC) develops within a metabolically remodeled tumor microenvironment, yet how metabolic programs evolve across specific cell populations during tumor progression remains poorly understood. We integrated single-cell RNA-sequencing data from five HNSCC cohorts comprising 645,351 cells from 114 patients and inferred metabolic flux using single-cell Flux Estimation Analysis (scFEA). Myeloid cells showed the highest and most consistent metabolic allocation to lactate export among major cell types. In ten patients with paired normal mucosa and carcinoma, myeloid lactate-export allocation increased from 1.72% to 3.31% of total inferred metabolic flux, with an increase in every patient. SPP1+ macrophages showed the highest lactate-export allocation in carcinoma. Lactate export was predominantly associated with MCT4/SLC16A3. In carcinoma, myeloid SLC16A3 expression exceeded SLC16A1 by 17-fold, and CPTAC-HNSCC confirmed tumor enrichment of MCT4 protein. Together, these findings identify increased MCT4-associated lactate export as a prominent feature of myeloid metabolic reprogramming during HNSCC progression.

Nonhomogeneous elastic turbulence in the two-dimensional Taylor-Couette flow

Zhongxuan Hou, Stefano Berti, Teodor Burghelea, Francesco Romanò — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1103/p4m5-4k5l

Elastic turbulence is a spatially and temporally disordered flow state appearing in viscoelastic fluids at vanishing fluid inertia and large elasticity. The resulting flows have broad technological interest, particularly to enhance mixing and heat transfer in microdevices. Although its experimental characterization is now well established in different setups, its theoretical understanding and numerical reproducibility remain challenging, especially in wall-bounded geometries. By means of numerical simulations, we investigate the onset of elastic turbulence and the characteristics of the developed turbulent-like states in the two-dimensional, confined, Taylor-Couette system. First, we characterize the purely elastic instability, addressing previously contrasting evidences. We then show that the fully nonlinear dynamics are weakly anisotropic and strongly nonhomogeneous. Indeed, they are confined in a dynamically active region adjacent to the inner wall, akin to the elastic boundary layer from previous predictions. Within this region,in spite of some non-negligible deviations due to the nonhomeogeneity of our setup, the statistical and spectral turbulent properties are to reasonable extent not far from the theoretical expectations and experimental observations.

Exploring calibration-free luminescence thermometry with Mn 4+ anti-Stokes/Stokes emission

Guitong Zhang, JiaPeng Wu, Yixuan Ma, L. Cui, Zhijie Dong — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aeh4432

Mn 4+ -activated phosphors offer a unique possibility of calibration-free primary luminescence thermometry using the ratio of anti-Stokes to Stokes (AS/S) vibronic emission lines of the 2 E → 4 A 2 transition. Previous work used the AS/S emission of Mn 4+ for thermometry but always relying on fitting procedures with effective vibrational energies that deviate from actual energies. Here, we explore a strategy without prior temperature calibration using two well-known Mn 4+ -doped hosts, Mg 14 Ge 5 O 24 (MGO) and K 2 SiF 6 (KSF). In both Mn 4+ occupies octahedral sites, replacing Ge 4+ or Si 4+ , resulting in a weak zero-phonon line (ZPL) and strong vibronic Stokes and anti-Stokes lines. From the temperature-dependent emission spectra the AS/S ratios ν 6 ′/ν 6 , ν 4 ′/ν 4 , ν 3 ′/ν 3 were determined to serve as input for luminescence intensity ratio (LIR) analysis. For the well-resolved vibronic lines of Mn 4+ in KSF it is feasible to realize temperature readout without temperature calibration – a capability rarely achieved in luminescence thermometry. In contrast, for Mn 4+ in MGO vibronic lines exhibit significant overlap, complicating single-phonon-mode modeling. Even spectral deconvolution methods do not allow for reliable calibration-free temperature sensing. This work establishes a generalizable approach to optical thermometry relying on anti-Stokes/Stokes vibronic lines, offering a path towards calibration-free temperature sensing.

Ligand Protonation Tunes Hydride-Transfer Thermochemistry in an Iron Metal-Ligand Cooperative Catalyst

Sergio Fernández — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.26434/chemrxiv.15010588/v1

Cyclopentadienone iron complexes are established metal-ligand cooperative hydrogenation catalysts, yet their bifunctional reactivity complicates the independent evaluation of proton- and hydride-transfer thermodynamics. Here, room-temperature H 2 -splitting enables deconvolution of these contributions, affording a new anionic hydride with a hydricity of 39.0 kcal·mol -1 . Ligand protonation weakens hydride-donor strength by 10 kcal mol -1 , highlighting protonation-state control of reactivity in MLC catalysis.

Altered glucagon and GIP responses in South Asian versus Nordic women after gestational diabetes mellitus

Archana Sharma, Sindre Lee-Ødegård, Jens Juul Holst, Bolette Hartmann, A. Anita S. Kvist — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1007/s00125-026-06898-w

AIMS/HYPOTHESIS: We aimed to compare ethnic differences in levels of glucagon, glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide (GIP) and glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) following a pregnancy complicated by gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM), and to assess whether the higher insulin levels observed in South Asian compared with Nordic women could be explained by differences in the hormone secretory responses. METHODS: We measured glucagon, GIP and GLP-1 in 178 South Asian and 107 Nordic women during an OGTT, and stratified participants by glycaemic status. Glucagon and total GLP-1 and GIP responses when fasting and post-OGTT were assessed by linear mixed models. Ethnic differences in incretin and glucagon levels were evaluated by linear regression analyses. Pre-hepatic insulin levels were estimated using deconvolution of C-peptide kinetics. RESULTS: Ethnic differences in glucagon and incretin levels varied by glycaemic status. In the normoglycaemic South Asian group, early (0-15 min) glucagon suppression post-OGTT was significantly attenuated compared with the Nordic group (p=0.002). Fasting glucagon levels did not differ (p=0.139), but total glucagon exposure (including fasting levels) was higher in South Asian women (p=0.013). No differences in GIP and GLP responses to the OGTT were observed (p=0.490 and p=0.802). In women with prediabetes or diabetes, fasting glucagon was higher in the South Asian group (p=0.010), but there were no significant differences in early glucagon suppression post-OGTT (p=0.986). GIP responses to the OGTT were lower among South Asian women (p<0.001), despite higher insulin responses compared with the Nordic women. GLP-1 responses did not differ between the ethnic groups (p=0.661). The higher pre-hepatic insulin levels in South Asian vs Nordic normoglycaemic women were not associated with differences in incretin levels, but were associated with lower insulin sensitivity in the regression analyses (p<0.001). CONCLUSIONS/INTERPRETATION: When examined 1-3 years post-GDM, normoglycaemic South Asian women had less early glucagon suppression and higher total glucagon exposure post-OGTT compared with Nordic women. The higher pre-hepatic insulin levels were not associated with higher incretin levels, but were associated with lower insulin sensitivity.

HNRNPH2-R114W remodels RNA splicing and neurodevelopmental gene programs in isogenic cerebral organoids

Carlo Donato Caiaffa, Neda Ghousifam, Stephen Watkins, Helder Imoto Nakaya, Rodney A. Bowling — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fbioe.2026.1922305

Autism spectrum disorder has been linked to disrupted RNA processing during corticogenesis, yet variant-specific mechanisms remain difficult to resolve. Isogenic cerebral organoids derived from a patient-derived induced pluripotent stem cell (iPSC) line carrying the HNRNPH2-R114W variant and its CRISPR-corrected counterpart serve as a human developmental model to investigate variant-associated RNA dysregulation. Imaging quantification and exploratory bulk RNA sequencing, performed at a single developmental time point on a pooled organoid RNA from each genotype, identify coordinated remodeling of neuronal and metabolic programs, along with junction-centered splicing dysregulation involving 6,310 differentially used junctions. Computational structural docking situates HNRNPH2 RNA recognition motifs (RRMs) 1–3 within a spliceosomal context and locates residue R114 near a predicted RNA-contact interface, indicating that the R114W substitution may alter RNA engagement. Integration of differential expression with event- and isoform-level analyses identifies regulatory hubs enriched for axon guidance and synaptic signaling pathways. Single-cell-reference-based deconvolution suggests modest compositional shifts that are unlikely to account for the extent of transcriptional and splicing alterations observed. Collectively, these findings imply HNRNPH2-R114W in widespread RNA rewiring and define candidate isoform-level endpoints for mechanistic and therapeutic investigation in human cortical organoids. These molecular signatures open new avenues to examine how HNRNPH2-associated RNA dysregulation evolves across developmental time and recurs across independent biological contexts, providing a foundation for further mechanistic studies of this rare neurodevelopmental disorder.

Serotonergic psychedelics delay resting state neurovascular coupling dynamics

Lautaro Mundel, Vicente Medel, Christopher Timmermann, Patricio Orio, Josephine Cruzat — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.10.09.758082

Psychedelics are increasingly explored as tools to investigate consciousness and cognition, and as potential treatments for mood disorders. While neuroimaging has been central to characterizing their mechanisms of action, the interpretation of these studies may be confounded by the neurovascular effects elicited by these drugs. Here, we investigated the modulation of resting state neurovascular coupling by the serotonergic psychedelics LSD and DMT. Applying a blind deconvolution algorithm to blood-oxygen-level-dependent (BOLD) signals, we found that both drugs increased the estimated hemodynamic delays in regions overlapping with the distribution of serotonin receptors implicated in psychedelic action and vascular regulation. Deconvolution enhanced the detection of psychedelic-induced changes in whole-brain functional connectivity, suggesting that vascular effects obscure connectivity changes instead of generating spurious results. Finally, a biophysical whole-brain model linked these effects to increased flow-dependent feedback and slower dissipation of the vasoactive drive. These findings underscore the need to account for vascular effects in future psychedelic neuroimaging studies.

Analysis code for: Nutrition–inflammation gene-expression axes associate with myeloid inflammatory activity but do not predict survival in non-small cell lung cancer: a TCGA transcriptomic analysis

Yuanjing Yang, Qi Hu, Jianqiong Luo, Mingxiang Chen — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23427752

Analysis pipeline for a TCGA pan-cancer RNA-seq study of three a-priori nutrition–inflammation gene-expression axes in non-small cell lung cancer. The three signatures (16 genes each) are prior definitions built as proxies for the components of the advanced lung cancer inflammation index; the study explicitly does not claim equivalence with the clinical index. Pipeline. Stream subsetting of the UCSC Xena Toil pan-cancer RSEM FPKM compendium to TCGA-LUAD/TCGA-LUSC; reversal of the stored log2(FPKM + 0.001) encoding and Ensembl-to-symbol collapse; ssGSEA single-sample scoring (GSVA 2.x) of the three axes; LASSO-Cox modelling in the LUAD training cohort (glmnet, 10-fold cross-validation); internal hold-out validation with a repeated 7:3 split (100 repetitions) and feature selection performed inside the training set only; cross-histology generalisation to TCGA-LUSC; and three pre-specified sensitivity analyses covering the survival endpoint (OS / DSS / PFI), the feature scheme (three prior axes vs. six real MSigDB pathways vs. 48 single genes) and the training direction (LUAD→LUSC and LUSC→LUAD); and two robustness modules that calibrate every observed association against 1,000 random 16-gene panels per axis under two null models (expression-matched and unconstrained) scored in the same ssGSEA run, and recompute all axis–feature associations as Spearman partial correlations controlling for the ESTIMATE tumour-purity estimate (n = 1,010 matched samples). A second analysis module relates the three axes to the tumour immune microenvironment using pathway-level activity proxies from real MSigDB C5 GO:BP and HALLMARK entries, reported explicitly as activity proxies rather than deconvolution estimates. Result. The axes associate with myeloid inflammatory activity but do not predict survival: internal hold-out C-index 0.494 (log-rank P = 0.888) and a median repeated-split test C-index of 0.542 (2.5th–97.5th percentile 0.456–0.600). The prognostic result is reported as an honest negative rather than a rescued model. Reproducibility. All scripts are shipped unmodified except for one hard-coded project root, which relocate.py rewrites on the user's machine. Derived result tables are included so that every reported number can be checked without re-downloading the 720 MB expression matrix. CHECKSUMS.sha256 covers every file in this deposit, and the MSigDB version is pinned by byte-level md5 comparison against the Broad public release archive. Note. The TCGA expression matrix, the TCGA-CDR clinical table, the sample barcode list and the MSigDB gene sets are third-party public data and are not redistributed here; all accessions, download locations and locally verified fingerprints are listed in DATA_SOURCES.md.

Immunoinformatics-Based Design of a Chimeric Vaccine Against Necator americanus Targeting the Na-APR-1, Na-GST-1, and Na-ASP-2 Vaccine Candidates

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Yanick Aqua Stong Tangan, Gordon Takop Nchanji, Kwi Pilate Nkineh, Teke Mary Efeti, Joan Amban Chick — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.10.09.757581

Hookworm infection, caused predominantly by Necator americanus, is a soil-transmitted helminthiasis that disproportionately affects impoverished communities across tropical and subtropical regions, with an estimated 406-480 million people infected globally. Control continues to rely largely on mass drug administration, an approach challenged by rapid reinfection, the absence of durable protective immunity, and growing concern over reduced drug efficacy. While the leading recombinant protein antigens Na-APR-1, Na-GST-1, and Na-ASP-2 have demonstrated clinical safety and immunogenicity, the withdrawal of Na-ASP-2 after triggering allergic reactions in individuals with prior natural exposure revealed both the promise and the risk of single-antigen approaches, underscoring the need for a rationally designed multi-antigen vaccine. We used an immunoinformatics approach to design a novel chimeric vaccine candidate by combining carefully selected B-cell and T-cell epitopes from the major clinical-stage hookworm protein antigens with an immunostimulatory adjuvant sequence. The candidate's 3D structure was predicted, refined, and validated using established bioinformatics approaches, and its interaction with the innate immune receptor TLR4 was assessed through molecular docking and normal-mode analyses. The selected antigens showed conservation across related hookworm species, suggesting the potential for broader cross-protection. The designed candidate was predicted to be antigenic, non-allergenic, and non-toxic, and immune simulation analyses indicated its capacity to elicit coordinated humoral and cellular immune responses. HLA coverage analysis indicated broad population applicability. Docking and normal-mode analyses of its interaction with TLR4 indicated stable and favourable binding. This study offers a rational, safety-driven approach to multi-epitope vaccine design against hookworm infection, integrating the strengths of clinically evaluated protein antigens while addressing the safety concerns raised by prior single-antigen candidates. Further experimental validation is required to confirm the safety and efficacy of the vaccine construct in vitro and in vivo. These findings lay the groundwork for future efforts toward an effective immunoprophylactic strategy against this neglected disease.

Pathology-guided multimodal transformer predicts postoperative survival benefit from adjuvant chemotherapy in glioma: from DNA damage repair to immune characterization

Zehui Li, Xin Duan, Junjie Lian, Xuan Li, Qian Liang — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2026.1845251

Introduction High-grade gliomas (HGGs) are aggressive primary brain tumors with high recurrence rates and poor prognosis. Survival benefit from adjuvant temozolomide (TMZ) varies substantially across patients, and reliable preoperative biomarkers for treatment stratification remain limited. This study aims to develop an interpretable multimodal deep learning model integrating whole-slide histopathological images and multiparametric MRI to preoperatively predict survival benefit from adjuvant TMZ in HGG patients. Methods A Transformer-based multimodal fusion model was proposed. Key tumor regions were identified from whole-slide pathology images via weakly supervised clustering, and pathological features were extracted using attention-based multiple instance learning. Concurrently, 2.5D tumor subregional features were derived from multiparametric MRI sequences. These multimodal features were deeply fused using a Transformer encoder. The model was developed and validated on a multicenter cohort (504 patients), and its biological basis was explored through transcriptomic analysis, gene set enrichment analysis, protein-protein interaction networks, and immune deconvolution. Results The fusion model achieved AUCs of 0.910, 0.861, and 0.833 in the training, internal validation, and external validation cohorts, respectively. Transcriptomic analysis implicated DNA damage repair processes in high-benefit patients, suggesting that enhanced DNA repair capacity may contribute to TMZ sensitivity. Immune deconvolution showed higher M1 macrophage infiltration and lower regulatory T cell abundance in the high-benefit group, consistent with a less immunosuppressive tumor microenvironment. Importantly, low-benefit patients who completed TMZ chemotherapy had shorter survival than those treated with concurrent chemoradiotherapy alone or surgery alone. Conclusion This model identifies patients unlikely to benefit from standard TMZ chemotherapy, providing a tool for postoperative personalized treatment stratification and optimization of therapeutic strategies in HGGs.

A predefined sulphur-handling transcriptional programme is associated with colorectal cancer molecular subtypes

Shaheryar Shafi, Arslan Hamid, Jiao Yang, Xiaoxia Cheng, Minxuan Sun — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fonc.2026.1976885

Background Colorectal cancer (CRC) exhibits substantial metabolic and transcriptomic heterogeneity, but the relationship between sulphur-handling transcriptional programmes and consensus molecular subtypes (CMS) remains incompletely resolved. Methods We analysed a predefined 14-gene sulphur-handling panel across The Cancer Genome Atlas colon and rectal adenocarcinoma cohorts (TCGA-COAD/READ) and six independent HG-U133 validation cohorts ( n = 1,337 tumours). Analytical workflows integrated differential expression comparing tumours with adjacent-normal mucosa, CMS association modelling, random-effects meta-analysis, adjustment for three orthogonal tumour-purity estimators (ESTIMATE, consensus purity estimates, and ABSOLUTE), reference-based single-cell deconvolution with independent cross-atlas verification, and direction-aware therapeutic triage. Results Colorectal tumours demonstrated branch-specific transcriptional alterations, marked by coordinated downregulation of sulphur oxidation and clearance genes ( SQOR , ETHE1 , TST ) and of the cysteine-catabolism genes CDO1 and CTH , and selective upregulation of SLC7A11 and AHCY , whereas CBS was not significantly altered. The composite programme score differed across CMS subtypes and was consistently lower in mesenchymal CMS4 tumours than in non-CMS4 tumours across all discovery and validation series. This association remained statistically detectable after adjustment for three global tumour-purity metrics. The programme score showed no statistically detectable independent association with overall, recurrence-free, or disease-free survival. Single-cell deconvolution supported expression across epithelial, stromal, and immune compartments. Direction-aware therapeutic triage prioritised SLC7A11/xCT inhibition as an exploratory candidate for preclinical testing. Conclusion Sulphur-handling transcriptional architecture is reproducibly linked to CRC molecular taxonomy and is not fully explained by measured tumour purity variation, providing a computational framework for future functional and compartment-resolved investigation of sulphur metabolism in stratified CRC populations.

Neural posterior unfolding

Fernando Torales Acosta, Jay Chan, Krish Desai, Vinicius Mikuni, Benjamin Nachman — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.5445/ir/1000198008

Differential cross section measurements are the currency of scientific exchange in particle and nuclear physics. A key challenge for these analyses is the correction for detector distortions, known as deconvolution or unfolding. Binned unfolding of cross section measurements traditionally rely on the regularized inversion of the response matrix that represents the detector response, mapping pre-detector (`particle level') observables to post-detector (`detector level') observables. In this paper we introduce Neural Posterior Unfolding, a modern, Bayesian approach that leverages normalizing flows for unfolding. By using normalizing flows for neural posterior estimation, NPU offers several key advantages including implicit regularization through the neural network architecture, fast amortized inference that eliminates the need for repeated retraining, and direct access to the full uncertainty in the unfolded result. In addition to introducing NPU, we implement a classical Bayesian unfolding method called Fully Bayesian Unfolding (FBU) in modern Python so it can also be studied. These tools are validated on simple Gaussian examples and then tested on simulated jet substructure examples from the Large Hadron Collider (LHC). We find that the Bayesian methods are effective and worth additional development to be analysis ready for cross section measurements at the LHC and beyond.

Trend filtering deconvolution methods in epidemiology

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Rachel Lobay — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.14288/1.0456606

Reported cases provide a delayed and incomplete view of infection incidence, and hence make it difficult to recover the true timing and magnitude of an epidemic. To use such surveillance data to estimate latent infections, this dissertation develops a trend filtering deconvolution framework. Trend filtering uses discrete difference-based regularization of the signal to allow for locally adaptive reconstruction of infections over time, while deconvolution uses estimated probabilities to statistically reconstruct a signal from convolved data. These two modelling frameworks are thus useful for modelling epidemics whereby changes in infection counts can occur rapidly and belated case data are provided. We first establish our trend filtering deconvolution framework and use it to retrospectively estimate daily COVID-19 infections across U.S. states prior to the Omicron period, incorporating state-specific delay distributions as well as variant-specific incubation periods to obtain state-variant-specific deconvolved case estimates. We then scale the deconvolved estimates for unreported infections by using seroprevalence data in an antibody prevalence model. To extend the approach into the Omicron era, we introduce wastewater concentration data that enable infection estimation when case and seroprevalence surveillance data become less reliable. We then study the statistical properties of the estimator for convolution settings by extending classical trend filtering theory to the case where the design arises from delay distributions as a Toeplitz convolution operator. Using this approach, we establish an error bound that captures the extent that estimation accuracy depends on the smoothness of the signal and the conditioning of the convolution operator. We also develop a backfitting approach for multi-component deconvolution problems in additive settings and show how it can be used in practice to separate and estimate variant-specific latent infection signals.

Integrated analysis of scRNA-seq and bulk RNA-seq identifies prognostic cell populations and genes in cholangiocarcinoma

Xifang Wang, Wei Zheng, Gaili An, Yi Liu, Qi Shi — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12876-026-05389-x

Abstract Background Cholangiocarcinoma is a relatively uncommon cancer, accounting for approximately 3% of all gastrointestinal malignancies. However, its global incidence has been steadily rising over the past few decades, and the precise factors driving this increase remain unclear. This study aimed to identify prognosis-related cell populations and genes in cholangiocarcinoma and to further validate selected findings experimentally. Methods In this study, we performed a comprehensive analysis of single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) data on cholangiocarcinoma obtained from publicly available databases. The analytical workflow included clustering and cell annotation, GO, KEGG, GSEA analysis, TCGA data acquisition and preprocessing, Scissor analysis, survival analysis, and deconvolution analysis. Key findings were further validated using cell-based experiments. The expression of MT2A and TKT was further validated in clinical cholangiocarcinoma samples by Western blot and qRT-PCR, and the effects of MT2A on cell proliferation, migration, invasion, and apoptosis were assessed by in vitro experiments. Results Our findings revealed a significant imbalance between malignant cells and T cells within cholangiocarcinoma tissues, and a higher abundance of malignant cells was associated with poorer prognosis. The distribution of Scissor-positive cells was highly consistent with that of malignant cells, while differentially expressed genes in Scissor-positive cells, such as MT2A and TKT, were associated with poor prognosis in patients with cholangiocarcinoma. Functional experiments further demonstrated that MT2A promoted the proliferation, migration, and invasion of cholangiocarcinoma cells. Conclusions We identified distinct prognosis-related cell populations and differentially expressed genes in cholangiocarcinoma and further confirmed the functional relevance of MT2A through cell-based experiments.
