Global thyroid cancer incidence in relation to dietary and socioeconomic factors: an ecological machine learning study

Yixing Li, Dehui Liu, Ruixue Qin, Xingchang Li, Mei Li — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fonc.2026.1842508

Background Thyroid cancer incidence has risen dramatically worldwide, with marked geographic heterogeneity suggesting dietary and environmental contributions beyond screening effects. The roles of specific dietary patterns in thyroid cancer remain poorly characterized at the global level. Objectives Age-standardized thyroid cancer incidence was the prespecified primary outcome. We aimed to (1) build machine learning models predicting country-level thyroid cancer incidence from dietary and socioeconomic features, (2) quantify confounder-adjusted dietary associations with incidence, including prespecified sensitivity analyses of the iodine-rich food availability index, and (3) identify key dietary predictors through explainability analyses and a descriptive decomposition of the development pathway. Mortality was analyzed only as a secondary outcome in order to evaluate whether the incidence patterns could be attributed to differential cancer detection rather than to differences in disease occurrence. Methods We integrated GLOBOCAN 2022 (cancer outcomes), FAOSTAT Food Balance Sheets (113 food items, 2010–2022; primary exposure 2015–2019 mean), the UNDP Human Development Report 2025 statistical annex (HDI 2023 and its life expectancy, education, and income sub-indices), and WHO obesity prevalence (2022) across 163 countries, yielding 155 harmonized variables and 150 numeric predictors. All modeling was performed inside a nested cross-validation framework: multiple imputation by chained equations, standardization, four-method consensus feature selection, and hyperparameter search were fitted exclusively on the training partition of each outer fold (10 folds × five repetitions), and performance was summarized from pooled out-of-fold predictions with 95% confidence intervals obtained by resampling countries (2,000 bootstrap replicates). Three analyses separated dietary signal from confounding: block-wise models comparing socioeconomic-only, diet-only and combined feature sets; leave-one-region-out validation across six world regions; and confounder-adjusted partial correlations under a basic (HDI, obesity) and an extended adjustment set (HDI, obesity, the three HDI sub-indices, and region fixed effects), with Benjamini–Hochberg and Bonferroni correction applied across all comparisons. Iodine-related exposure was represented by an iodine-rich food availability index constructed from seafood, dairy, and egg supply; its construction was interrogated in four sensitivity analyses in which the components were entered separately and jointly, four weighting schemes were compared, total animal protein supply was added to the adjustment set, and an iodine-poor animal-origin food index was used as a specificity control. Binary classification and all mortality analyses were secondary. Results Regression on incidence was the primary task. Stacking ensemble performed best, with a pooled out-of-fold R ² of 0.613 (95% CI [0.510, 0.726]), RMSE = 3.04, MAE = 1.82, and Spearman ρ = 0.901 (95% CI [0.862, 0.924]); random forest ( R ² = 0.609), bagging ( R ² = 0.599), and KNN ( R ² = 0.580) followed, and a high-dimensional deep tabular model (DynaTab) was statistically comparable ( R ² = 0.626). Predictive accuracy was, however, almost entirely socioeconomic: nine development variables alone gave R ² = 0.609, 140 dietary variables alone gave 0.284, and the incremental contribution of the dietary block was –0.048 (95% CI −0.169 to +0.034), +0.021, and +0.017 across model families. Under leave-one-region-out validation, the rank ordering of countries was largely preserved (pooled ρ = 0.813), but the level was not. Unadjusted associations with incidence were positive for the iodine-rich food availability index ( ρ = 0.234), total energy ( ρ = 0.230), seafood ( ρ = 0.219), and the animal-to-plant protein ratio ( ρ = 0.221). After adjustment and Benjamini–Hochberg correction, seafood supply, dairy supply, the iodine-rich food availability index, the animal-to-plant protein ratio, and mollusks remained significant under the extended adjustment set (seafood partial r = 0.196, q = 0.031; iodine-rich food availability index 0.224, q = 0.026), whereas plant protein did not (–0.095, q = 0.309). In sensitivity analyses, the index association was carried by seafood (0.196, q = 0.017) and dairy (0.198, q = 0.017) but not eggs (0.065, q = 0.411), was stable across equal-weighted, z-score, and iodine-content-weighted constructions (0.238–0.256), survived further adjustment for total animal protein supply (0.203, q = 0.032), and was not reproduced by an iodine-poor animal-origin food index (0.102, q = 0.198). Path decomposition attributed 50.2% of the seafood–incidence correlation to the HDI pathway (95% bootstrap CI 16%–90%). In the secondary mortality analysis, the dietary ordering was preserved, but associations with the mortality-to-incidence ratio were close to zero (HDI ρ = 0.053; seafood ρ = 0.065), and in the sensitivity classification analysis, Extra Trees reached AUC = 0.932 (95% CI [0.893, 0.964]) at the median split. Conclusions With all model construction confined to training folds, machine learning models built from national food supply and socioeconomic data explain a moderate share of the between-country variation in thyroid cancer incidence, but that predictive accuracy is carried by development indicators rather than by diet. The availability of iodine-rich food groups, seafood supply, and the animal-to-plant protein ratio nevertheless retained positive ecological associations with incidence after adjustment for development and metabolic confounders and after correction for multiple comparisons, and the index association proved robust to how the index was constructed and specific to iodine-rich rather than to animal-origin food groups in general; plant protein showed no association once multiplicity was controlled. Roughly half of the seafood–incidence correlation tracks the development pathway, and the secondary mortality analysis is consistent with a substantial contribution from differential detection. These findings are ecological associations with the availability of iodine-rich food groups, not with measured iodine intake or exposure; they generate testable hypotheses for individual-level studies with measured iodine status and do not establish causation.

β-Boswellic Acid Suppresses Migration and Invasion and Attenuates TGF-β1-Induced EMT-Related Changes in PC-3 Prostate Cancer Cells

Kaixin Song, Kunpeng Zang, Zihan Yang, Zhirong Tan, Xiaohui Geng — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3390/cimb48101069

β-Boswellic acid (BA) has potential antitumor activity, but its effects on migration, invasion, and epithelial–mesenchymal transition (EMT)-related phenotypes in prostate cancer cells remain unclear. In this study, we integrated public transcriptomic data, candidate molecule prediction, machine learning, functional association analysis, and molecular simulations with MTT, wound-healing, Transwell, qPCR, Western blotting, transcriptomic analysis, and data-independent acquisition proteomics to evaluate BA effects on PC-3 cells and explore its potential molecular basis. We identified 22 candidate BA-related molecules and prioritised DPP4, MME, and ABL1 for further analysis. Higher BA concentrations reduced PC-3 cell viability. At the same time, BA suppressed wound closure, migration, and invasion and attenuated TGF-β1-induced EMT-related changes, accompanied by increased E-cadherin and decreased N-cadherin, Snail, and ZEB1 levels. DPP4, MME, and ABL1 also exhibited BA-associated expression changes. Integrated transcriptomic and proteomic analyses suggested that BA-associated molecular alterations involved the cell cycle, DNA replication, cell adhesion, cytoskeletal remodelling, and TGF-β-related processes. In conclusion, BA suppresses migration and invasion in PC-3 cells and attenuates TGF-β1-induced EMT-related changes. Candidate molecules including DPP4, MME, and ABL1, together with the multi-omics findings, provide exploratory clues for further investigation of the potential molecular basis of BA activity.

MAOMAO: an ontology-guided FAIR resource for harmonized peptide toxicity data

Nicole Soto-García, Julián Alfonso García - Vinuesa, Roberto Uribe-Paredes, Leandro Murgas, Karen Oróstica — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fphar.2026.1952924

Therapeutic peptides have emerged as a rapidly expanding class of biomolecules with applications spanning antimicrobial, antiviral, anticancer, immunomodulatory, metabolic, and diagnostic settings (8). Their high target specificity, favorable safety profiles, and growing clinical success have positioned them as attractive alternatives or complements to traditional small-molecule therapeutics (36). The number of experimentally characterized bioactive peptides has therefore increased substantially during the last decade (38), supporting the development of specialized databases and computational resources for peptide discovery, functional annotation, and rational design (5,15,31,35). Despite these advances, toxicity remains a major limitation to peptide development and clinical translation, constraining candidate selection during early-stage screening and optimization (3).Peptide toxicity encompasses a broad spectrum of adverse biological effects that arise from the interaction between peptides and biological systems. Most toxicity phenotypes are routinely evaluated during peptide development including cytotoxicity, hemolysis, neurotoxicity, embryotoxicity, and ichthyotoxicity (19,27). These toxicity endpoints have direct implications for efficacy, safety, dosage, and regulatory approval. Toxicity annotations have consequently accumulated across public databases (31,18,24), literature-derived resources (26,29,28,34), supplementary datasets (9,4,23), and experimental studies (33,39). However, these resources remain fragmented, employ heterogeneous terminology and annotation criteria, and provide variable levels of metadata and evidence quality (21). Their development for distinct biological objectives further complicates integration, reproducibility, and large-scale analysis (17).The growing availability of toxicity data creates opportunities for computational toxicology, data-driven peptide engineering, and large-scale biological analyses (32). Their utility, however, depends on the quality, consistency, and transparency of the underlying data (14,7). Annotation ambiguity, conflicting evidence, heterogeneous endpoint definitions, incomplete metadata, and limited provenance tracking can reduce reproducibility and complicate downstream reuse (1). The FAIR principles-Findability, Accessibility, Interoperability, and Reusability-provide a framework for organizing and distributing scientific data to support discovery, integration, reproducibility, and long-term reuse (22). Nevertheless, a standardized framework that harmonizes heterogeneous peptidetoxicity annotations while preserving evidence, provenance, and metadata remains largely unavailable (21). FAIR-oriented resources tailored to both biological interpretation and computational reuse are therefore still needed (37,13).To address these limitations, we developed MAOMAO (Metadata-Aware Ontology for Multi-source Annotation Organization), an ontology-guided resource integrating peptide-toxicity information from public databases, literature-derived resources, and curated datasets. MAOMAO standardizes toxicity terminology, preserves source-level provenance, distinguishes uncertainty-related evidence states, and organizes annotations within a unified hierarchical framework. The resource also provides harmonized metadata, descriptor-based representations, protein language model embeddings, benchmark resources, and supporting documentation. These components establish a FAIR-oriented and reusable foundation for biological interpretation, computational toxicology, and predictive modeling of peptide toxicity.The workflow used to construct MAOMAO is summarized in Figure 1A. Toxicity-related information from heterogeneous sources was preprocessed, harmonized, mapped to a unified toxicity ontology, evaluated for annotation conflicts and evidence quality, enriched with standardized metadata, and consolidated into the final resource. To support dissemination, computational reuse, and long-term maintenance, MAOMAO was organized into a modular architecture comprising complementary resource layers (Figure 1B).MAOMAO was constructed by integrating toxicity-related information from publicly available databases, literature-derived resources, supplementary datasets, and manually curated repositories. Candidate resources were identified through a structured exploration of the peptide toxicity landscape using scientific search engines, bibliographic repositories, specialized peptide databases, review articles, and citation tracking (2,16). Searches combined general terms, including peptide toxicity, toxic peptides, toxic peptide databases, and toxic peptide predictive models, with endpoint-specific queries for hemolytic peptides, cytotoxic peptides, cytolytic peptides, neurotoxic peptides, embryotoxic peptides, ichthyotoxic peptides, and anti mammalian cells peptides. Additional resources were identified from toxicity prediction tools, benchmark datasets, supplementary materials, associated repositories, and specialized peptide resources. Searches were conducted using Google Scholar and PubMed (6) between June 2025 and September 2026 and covered resources available through September 2026.Candidate resources were evaluated according to predefined eligibility criteria, including relevance to at least one MAOMAO toxicity endpoint, availability of peptide sequences with corresponding categorical toxicity annotations or quantitative toxicity measurements, sufficient provenance information, and the possibility of programmatic or manual retrieval. Resources were excluded when the underlying dataset could not be retrieved through the reported publication, supplementary material, repository, or web server, or when the available material lacked sequences suitable for sequence-level integration. This included resources containing only numerical representations, peptide names, or functional annotations without the corresponding sequences. Candidate-source screening, eligibility decisions, integrated and excluded resources, and source-specific exclusion reasons are detailed in Supplementary Section S1. (A) Ontology-guided harmonization workflow used to construct the resource. Toxicity-related information collected from public databases, literature-derived datasets, and curated resources undergoes preprocessing, terminology harmonization, ontology mapping, evidence integration, quantitative-property parsing, and metadata harmonization to generate a standardized and provenance-aware toxicity resource. (B) Modular architecture of MAOMAO. The resource is organized into complementary layers comprising harmonized toxicity annotations, numerical toxicity properties, sequence profiles, and metadata (Core Layer), sequence-derived descriptors (Descriptor Layer), protein language model embeddings (Embedding Layer), benchmark resources (Benchmark Layer), practical usage examples, and supporting documentation (Documentation Layer). Together, these components establish a FAIR-oriented and reusable resource for peptide toxicity research.Data acquisition combined direct database downloads, repository and supplementary-file retrieval, and manual extraction when required. Retrieved records were converted into a standardized intermediate representation, preserving peptide sequences, toxicity annotations, quantitative toxicity measurements when available, source identifiers, literature references, and available metadata. Source-level quality control verified the reported access route, the availability of primary sequences and interpretable toxicity information, and the associated publication or repository. Records lacking peptide sequences, containing toxicity information that could not be interpreted, or presenting irrecoverable formatting inconsistencies were excluded. Retained records were then forwarded to preprocessing, terminology harmonization, ontology mapping, metadata harmonization, and evidence integration.Following data collection, records underwent standardized preprocessing to support integration across heterogeneous resources (Figure 1A). This stage included sequence validation, format standardization, annotation parsing, identifier normalization, and quality control. The final resource was restricted to peptides containing the 20 canonical amino acids and lengths between 5 and 70 residues (38,25). Recoverable formatting inconsistencies were corrected, exact duplicates within each source were removed, and identical sequences reported by different sources were retained to preserve provenance and support evidence integration. Each normalized sequence was assigned a deterministic identifier generated by applying SHA-256 to the UTF-8 encoding of the uppercase, whitespace-free sequence. Quantitative HC50, LC50, LD50, and MHC observations were parsed while preserving their reported relation, value, unit, and provenance, and were linked to Core sequences through this identifier. Detailed sequence-processing, identifier-construction, and quantitative-property procedures are provided in Supplementary Section S1.To prevent terminology inconsistencies during cross-source integration, toxicity-related annotations were examined for alternative spellings, synonyms, endpoint-specific descriptions, and sourcedependent nomenclature referring to equivalent biological concepts. These variants were mapped to a standardized vocabulary while preserving their original biological meaning. For example, haemolytic was normalized to hemolytic, and cytolytic to cytolysis.Harmonized annotations were mapped to the MAOMAO toxicity ontology, centered on the root category Toxic. The ontology comprises Cytotoxic, Neurotoxic, Embryotoxic, and Ichthyotoxic as primary classes, with Hemolytic, Cytolysis, and Anti mammalian cells represented as subclasses of Cytotoxic. Hemolytic denotes toxicity involving erythrocyte lysis, whereas Cytolysis captures cell lysis or membrane-disruptive activity without a specified cell type. Anti-mammalian-cell activity denotes damage to mammalian cells or reduced cell viability (20,30,40). The vocabulary was restricted to distinct toxicity concepts supported by retrievable sequence-level evidence. Complete endpoint definitions, mappings, and assignment rules are provided in Supplementary Section S2.Following data collection, harmonization, and ontology mapping, annotations associated with identical peptide sequences were aggregated across sources (Figure 1A). Because the same sequence could be reported with different toxicity annotations, an evidence-management framework was applied to classify each peptide-endpoint pair as positive, negative, ambiguous, unlabeled, or no information. Positive states represented direct or hierarchy-supported toxicity evidence, whereas negative states corresponded to direct endpoint-specific non-toxic evidence. Ambiguous states captured conflicting or unresolved annotations. Unlabeled states denoted sequences retained in endpoint-specific sources without an explicit class assignment, while no information indicated the absence of evidence for that endpoint.Negative evidence was further classified as strong or weak according to the supporting information available in the original source. Strong negatives were explicitly supported by experimental evidence, whereas weak negatives originated from randomly selected negative sets or lacked confirmation of the absence of toxicity annotations. Evidence strength was retained in the metadata and provenance records, enabling downstream analyses to account for confidence, uncertainty, and conflicting evidence. Definitions, assignment criteria, and conflict-resolution rules are provided in Supplementary Section S2.To support transparency, interoperability, and long-term reuse, source-and endpoint-level metadata were normalized into a unified schema. Harmonized fields included source identifiers, literature references, organism information, annotation provenance, retrieval dates, file formats, and version information when available. Missing values were explicitly preserved without inference. The schema also retained original source labels, ontology mappings, evidence categories, publication references, and resource-specific annotations, providing a standardized representation of the integrated biological and technical information.Provenance was maintained by linking harmonized annotations to their source-specific processed datasets, endpoint-integration outputs, and metadata records. The sequence-level pivot provides the harmonized access layer, while provenance records, the ambiguity-support table, and the hierarchyaudit table document evidence aggregation, conflict resolution, and hierarchy-derived changes. These files allow each sequence-endpoint state to be traced to its supporting sources and processing decisions. Metadata fields, controlled vocabularies, and data types are detailed in Supplementary Section S3.To support reproducibility, computational reuse, and long-term maintenance, MAOMAO was organized into complementary functional layers (Figure 1B). This modular architecture separates harmonized biological data, computational representations, benchmark resources, and supporting documentation while preserving interoperability across the resource.The Core Layer contains harmonized peptide sequences, toxicity annotations, ontology mappings, evidence classifications, provenance information, and metadata. It additionally provides numerical toxicity properties and sequence profiles. Descriptor-based representations were generated using Roxy (11), whereas embedding-based representations were obtained with Sylphy (12) using publicly available protein language models. Benchmark resources were generated using partitioning strategies implemented through BioSieve (10). The Documentation Layer contains ontology specifications, metadata schemas, resource descriptions, licensing information, and versioning records. Supported representations, benchmark resources, resource organization, and file structures are detailed in Supplementary Sections S4-S5.FAIR principles guided the design and construction of MAOMAO to support transparency, reproducibility, interoperability, and long-term sustainability. FAIR-oriented practices were incorporated across data collection, terminology harmonization, ontology development, evidence management, metadata integration, and resource organization.Interoperability and reusability were supported through standardized toxicity terminology, ontology-guided endpoint mapping, harmonized metadata schemas, explicit evidence categorization, provenance tracking, and version-aware organization. These components enable heterogeneous toxicity annotations to be integrated while preserving the original information associated with each record.Findability, accessibility, and computational reuse were supported by distributing resource components in machine-readable formats and using standardized identifiers linking sequences, annotations, metadata, ontology terms, and evidence categories. The sequence-derived SHA-256 identifiers provide a stable key for linking corresponding records across the Core, Descriptor, Embedding, Benchmark, and sequence-profile resources. Harmonization procedures, ontology definitions, evidence-management rules, metadata specifications, and generation workflows were also documented to support validation, updates, and downstream use. Resource availability, file organization, versioning, and supporting documentation are detailed in Supplementary Section S5.An overview of MAOMAO is presented in Figure 2A. The resource integrates toxicity-related information from 56 publicly available databases, literature-derived resources, supplementary datasets, and manually curated repositories. Of 1,372,190 non-deduplicated source-level records initially collected, 898,637 were retained after source-specific preprocessing, representing a reduction of approximately 34.5%. Removed records included duplicates, incomplete entries, formatting inconsistencies, and annotations that did not satisfy the inclusion criteria.Following terminology harmonization, ontology mapping, cross-source evidence aggregation, state resolution, and quality control, the final release comprised 71,857 unique peptide sequences across eight toxicity-related endpoints. These were organized into 574,856 sequence-endpoint combinations, together with standardized metadata and provenance records linking annotations, ontology assignments, evidence classifications, and source-specific information.Publication references, repository or server information, download dates, file formats, source types, and resource behavior were documented for all 56 sources. Annotation content and last-update dates were available for 55 sources (98.21%), whereas explicit licensing information was available for 15 sources (26.79%). Missing metadata were preserved without inference. These components provide a harmonized resource for organizing, disseminating, and computationally analyzing peptide toxicity information.The ontology-guided organization of toxicity annotations is presented in Figure 2B. The root category Toxic contains 57,080 available peptide-endpoint states, including 13,824 positives, and comprises four primary classes, namely Cytotoxic (25,454 available states; 7,122 positive), Neurotoxic (3,183; 1,481 positive), Embryotoxic (2 positive), and Ichthyotoxic (5 positive). The Cytotoxic branch is further divided into Hemolytic (35,960 available states; 4,714 positive), Cytolysis (343; 341 positive), and Anti mammalian cells (21,393 available states; 3,664 positive), preserving more specific biological contexts. These categories define the endpoint structure used throughout MAOMAO.Evidence-state distributions are summarized in Figure 2C and Supplementary Table S10. Across MAOMAO, 31,153 states were positive, 91,506 negative, 15,717 ambiguous, and 5,044 unlabeled. Because only positive annotations were propagated through the hierarchy, all other states remained endpoint-specific, and no-information states were excluded from the percentages shown in Figure 2C. Toxic contained 13,824 positives, whereas Hemolytic had the highest proportion of ambiguous states (19.05%) and was the only endpoint with unlabeled states, representing 14.03% of its available annotations.Across the seven non-root endpoints, 7,331 of the 9,421 peptides with at least one positive annotation were assigned to multiple toxicity categories. This corresponds to 77.82% of positive peptides across these endpoints and 10.2% of all MAOMAO sequences. Percentages were calculated relative to the positive sequences assigned to each row endpoint and should therefore be interpreted directionally. The largest overlap occurred between Cytotoxic and Hemolytic, with 4,128 shared sequences, representing 57.96% of cytotoxic-positive and 87.57% of hemolytic-positive sequences. Complete pairwise counts and directional percentages are provided in Supplementary Section S2.MAOMAO distributes harmonized annotations, numerical representations, benchmark resources, metadata, ontology definitions, and documentation across the functional layers shown in Figure 1B.The Core Layer contains 574,856 harmonized peptide-endpoint combinations corresponding to 71,857 unique sequences, together with ontology assignments, evidence classifications, provenance, and standardized metadata. It additionally provides 169 HC50, LC50, LD50, and MHC measurement records. Most were linked to the Core sequence universe, while six LD50 records remained unmatched because their sequences exceeded the 70-residue limit. One profile per Core sequence summarizes its activity annotations, supporting evidence, ontology effects, toxicity targets, selected descriptors, and available numerical toxicity properties. The Descriptor Layer provides 41 features capturing sequence composition and physicochemical properties.The Embedding Layer includes 10 protein-language-model embedding matrices and a one-hot representation, all covering the complete set of 71,857 MAOMAO sequences.The Benchmark Layer contains 300 endpoint-seed-strategy configurations generated through random and stratified five-fold partitioning of five eligible endpoints (Toxic, Cytotoxic, Hemolytic, Neurotoxic, and Anti mammalian cells) using 30 random seeds. These correspond to 1,500 data partitions and, across the 11 numerical representations, 3,300 representation-specific configurations and 16,500 fold instances. Associated parameters, reports, summaries, and execution records are also provided.The Documentation Layer includes ontology definitions, metadata schemas, resource descriptions, versioning records, and licensing information. Files are distributed in standard formats, including CSV, JSON, and YAML, with detailed component descriptions and inventories provided in Supplementary Sections S4-S5.To facilitate direct reuse of the distributed assets, MAOMAO provides five executable notebooks that operate on the released files rather than reconstructing the resource from the original sources. The introductory workflow covers resource access, file validation, navigation of the Core Layer, and linkage of annotations, numerical representations, and benchmark partitions through stable sequence identifiers. Complementary notebooks support descriptive characterization of endpoint states, class balance, co-occurrence, sequence lengths, ontology consistency, and representation coverage, as well as retrieval and querying of individual sequence profiles. Generated tables and figures are stored separately from the released resource files.Two additional notebooks illustrate downstream machine-learning reuse. The neurotoxicity classification example joins a released train-validation-test partition with an existing protein language model representation and evaluates logistic regression, a linear support vector machine, and random forest models using fixed settings. The quantitative regression example shows how MAOMAO's numerical toxicity measurements can be combined with a released sequence representation in a simple regression analysis. These workflows are intended to demonstrate resource interoperability and reproducible reuse rather than establish state-of-the-art predictors or replace the released benchmark design. Detailed descriptions of the demonstration notebooks, execution requirements, and generated outputs are provided in Supplementary Section S6MAOMAO was designed as a reusable resource for peptide toxicity research, providing a harmonized collection of toxicity annotations, ontology mappings, evidence classifications, metadata records, numerical representations, and benchmark resources. By integrating heterogeneous toxicity information into a unified framework, the resource reduces challenges in data terminology inconsistencies, annotation ambiguity, and dataset enabling to on downstream biological and computational predictive The resource provides standardized toxicity endpoints, annotations, and benchmark partitions that can be used to and models for toxicity The availability of descriptor-based representations, protein language model embeddings, and predefined dataset partitions reproducible and of computational toxicity properties this to regression and quantitative activity while sequence enable sequence-level of annotations, supporting evidence, ontology effects, toxicity targets, and available MAOMAO support weak and and The explicit of annotation provenance, evidence categories, and ontology computational to account for annotation and interpretation and model The harmonized annotation structure also toxicity landscape endpoint peptide discovery, and peptide this reuse endpoint depends on the availability of retrievable evidence across the curated in class and limited representation of endpoints, Cytolysis, Embryotoxic, and Metadata and evidence strength also across and this downstream analyses the of provenance, conflicting annotations, and evidence categories. the predefined benchmark partitions on random and stratified without control and should therefore not be interpreted as address these through the of available toxicity datasets, endpoint and coverage, metadata numerical representations, and benchmark The modular architecture and deterministic sequence identifiers provide a for MAOMAO across resource

AI-Based Optimization Models for High-Performance Computing and Intelligent Network Architectures

Dr Atthapu Thirupathaiah, K. Arpitha, Kaushik Paul, Monisha Monisha, Armstrong Joseph J — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25258/ijddt.16.71s.154

Artificial Intelligence (AI) has become a necessary technology to optimize High-Performance computers (HPC) systems and intelligent network architecture, such as optimizing the use of resources, the schedulization of workloads and network efficiency. This paper discusses how the three AI-based optimization tools, i.e., Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL), Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) and Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) can be optimized to enhance performance in the HPC environment. It was done using a quantitative, simulation based, approach on the benchmark HPC workload and HPC network datasets. Data cleaning, feature selection, normalization and train-test splitting were used to preprocess the datasets and then model implementation and evaluation took place. The models were evaluated based on performance such as accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, execution time, resource consumption, throughput, latency as well as the energy consumption. Experimental results demonstrated that the DRL model achieved the highest prediction accuracy of 96.8, 93.8 CPU resources consumption, 96.1 network efficiency, 12.8 Gbps throughput and 5.3 ms (lowest) network latency values. LSTM had the highest prediction accuracy of 95.2, and XGBoost had a score of 93.9 with the lowest training time rate of 39 minutes. The findings indicate that AI-based optimization is tremendously more effective regarding calculations and adaptability, scheduling, and resource allocation compared to the conventional ones. The suggested framework offers a viable solution to scalable, smart and energy efficient, intelligent HPC systems, which can be used to support next generations of computing and networking systems.

CHARACTERIZATION OF DOPPLER SPECTROSCOPY ASSOCIATED WITH DEVELOPMENTAL COMPETENCE OF IN VITRO-PRODUCED PIG EMBRYOS

Kayode B Balogun — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.25394/pgs.34595979

One of the major unsolved challenges in assisted reproductive technology (ART) to date is the prediction of embryo viability before transfer. While conventional morphological grading does not require contact with embryos, it is also evaluator-dependent, subjective, and intrinsically limited, assessing only shape and symmetry, and not the actual signature of the physiological processes that take place inside an embryo. Two embryos that are identical under a microscope, and judged to be viable based on morphology, can have a very different underlying metabolic behavior and cytoskeletal activity. Those underlying characteristics, not only gross appearance, are what consequently determine whether an in vitro-produced embryo can develop suitably to a blastocyst that is transferable, or whether it finally ceases development and fails. The present dissertation aims to further address the above-mentioned lack of a quantitative and objective approach to viability assessment of porcine embryos by developing and validating a biodynamic imaging (BDI)-based approach. It comprises three studies, which are presented sequentially and are each a step towards the larger problem. The first was to establish the culture infrastructure to link a single optical measurement to the eventual fate of an embryo; the second was to show with the original BDI instrument that its spectral output really reflects viability; and the third was to re-engineer the imaging instrument itself to a more stable, physiologically interpretable configuration that is the system's future.The first study was a foundation, testing that in vitro-produced (IVP) porcine embryos can be cultured in the Well-of-the-Well (WOW) microwell culture system. In contrast to conventional group culture, which uses 10 or more embryos in one droplet of medium, the WOW dishes allow each embryo to be cultured in a separate microwell in a single droplet of medium, yet preserving the autocrine and paracrine benefits of group culture. This was a prerequisite for a subsequent study to correlate BDI spectral reading with the actual developmental result of an embryo. In a first experiment, cumulus-oocyte complexes under standardized in vitro maturation (IVM) and in vitro fertilization (IVF) conditions were allocated to five culture conditions, including individual microwell culture and two group culture conditions at different medium volumes, and a conventional dish control (500 embryos). This was followed by a second experiment (350 embryos), in which the best of the above tests was isolated and compared with a conventional control. There were no differences in the cleavage rates among any of the conditions examined, suggesting that the microwell effect is not due to early developmental competence and therefore occurs later in development. By contrast, blastocyst formation was significantly and consistently higher in embryos cultured individually in 50 μL WOW microwells compared to any of the grouped or conventional culture conditions (42.0% vs. 13.0 – 29.0%, P < 0.001 in Experiment 1; 37.9% vs. 28.1%, P = 0.040 in Experiment 2), and the blastocysts formed under the WOW microwells were of higher quality based on the number of nuclei (38.97 vs. 33.21, P = 0.017). These results not only report that the development of porcine IVP embryos in microwells is enhanced, but they also show that embryo quality is not compromised. In addition, individual embryo traceability was made possible, which is crucial for the remaining parts of the dissertation.The second study, building on the findings of the first, is a non-contact, label-free optical system using Doppler spectroscopy that characterizes the embryo viability signal using a original Biodynamic Imaging system. The BDI was able to assess embryo intracellular motion without disrupting the developmental trajectory and generate a 128-point spectral profile of each embryo in approximately three minutes. Porcine embryos were individually measured once per experimental session (13 independent sessions), distributed to the WOW microwells, and cultured to a developmental endpoint of expanded/hatched blastocyst stage at day 7, for a total of 199 individually tracked porcine embryos (104 identified as viable and 95 identified as non-viable). Nine biomarkers were extracted from each spectral profile, ranging from the low-frequency plateau, the mid-spectrum knee, the high-frequency Nyquist region, their running-mean equivalents, dynamic range, backscatter brightness, particle count, and normalized standard deviation. Five of these nine biomarkers significantly separated viable from nonviable embryos: four spectral-amplitude biomarkers spanning the plateau, knee, and Nyquist regions showed uniformly elevated values in viable embryos (Cohen's d = 0.42–0.48), and particle count showed a smaller but still significant elevation (d = 0.31). Significant separation was observed at all 128 spectral indices tested, with no evidence of a localized change in spectral shape, as there was no significant difference between viable and nonviable embryos in dynamic range. The nine-biomarker Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) classifier yielded an AUC of 0.645 in the training set, and an AUC of 0.550 ± 0.091 in five-fold cross-validation. This confirmed that the signal in the original BDI system is a viable signal that is indeed meaningful from a biological point of view. The discriminative value of this signal was relatively weak, indicating the need for a more stable and more physiologically specific measure to further refine this classification.The third study partially addresses the need identified in the second study by re-engineering the optical configuration of the BDI. The configuration of the original BDI’s two-arm interferometer was changed to a common-path Fresnel biprism configuration where the reference beam and the object beam both share the same optical elements, instead of traveling two different paths. This makes the system more stable against long-time exposures to vibration and path-length drift. This system recorded backscattered light from 4-cell stage IVP porcine embryos and then numerically reconstructed it into holograms and optical coherence images. Temporal Fourier transforms of the pixel-wise intensity time series were then used to produce Doppler power spectra. It was possible to fit the spectra with a single physiologically interpretable parameter, the knee frequency describing a qualitative difference between diffusive and actively driven intracellular transport. While dead embryos were entirely devoid of any knee, living embryos consistently exhibited a knee, clustering around approximately 0.5 Hz, making the difference between the two more of a categorical distinction than only a statistical one, and indicating complete cessation of active, energy-dependent intracellular transport at death. The knee frequency was then monitored continuously over a period of 45 minutes in the living embryos, revealing a progressive decrease in the frequency over time, thus achieving a snapshot of the loss of metabolic vigor that would never have been seen on a single snapshot measurement.Each of these three studies describes a clear progression: A validated culture platform that enables the tracking of individual-embryo viability, validation of BDI-derived spectral viability biomarkers that are globally distributed, and a re-engineered, more stable Fresnel biprism instrument that can generate a single physiologically interpretable biomarker of embryo viability, usable to classify viability and track its decline continuously over time. When viewed in the context of the entire dissertation, this series points to the direction of success in achieving a quantitative, physiologically inspired, machine-learning-based approach to predicting the viability of early embryos in a porcine model.

Application of computer simulation technology in the optimization and performance evaluation of smart sports teaching systems in universities

Yongkang Zhao, Sahar Khayatnezhad — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-75236-2

This study proposes a computer-simulation-driven framework for optimizing and evaluating smart sports teaching systems in universities. Using MHEALTH as the main multimodal wearable-sensor dataset, and UCI HAR Smartphone and Daily and Sports Activities as external validation datasets, the framework integrates wearable-sensor preprocessing, window-level feature construction, activity recognition, teaching-state indicator construction, sensor-ablation analysis, scenario simulation and optimization, ...statistical validation, and computational implementation-feasibility assessment. Activity Load Index, movement stability, and a sensor-derived safety proxy were constructed to translate acceleration, gyroscope, magnetometer, and ECG signals into interpretable teaching-state representations. To address subject-dependence and potential overestimation from a fixed train/test split, classification performance was re-evaluated using leave-one-subject-out validation. In the realistic MHEALTH setting including the dominant null class, Gaussian Naive Bayes achieved the highest mean Macro F1 of 0.652, while tuned Extra Trees achieved an accuracy of 0.819 and a Macro F1 of 0.563. In the activity-only setting, Random Forest and Extra Trees achieved mean Macro F1 scores of approximately 0.95, indicating strong but not universally perfect subject-independent recognition. External validation produced Extra Trees accuracies of 0.941 on UCI HAR and 0.964 on Daily Sports. Sensitivity analyses showed that the principal high-load activity assignments remained stable under plausible variations in Activity Load Index weights and thresholds. Formal constrained optimization identified a 25% low, 65% moderate, 8% high, and 2% very-high state allocation as the utility-maximizing solution, corresponding to a 13.7% simulated utility gain over the observed baseline. These results support the use of the proposed framework as an interpretable, subject-independent decision-support approach for evaluating wearable-sensor-based sports teaching scenarios.

Pelvic magnetic stimulation added to pelvic-floor muscle training for female vaginal laxity: a randomized controlled trial

Runzhi Shi, Xi Ye, Yangyang Tan, Runqian Shi, Xinglian Du — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-75802-8

To evaluate the short-term efficacy of pelvic magnetic stimulation combined with pelvic floor rehabilitation training (Intervention) versus isolated pelvic floor muscle training (PFMT) among women diagnosed with vaginal laxity (Population). The Vaginal Laxity Questionnaire (VLQ) total score assessed at 8 weeks of treatment was predefined as the primary outcome (Outcome), and multiple subjective and objective indicators were adopted as secondary outcomes to comprehensively reflect pelvic floor function recovery. A total of 147 patients diagnosed with vaginal laxity at the Department of Obstetrics and Gynecology, Qinghai Red Cross Hospital—either during outpatient visits or hospitalization—from March 2025 to October 2025 and meeting the inclusion criteria were enrolled. After providing informed consent, all participants were randomly assigned using a random number table into two groups: the control group (n = 73) receiving PFMT alone, and the combined-intervention group (n = 74) receiving the combined intervention. Both groups underwent an 8-week treatment regimen, with a treatment log established for each participant. Assessments were conducted at three predefined time points, evaluating the following eight outcome measures: VLQ, female sexual function, degree of vaginal laxity, vaginal health index score(VHIS), pelvic floor type I and type II muscle fibers, surface electromyography (sEMG) of the pelvic floor muscles, three-dimensional ultrasound parameters, and mean vaginal pressure. The primary outcome (8-week VLQ total score) was significantly higher in the combined-intervention group compared with the control group at both 4-week and 8-week follow-ups (both P < 0.001). However, although the primary analysis employed cross-sectional between-group comparisons at each timepoint rather than a formal group × time interaction test, a supplementary linear mixed-effects model with group × time interaction was performed as a sensitivity analysis and confirmed significant differential change trajectories between groups (Group × Time interaction: F = 29.587, P < 0.001 for VLQ; F = 495.662, P < 0.001 for FSFI total score). All secondary subjective indicators including total Female Sexual Function Index (FSFI) and its six subdomains, vaginal laxity finger grading, type I/II pelvic floor muscle fiber strength and fatigue resistance showed significantly superior improvements in the combined-intervention group ( P < 0.05). For objective imaging and functional metrics, most 3D pelvic floor ultrasound parameters and vaginal resting/cough/defecation pressure values were significantly better in the combined-intervention group at week 8 ( P < 0.05). No intergroup statistical differences were detected in vaginal health index (VHIS) total score, post-contraction resting-phase sEMG value, resting bladder neck initial position and levator-urethra gap(LUG) at either follow-up time point ( P > 0.05). These preliminary findings suggest that combined pelvic magnetic stimulation and PFMT may improve vaginal laxity and sexual function compared with PFMT alone. Results require confirmation in multicenter, sham-controlled trials with longer follow-up. Trial registration : Chinese Clinical Trial Registry (ChiCTR2600122143); registered on April 9, 2026.

Identifiability from Moments in Independent Component Analysis Mixture Models

Pardis Semnani, Konstantin Usevich, Elina Robeva — 2026-10-16 — arxiv

DOI : —

We study Independent Component Analysis (ICA) mixture models. These models consist of mixtures of ICA densities. An ICA density is associated with a random vector whose coordinates become mutually independent after an invertible linear transformation. ICA mixture models are nonparametric extensions of the celebrated family of Gaussian mixtures. We show that in many cases the ICA mixtures are identifiable from the collection of the observed moment tensors, that is, the mixing coefficients, linear transformations, and source moments of order up to $d$ can be identified from the moment tensors of order up to $d$ for generic parameters. We address the problem by studying the corresponding secant varieties and their algebraic properties such as dimensions and Gauss maps. In particular, for $n$-variate distributions, we establish that generically and up to the trivial scaling and permutation symmetries, when $d\geq 5$, the parameters can be recovered uniquely for asymptotically up to $n^{d-2}/d!$ mixture components. For $d=3,4$, we show identifiability up to a finite equivalence class for asymptotically up to $n^{d-2}/d!$ and $0.5 n^{d-2}/d!$ components respectively, where for $d=4$ the mixing coefficients need to be known a priori. The obtained bounds on the component numbers have the maximum possible orders in $n$, and for $d = 3$ and $d\geq 5$, they are also asymptotically sharp.

An Internet-Based Qualitative Research on Vaccine Opposition and Hesitation in Türkiye: Scientific and Ethical Analysis of Social Media Posts from a Primary Care Perspective

Murat Doğan, Arif Hüdai Köken — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.4274/hamidiyemedj.galenos.2026.08379

Background: Vaccine opposition and hesitancy have become increasingly visible in digital environments, where scientific claims are frequently intertwined with ethical concerns, cultural interpretations, and personal beliefs.This study examines how vaccine-critical narratives are articulated on Turkish social media and explores their underlying scientific and ethical dimensions from a primary care perspective. Materials and Methods:A qualitative case-study design was adopted.Publicly accessible posts shared on X (formerly Twitter) and Facebook between January and March 2025 were identified through purposive sampling.Posts written in Turkish and expressing vaccine-hesitant or anti-vaccine viewpoints were included.A total of 1,100 posts were analyzed using descriptive content analysis informed by a phenomenological interpretive framework, enabling the identification of recurring themes and shared meanings within user narratives.Results: The analysis identified four main thematic domains: (1) biopolitics and global power structures, (2) vaccine insecurity linked to insufficient or distorted information, (3) tensions between individual freedom and state intervention, and (4) religious and cultural interpretations of vaccination.Across these domains, distrust of pharmaceutical companies, fears about long-term side effects, and opposition to compulsory vaccination emerged as particularly prominent patterns. Conclusion:The findings demonstrate that social media serves not only as a source of information but also as a space in which vaccine skepticism is socially reinforced and ethically legitimized.Addressing vaccine hesitancy in this context requires strategies that extend beyond factual correction, emphasizing transparent scientific communication, ethical awareness, and empathetic engagement, especially within primary care settings where trust-based relationships play a central role.

Assessing a local vaccination initiative: Eight years of “Immuniser Lyon” in France

Juliette Dessemon, Romain Saux, Christelle Elias, Anne-Sophie Ronnaux-Baron, Anne Sophie Malachane — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pgph.0007468

"Immuniser Lyon" (IL) is a French city-level initiative launched in 2015 to promote vaccination through coordinated, locally tailored actions. The objective of this study is to describe and compare temporal trends in vaccine coverage for HPV and influenza in the Rhône area versus the neighboring Auvergne-Rhône-Alpes (ARA) region, and to explore whether observed patterns are consistent with a potential contribution of the IL initiative. A comparative analysis of vaccine coverage was performed for HPV (2010-2022) and influenza (2016-2020) between different areas within the Auvergne-Rhône-Alpes (ARA) region versus the ARA region, using binomial log-linear models. HPV vaccine coverage in Rhône and ARA followed similar patterns, increasing from 2015 to 2022 after a nationwide decline. For influenza, vaccine coverage among adults aged ≥65 was significantly higher in Lyon in 2020 (64.5%) than in the rest of ARA (59.2%, OR=1.25, 95% CI [1.24-1.26], p < 0.001). While HPV trends reflected broader national dynamics, the observed pattern may be consistent with a potential contribution of IL to the accelerated uptake of influenza vaccines in older adults. These findings highlight the potential of sustained local action to complement national immunization strategies.

Identification of stable reference genes for quantitative real-time PCR in human fibroblasts from lymph nodes and synovium

Sajede Rasouli, Chaja M. J. van Ansenwoude, Johanna F. Semmelink, Lisa G. M. van Baarsen, Tineke Anna de Jong — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-75261-1

Abstract Real-time quantitative PCR (RT-qPCR) is a widely used method for gene expression analysis; however, its accuracy depends on the selection of stable reference genes for normalization. To date, no study has systematically identified suitable reference genes for primary human fibroblast-like synoviocytes (FLS) and lymph node stromal cells (LNSCs) in the context of rheumatoid arthritis (RA), despite the critical role of these stromal cells in disease pathogenesis. We evaluated the expression stability of 12 candidate reference genes in FLS and LNSCs subjected to various in vitro conditions, including inflammatory stimulation, senescence induction, and differentiation. Candidate genes were selected based on common usage and Genevestigator analysis of publicly available transcriptomic datasets. Expression stability was assessed using Clarida and RefFinder software, integrating geNorm, NormFinder, BestKeeper, and the comparative delta Ct method. The number and identity of recommended reference genes depended on cell type and experimental condition. In LNSCs, two genes, RPLP0 and POLR2G , were recommended across all tested conditions. In contrast, FLS exhibited condition-dependent variability, with POLR2G and TBP being recommended for untreated FLS. In contrast, combinations of three genes were recommended for FLS subjected to TNFαLTαβ stimulation ( EEF1A1 , NFIC and POLR2A ), senescence-related conditions ( EEF1A1 , GAPDH and NFIC) and adipogenesis ( POLR2G , EEF1A1 and TBP) . Comparison of normalization strategies in untreated FLS showed that reference gene choice influenced relative target gene expression patterns and their interpretation. This study provides the first systematic evaluation of reference genes for RT-qPCR normalization in human FLS and LNSCs. Our findings highlight the necessity of condition- and cell type-specific validation of reference genes to ensure accurate and reproducible gene expression analysis in studies of fibroblast biology and inflammatory disease.

Investigation of the Relationship Between Epstein-Barr Virus and Serum Magnesium Levels in Lymphoma Patients

Mehmet Batuhan Duman, Fuat Erdem, Gülden Sincan — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.4274/ahot.galenos.2026.2025-11-7

Investigation of the Relationship Between Epstein-Barr Virus and Serum Magnesium Levels in Lymphoma PatientsAim: Lymphoma pathogenesis involves complex interactions of genetic, environmental, and immunological factors.While Epstein-Barr virüs (EBV) is recognized as a contributing factor in several lymphoma subtypes, and magnesium is essential for immune regulation.This study investigated the relationship between serum magnesium levels and EBV positivity in lymphoma patients, and the impact of magnesium on survival.Methods: This retrospective study included 108 lymphoma patients.Demographic, clinical, EBV status, and serum magnesium levels were collected.Statistical analyses, including the chi-square test, Kaplan-Meier estimation, and receiver operating characteristic curve analysis, were performed.Results: Serum magnesium levels were significantly lower in EBV-positive patients (1.82 mg/dL) compared to EBV-negative patients (1.98 mg/dL) (p=0.049).Patients with magnesium levels ≤1.68 mg/dL had significantly shorter overall survival and disease-free survival, particularly in Hodgkin lymphoma (HL).In diffuse large B-cell lymphoma, diminished magnesium levels were associated with inferior survival outcomes.Low magnesium levels were also associated with advanced disease stage (p=0.006).Conclusion: Lower serum magnesium concentrations in lymphoma patients are associated with EBV positivity and poorer survival, particularly within the HL subgroup.Magnesium levels may be associated with prognosis in lymphoma patients; however, larger prospective studies are needed to determine their independent prognostic value.

Epigenetic and Metabolic Changes That Drive Trained Immunity in Response to Microbes and Vaccines

Desalegn Abebaw, Aytenew Atnaf, Bantayehu Addis Tegegne, Abebe Fenta, Bantegzie Selabat — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1111/imm.70216

Trained immunity is a form of innate immune memory in which initial exposure to microbial components or vaccines leads to long-lasting functional reprogramming of innate immune cells through epigenetic and metabolic changes. Unlike adaptive immunity, it does not rely on B or T lymphocytes and provides broad, nonspecific protection against various pathogens. This phenomenon has been observed across a range of immune cells, including monocytes, macrophages, neutrophils, NK cells and even γδ T-cells, as well as at the level of haematopoietic stem and progenitor cells (HSPCs). Key inducers include microbial products (e.g., β-glucan, BCG and MMR vaccines), metabolic byproducts (e.g., mevalonate and lactate) and cytokines such as IL-1β. These agents activate signalling pathways such as the Akt/mTOR/HIF-1α pathway and lead to chromatin remodelling via histone modifications such as H3K4me3 and H3K9me3. Immunometabolism plays a pivotal role, with trained cells shifting toward glycolysis, glutaminolysis and cholesterol synthesis. Lactate and fumarate further link metabolism to epigenetic remodelling. Notably, trained immunity can be protective in infectious diseases and beneficial in noninfectious contexts, such as cancer immunotherapy and autoimmune regulation. This review provides a comprehensive overview of the cellular, epigenetic and metabolic mechanisms underlying trained immunity, highlighting how innate immune cells acquire memory-like properties independent of adaptive immunity. This study also provides insights into the therapeutic potential of leveraging trained immunity for vaccine development, infection control and immunomodulation in noninfectious diseases.

The tripartite intersection of entrepreneurship, gender, and digitalization through an integrated lens of the FIT-5M framework

Rashed Alzahmi, Urwa Muhammad Tariq, Safa Veeran Kutty, Issa AlBalushi, Raihan Taqui Syed — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1108/jeee-11-2025-0700

Purpose This study aims investigate how Emirati women entrepreneurs in the United Arab Emirates food and beverage (F&B) sector negotiate gendered institutional norms within digital entrepreneurial ecosystems, using an integrated Feminist Institutional Theory (FIT)-5M framework to connect institutional logics with resource mobilization and role expectations. Integrating FIT with the 5M model is theoretically warranted because FIT clarifies how formal, informal and cognitive institutions shape women’s opportunities, while the 5M dimensions (money, market, management, motherhood, macro/meso) specify how these institutional forces are experienced as concrete constraints and enablers in women’s entrepreneurial lives. Design/methodology/approach Adopting an interpretivist, feminist stance, the study uses reflexive thematic analysis to capture how meaning-making around legitimacy, modesty and agency is co-constructed between the researcher and participants, which aligns more closely with the study’s epistemology than structured coding templates such as Gioia’s. Twenty Emirati women founders and co-founders of digitally enabled F&B ventures were selected through purposive and snowball sampling to secure information-rich variation across age, emirate, venture maturity and format (home-based bakeries, hybrid cafés and restaurants, confectionery brands and online-only concepts), with thematic sufficiency achieved consistent with qualitative entrepreneurship standards. Findings Findings reveal that digital platforms expand reach and autonomy but simultaneously reinforce normative boundaries, producing context-specific forms of bounded visibility and legitimacy work. Five interlinked mechanisms are identified – bounded visibility, family governance and moral pacing, meso-level translation, capability formation and online-to-offline risk work – showing how motherhood and macro/meso condition money, market and management in the Gulf context. Research limitations/implications The focus on Emirati nationals in the F&B sector limits statistical generalizability but offers analytically transferable insights for similar patriarchal, digitally transforming economies. Future research could incorporate cross-sectoral or comparative regional studies to validate and generalize these mechanisms. Practical implications The study highlights the need for culturally congruent ecosystem supports, including women-led mentorship, gender-responsive platform and policy design. Originality/value The research advances a context-specific theory of bounded agency in platformised markets, deepening both FIT and 5M by demonstrating how digital technologies relocate, rather than erase, patriarchal institutional logics.

Disclosing pathogenic processes of monkeypox (MPOX) and drug repurposing through host-transcriptomics analysis

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Sabkat Mahmud, Alvira Ajadee, Md. Ahad Ali, Md. Feroj Ahmed, Reaz Ahmmed — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pntd.0014797

Monkeypox (MPOX) virus infection outbreak in Africa in 2022 was initially considered a neglected tropical disease (NTD). Now it is recognized as a global health problem. The recent global spread has emphasized the need for increased attention to combat MPOX. Although there are some vaccines against MPOX, most people worldwide are still not vaccinated due to their unawareness or unavailability. However, so far, there is no approved drug against MPOX yet. Therefore, effective drugs against MPOX are also essential. This study attempted to disclose pathogenic processes and therapeutic strategies of MPOX based on MPOX-associated host key-genes (hKGs). At first, three transcriptomic profile datasets were analyzed, identifying 166 common host differentially expressed genes (hDEGs) between MPOX patients and control groups, comprising 75 upregulated and 91 downregulated hDEGs. Subsequently, six top-ranked host key genes (hKGs): ALB, HSP90AA1, CDH1, PTPRC, IGF1, and ABL1, were identified as potential MPOX-causing genes through protein-protein interaction (PPI) network analysis. Functional enrichment analysis of these hKGs revealed critical pathogenic processes of MPOX infection, including biological processes, molecular functions, cellular components, and KEGG pathways. Regulatory network analysis involving transcription factors and miRNAs provided further insights into disease mechanisms. Based on molecular docking analysis, five top-ranked drug molecules Tecovirimat, Beclabuvir, Maraviroc, Raltegravir, and Brincidofovir were identified as potential repurposable therapeutics against MPOX. ADME/T and drug-likeness evaluations confirmed favorable pharmacokinetic properties for these candidates. Additionally, 100 ns molecular dynamics (MD) simulations of the top three complexes (ALB-Tecovirimat, CDH1-Maraviroc, and PPARG-Beclabuvir) demonstrated strong binding stability, supported by RMSD, RMSF, and MM-GBSA results. Therefore, the results provided in this study might be potential resources for diagnosis and therapies of MPOX patients. However, the findings require experimental validation in wet lab for further confirmation.

Perceived viewing-experience enhancement and audience acceptance: applying TAM to AI-based sports broadcasting services

Liuxin Li — 2026-10-16 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fspor.2026.1945033

Introduction Despite the rapid proliferation of artificial intelligence (AI) technologies in sports broadcasting, empirical evidence validating the Technology Acceptance Model (TAM) and experiential outcomes in this specific context remains limited. This study aims to address this gap by investigating the statistical relationships among core TAM constructs, usage attitude, behavioral intention, and perceived viewing-experience enhancement within AI-based sports broadcasting services. Methods Based on the theoretical framework of TAM, this study collected valid data from 630 Chinese respondents. Structural equation modeling (SEM) was employed to quantitatively test the hypothesized research model and examine the interrelationships between the proposed variables. Results The empirical results demonstrate that perceived ease of use positively predicts perceived usefulness, and both perceived ease of use and perceived usefulness exert significant positive effects on users' usage attitude. Furthermore, usage attitude is positively associated with behavioral intention and perceived viewing-experience enhancement. Discussion This research contributes context-specific empirical validation to the TAM framework in the emerging domain of AI-driven sports broadcasting. It extends existing TAM literature by integrating individual-level experiential outcomes alongside traditional behavioral intention indicators in sports media consumption. Practically, the findings provide actionable insights for optimizing user experience and improving service design for AI sports broadcasting platforms.

Optimal approximation of smooth functions by ReLU neural networks

Thomas Nagler — 2026-10-15 — arxiv

DOI : —

We study uniform approximation of the Hölder ball $\mathcal H_1^s([0,1]^D)$ by fully connected ReLU networks. Except in the one-dimensional Lipschitz case, existing constructions remain separated from the information-theoretic efficiency limit. This article closes the gap for every $s>0$ and $D\ge1$: networks with width $O(N)$, depth $O(L)$, and parameter magnitude $O(1)$ attain uniform approximation error of order $(N^2L^2\log N)^{-s/D}$. No relation between width and depth is required. The construction uses new encoding and decoding mechanisms that exploit higher-order finite-difference bounds for local Taylor coefficients of the target function. As a statistical consequence, empirical risk minimizers over the corresponding clipped network classes attain the minimax regression rate $n^{-2s/(2s+D)}$ without additional logarithmic factors. The paper also gives a detailed exposition of the key approximation techniques, covering both established and new mechanisms.

A Bayesian Hidden Markov Model with Singh--Maddala Emission Distributions for Heavy-Tailed Regime-Switching Time Series

Safaa K. Kadhem — 2026-10-15 — arxiv

DOI : —

Heavy-tailed time series with regime switching arise in environmental, financial, and engineering applications, yet conventional emission distributions such as Gaussian, Gamma, or Weibull fail to capture simultaneously extreme right-skewness, slowly decaying upper tails, and temporal dependence. This paper develops a fully Bayesian Hidden Markov Model with Singh-Maddala (Burr Type XII) emission distributions (HMMSM). We derive the complete Bayesian structure and design an adaptive Metropolis-within-Gibbs sampler combining Forward-Filtering Backward-Sampling, conjugate Dirichlet updates, and adaptive Metropolis steps. Simulations confirm parameter recovery with relative errors below 10% and 95% prediction coverage of 94.5%. On a simulated benchmark with heavy tails, Bayesian model selection via WAIC, DIC, and LOO correctly identifies K = 3, and HMMSM outperforms seven competing specifications by large margins. On a 26-year gridded rainfall record for Ireland (T = 312 monthly observations), HMMSM attains the lowest WAIC but is statistically indistinguishable from HMM-Gamma, reflecting the light tails of Irish rainfall. The selected two-regime model identifies a Moderate regime (mean approx. 87 mm) and a Wet regime (approx. 134 mm) that are persistent and seasonally coherent. We also document weak identifiability of the Singh-Maddala scale parameter. The framework is general-purpose, and reproducible code is provided.

Robust Estimation under Spurious Correlation via Statistical Counterfactual Augmentation

Yi-Hui Zhou, George Sun — 2026-10-15 — arxiv

DOI : —

Spurious correlations between nuisance features and outcomes present a fundamental challenge for statistical learning and inference. Counterfactual Data Augmentation (CDA), the practice of resampling nuisance features while preserving features of interest, has emerged as a popular strategy to mitigate such correlations, yet its statistical properties, and its reliance on an untestable validity assumption, remain largely unexplored. We provide a statistical treatment of CDA, termed Statistical CDA (SCDA), with two components. First, casting SCDA as a simulated-moment M-estimator, we establish consistency, asymptotic normality with a sandwich covariance and a finite-augmentation-draw variance correction, oracle efficiency for the target parameter, and the equivalence of iterative and one-shot procedures via stochastic approximation, yielding valid large-sample inference where previously none was available. Second, because the augmentation's validity cannot be tested from the observed data when features are entangled (a fact we prove), we develop a sensitivity analysis that bounds the residual nuisance--outcome dependence by a parameter $Γ$, giving sharp identified intervals for the target with valid confidence intervals and a breakdown frontier; the point-identified theory is the $Γ=0$ special case. Simulations confirm the efficiency gains under validity and the robustness bounds under possible invalidity, placing a widely used augmentation method on a transparent inferential footing.

Alert-List Concordance for Evaluating Imputation Strategies in ICU Sepsis Prediction

Priyanka Siddappa, Muntaqim Ahmed Raju, Shifat Haider Al Amin, Ruizhe Ma — 2026-10-15 — arxiv

DOI : —

Preprocessing is an under-measured reliability risk in clinical prediction: a model can keep a similar AUROC and still change who receives an alert. Imputation quality is commonly evaluated using reconstruction error or aggregate discrimination, neither of which directly measures agreement on which patients are flagged. At a matched alert rate, pipelines with similar aggregate performance can produce different patient lists. This question is especially relevant under the frequent, structured missingness of real ICU records. We therefore introduce alert-list concordance, which asks whether two pipelines identify the same high-risk patients, measured by the Jaccard similarity of their top-k% alert sets with patient-level bootstrap confidence intervals. On PhysioNet/CinC 2019 (40,336 patients, 71.9% missingness), we compare nine imputation strategies and a native-missingness baseline using a fixed XGBoost configuration, fitted and Platt-calibrated separately for each strategy. After Bonferroni correction, 22 of the 28 reconstruction-method pairs showed no statistically significant difference in patient-level AUROC, yet their alert lists overlap at Jaccard 0.518 to 0.750. Similar aggregate discrimination can therefore accompany different patient selections. ForwardFill is a separate outlier, pairing higher discrimination with the lowest overlap in the matrix, 0.40 to 0.45. The broad pattern holds within each hospital system and under six patient-level aggregation rules. At the row level, eight of ten methods fall within an AUROC range of 0.011. In this row-level pipeline, deep imputers did not outperform mean imputation on discrimination, and the sequential implementations cost 5 to 12 times mean imputation in total pipeline wall-clock time on our hardware.

Neural network methods for two-dimensional finite-source reflector design

Roel Hacking, Lisa Kusch, Koondanibha Mitra, Martijn Anthonissen, Wilbert L. IJzerman — 2026-10-15 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1088/2632-2153/aeb503

Abstract We address the inverse problem of designing two-dimensional reflectors that transform light from a finite, extended source into a prescribed far-field distribution. We propose a neural-network parameterization of the reflector height and develop two differentiable objective functions: (i) a direct change-of-variables loss that pushes the source distribution through the learned inverse mapping, and (ii) a mesh-based loss that maps a target-space grid back to the source, integrates over intersections, and remains continuous even when the source is discontinuous. Gradients are obtained via automatic differentiation and optimized with a robust quasi-Newton method. As a comparison, we formulate a deconvolution baseline built on a simplified finite-source approximation: a one-dimensional monotone mapping is recovered from flux balance, yielding an ordinary differential equation solved in integrating-factor form; this solver is embedded in a modified Van~Cittert iteration with nonnegativity clipping and a ray-traced forward operator. Across four benchmarks---continuous and discontinuous sources, and with and without minimum-height constraints---we evaluate accuracy by the ray-traced normalized mean absolute error. Our neural-network approach converges faster and achieves consistently lower errors than the deconvolution method, and handles height constraints naturally. On our two main benchmarks the neural network reduces this error to $2\times 10^{-5}$ for a continuous source and $5\times 10^{-5}$ for a discontinuous source within a few seconds of wall-clock time on a single GPU, against $4\times 10^{-3}$ and $5\times 10^{-2}$ for the deconvolution baseline after several hundred seconds---two to three orders of magnitude lower error at two orders of magnitude less wall-clock time. We discuss how the method may be extended to rotationally symmetric and full three-dimensional settings via iterative correction schemes.

Physics-guided residual inverse-transition modeling for continuum robots with simulation and ordered experimental evaluation

Mohamed Razi Morakchi, Talhi Amar, Abdelhamid Ghoul, Selman Djeffal, Badreddine Bendriss — 2026-10-15 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-026-73975-w

Abstract This study distinguishes two levels that were conflated in the original submission: a tendon-driven mechanical dynamics model used only in simulation, and an ordered one-step inverse-transition estimator used for the recorded experiment. The experimental estimator does not identify force-level inverse dynamics because the supplied dataset contains no verified sampling interval, velocity, acceleration, tendon tension, actuator torque, pneumatic pressure, or contact-force channel. Instead, it predicts the next measured six-channel length-state increment from the present Cartesian state, desired Cartesian increment, and a structured representation of the measured length state. The first-order prior is a global empirical differential map, $$\widehat{{\bf J}}\in \mathbb {R}^{3\times 6}$$ , estimated only from the training block; it has rank 3, condition number 3.49, and singular values 1.425, 1.317, and 0.409. Reviewer-driven ablation further showed that the Clarke-like $$3\times 3$$ transform is a full-rank coordinate change rather than a dimensionality reduction, that appending both raw channels and deterministic modes reweights Gaussian-kernel distances, and that the one-step history term does not improve the held-out experimental prediction. The revised experimental instantiation therefore retains only the four bending coordinates $$(q_{\alpha ,1},q_{\beta ,1},q_{\alpha ,2},q_{\beta ,2})$$ in the nonlinear residual learner and excludes both the common mode and duplicated raw/mode features. On the chronologically held-out 39-transition test block, this bending-mode residual KRR obtains a task-space RMSE of 0.365 mm and a cable/length-state RMSE of 0.745 mm, compared with 0.469 mm for a validation-tuned direct KRR, 0.596 mm for ridge regression, 0.654 mm for the NARX-feature model, and 0.795 mm for the global empirical prior. A four-sample moving-block bootstrap gives a within-trajectory 95% interval of approximately 0.282–0.437 mm for the revised task-space RMSE. A paired sign-randomization test against direct KRR gives $$p\approx 0.060$$ , so the small test block does not justify a claim of statistically conclusive superiority over that strongest nonlinear baseline. The independently learned forward evaluator has a test component RMSE of 0.139 mm and vector RMSE of 0.240 mm, but its larger validation error also reveals chronological distribution shift. Accordingly, the revised claims are restricted to free-space, one-step inverse-transition prediction on the recorded trajectory family; no cross-speed, cross-platform, force/contact, or closed-loop hardware generalization is claimed.

Ashutosh Pandey, Manoj Kumar Mıshra, Lalchand Verma — 2026-10-15 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s11671-026-04841-x

Entropy refers to the randomness and disorderness of the system, which significantly affects the efficiency and performance of thermal systems. The motive of the research article is to carry out the comprehensive analysis of entropy along with the effect of thermal radiation on the Marangoni convective flow of nanofluid over an infinite porous disk using a passive flow control technique. In this analysis, the unit effect thermal slip condition and Stefan's blowing have also been considered. Mathematical modeling has been done with the help of partial differential equation (PDE) which has been converted to a non-linear dimensionless ordinary differential equation (ODE) with the help of Karman's transformations.
