Apart from that, entropy analysis has been carried out for the thermal system. The findings suggest that the Nusselt number increases corresponding to the increasing value of thermal radiation. Also, the entropy of the thermal system increases corresponding to the increasing value of thermal radiation and Brinkman number. This problem has significant potential for real-life applications, viz. material processing and manufacturing, environmental systems, and various industrial processes including cooling and heating operations.

Separating mixtures of single-fiber orientation distributions for increased sensitivity to crossing white matter fibers

Adam M. Saunders, Gaurav Rudravaram, Michael E. Kim, Elyssa M. McMaster, Yifan Shuai — 2026-10-14 — arxiv

DOI : —

Intro: Diffusion MRI allows for in vivo probing of brain tissue architecture via micron-level diffusion aggregated at the millimeter scale, but crossing white matter fibers decrease the sensitivity of downstream tasks such as microstructural modeling and tractography. Current fixel-based methods for identifying the number, orientation, and spread of fibers rely on finding the peaks of fiber orientation distributions from constrained spherical deconvolution. However, this method is biased by the spreads of nearby fibers and fails at angular separations lower than approximately 40-50 degrees. Methods: In this work, we perform a constrained nonlinear optimization called fiber segmentation by signal splitting and iterative least squares estimation (FISSILE), modeling each voxel as a mixture of single-fiber distributions. We use FISSILE to fit axially symmetric fibers in simulations and on three in vivo human subjects from the Human Connectome Project, an ex vivo raccoon brain, as well as the same human subject scanned twice at two different signal-to-noise ratios (SNRs). Results: On simulated data, FISSILE successfully identifies fibers at an angular separation of 40° and a volume fraction of 0.1 at an SNR as low as 30, while MRtrix3's fod2fixel is limited to 60° separation and 0.1 volume fraction at the same SNR. On acquired data, FISSILE identified 85.8% of white matter voxels on average in human brains and 79.4% in the raccoon with at least one fiber crossing, whereas fod2fixel identified 30.1% and 44.5%. The number of white matter fiber crossings was mildly sensitive to different noise levels in the human subject with two acquisitions. Conclusion: A method that models the crossing fiber phenomenon as an unmixing problem increases sensitivity to individual fibers for more precise characterization of anatomy.

Factor-Assisted Density Estimation: When Ambient Dimension Enables Oracle Rates

Sunrit Chakraborty, David Dunson — 2026-10-14 — arxiv

DOI : —

We study density estimation in the high-dimensional factor model, whose fixed-dimensional latent factors have an unknown smooth density. We propose factor-assisted density estimation (FADE), a non-iterative procedure that applies kernel density estimation to spectral estimates of the latent scores. The score proxies are dependent and do not follow the latent density, so standard kernel-density arguments do not apply. A refined rowwise expansion separates error into a conditionally centered Gaussian perturbation and a uniformly controlled remainder. We show that FADE achieves the oracle rate in sufficiently high ambient dimension and prove a minimax lower bound separating ordinary nonparametric difficulty from a Gaussian deconvolution obstruction. Together, the upper and lower bounds establish rate optimality of FADE in specified high-dimensional regimes. We also derive $L^1$ upper and lower bounds for the induced observation density, distinguishing latent-distribution uncertainty from the cost of estimating a growing number of parameters. These results formalize a blessing of ambient dimension: sufficiently many observed coordinates can make a simple spectral reduction statistically lossless even for the nonparametric task of recovering an unobserved low-dimensional density.

A comparison of mixed-models for the analysis of non-linear longitudinal data: application to late-life cognitive trajectories

Maude Wagner, Don Hedeker, Tianhao Wang, Graciela Muniz-Terrera, Ana W. Capuano — 2026-10-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1186/s12874-026-02967-1

Several mixed-effects models for Gaussian longitudinal outcomes have been proposed to accommodate the non-linearity of late-life cognitive trajectories and assess the putative influence of covariates on it. No prior research provides a side-by-side examination of these models to offer guidance on their proper application and interpretation. In this work, we examined five statistical approaches commonly used to answer research questions related to non-linear changes in cognitive aging: the linear mixed model with a quadratic term, the linear mixed model with a function of time approximated with splines, the functional mixed model, the piecewise linear mixed model, and the sigmoidal mixed model. We first theoretically describe the models. Next, we conducted a simulation study to evaluate the models empirically and provide practical recommendations. Lastly, using real data from two prospective cohorts with annual cognitive testing, we compared the interpretation of the models by examining the associations between education and cognitive change before death. Except for the linear mixed model with a quadratic term for time, the fit of all models was generally adequate in capturing the non-linearity of cognitive change, and models were relatively robust. Although spline-based models have time-related parameters that are not directly interpretable, their convergence was easier to achieve, and they allow for graphical interpretation. In contrast, piecewise and sigmoidal models provide directly interpretable parameters but may require more data to achieve convergence. This study provided insights into how each strategy can help better characterize cognitive change over time. By performing simulations, we also emphasized that a quadratic function of time is not recommended in the context of these data. Lastly, we highlighted that more sophisticated models for longitudinal data, such as the piecewise and sigmoidal mixed models, should be considered more frequently in applications than they currently are.

Biphasic Production of 5‐Hydroxymethylfurfural Catalysed by Sulphated Zirconia in H 2 O/Cyclohexanone: Kinetic Modelling and Green Metrics

Dominik Soukup-Carne, Christopher M. A. Parlett, Juan Manuel Bolivar, Xiaolei Fan, Jesús Esteban — 2026-10-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1002/cssc.71114

The production of 5‐hydroxymethylfurfural (HMF) presents the challenge of mitigating the undesired side reactions in its production via fructose dehydration to insoluble humins and rehydration products. For this, biphasic systems for in situ HMF extraction have proven effective. Here, a H 2 O/cyclohexanone biphasic system showed a remarkable partition ratio of 2.91 compared to other biphasic systems to separate HMF as the reaction occurs. Sulphated zirconia, SO 4 2− /ZrO 2 , was used as acid catalyst, chosen among other heterogeneous catalysts through activity and recyclability screening over six cycles, with return to the initial activity upon regeneration. A phenomenological approach to kinetic model development was implemented, wherein a total of 12 reaction models were constructed to investigate the complexity of humin formation. Based on statistical discrimination criteria, a Langmuir–Hinshelwood–Hougen–Watson model with variable reaction orders for the production of humins was deemed the most plausible (RMSE = 0.1075), with activation energy for dehydration of fructose to HMF of 137.97 ± 6.40 kJ mol −1 , and reaction orders towards humin formation between 0.71 and 1.60. The calculation and comparison of green metrics show the promise of this biphasic system to conduct the reaction in the context of a process, although catalyst selectivity remains key to enhancing mass efficiency.

TENSORnet: a thermodynamically motivated entropy protocol for infrastructure-induced metabolic arrest detection in cross-asset financial networks

Ntebogang Dinah Moroke — 2026-10-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.3389/fphy.2026.1943577

Conventional financial risk systems treat physical infrastructure as a fixed, frictionless background, an assumption that breaks down when electricity supply becomes binding. Markets then enter metabolic arrest : information-processing capacity collapses under a documented inversion between cross-asset correlation and entropy. TENSORnet (Temporal Entropy-aware Network for Systemic Onset Recognition) is a thermodynamically motivated protocol that fuses a VIX-calibrated inhibitory gate, grounded in Landauer’s energy-information bound, with cross-asset Shannon entropy. Rather than assuming architectural value, this study directly tests whether the protocol provides classification value beyond infrastructure-stage information alone. TENSORnet—applied to 2,838 JSE trading days (January 2015–April 2026) across seven asset classes under South Africa’s load-shedding crisis, using a three-way train/validation/test split motivated by the documented escalation history (Stage 4 first occurred February 2019)—achieves F1 = 99.55% and AUC = 0.9996 on the held-out test period (2020–2026, 1,581 days) at the primary Stage 3+ definition; a DeLong test finds this statistically indistinguishable from infrastructure-stage information alone ( p = 0.326 ) . Ablation confirms that the gate is load-bearing relative to purely market-based alternatives (AUC collapses to 0.568 without it; GARCH and network-connectedness achieve AUC ≤ 0.62), and the entropy layer outperforms a volatility-based substitute and a gradient-boosted benchmark under matched small-sample conditions, though it does not exceed the stage information itself. A cross-scale divergence signal, proposed as an advance-warning mechanism, is directly tested and does not survive (95% false alarm rate)—reported as a negative result. The densification paradox is empirically supported and confirmed via permutation testing against a mechanical-artefact null: rising cross-asset correlation accompanies falling entropy under infrastructure stress ( r = − 0.468 at Stage 4+ vs. r = − 0.184 at Stage 0, p < 0.001 )—this study’s most robust independent finding. A port-Hamiltonian neural ODE extension specifies an unvalidated conceptual pathway for future research. This study offers a disciplined example of testing, rather than assuming, whether a thermodynamically motivated architecture provides value beyond simpler alternatives, reporting results honestly where they complicate the original hypothesis.

Informing risk stratification and vaccine policy via a predictive mathematical model of SARS-COV-2 infection

Sélectionné · envoyé par email

Benny Chain, Curt Callan, David Scobie, Matthew Cowley, Jana Jang — 2026-10-14 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.10.12.26365315

Abstract SARS-CoV-2 human challenge studies enabled unprecedented high frequency temporal measurements of immune determinants of viral control. We integrated prior knowledge of SARS-CoV-2 replication, innate, cytotoxic T cell and antibody responses into a dynamic model which captured viral-load kinetics in seronegative volunteers challenged with wildtype virus. The model predicted measured innate and adaptive immune dynamics. Perturbation analyses indicated that innate immunity limits peak viral load, whereas antibodies principally mediate clearance while cytotoxic T cells become critical in antibody deficiency. Pre-existing antibodies suppressed both viral replication and subsequent immune activation- a prediction validated in a second challenge study of seropositive participants infected with the SARS-CoV-2 Delta variant. The model explains how specific immunodeficiencies may increase susceptibility to severe or persistent infection, supporting clinical risk stratification. It suggests that T cell-targeted vaccination strategies may provide limited protection without effective neutralising antibodies, and that attenuated boosting after low-dose exposure requires repeated vaccination to reduce breakthrough infections, informing public health policy. One Sentence Summary A mathematical model of SARS-COV-2 infection predicts innate and adaptive immunity, and informs risk stratification and vaccine policy.

Long Term Pediatric Vaccine Tender Scheduling with Quantity Discounts

Nicholas Uhorchak, Ruben Proano, Sandra Eksioglu, Burak Eksioglu — 2026-10-13 — arxiv

DOI : —

Pediatric vaccine procurement for low- and lower-middle-income countries (LICs and LMICs) requires long-term coordination across heterogeneous vaccine markets, competing stakeholder objectives, and uncertain demand, yet tenders are currently scheduled as a series of independent renewals. We extend the Hypothetically Coordinated Vaccine Market (HCVM) framework to schedule multi-year tenders with flexible lengths and all-unit quantity discounts, and compare three objectives: a balanced coordinatingentity model (UG), a social-benefit model that minimizes missed doses (SB), and a producer profit-maximizing model (MP). Using UNICEF ten-year demand forecasts and 30 demand replications across six discount scenarios, we find that the procurement objective, not demand variability, drives cost-to-tender and schedule complexity. Quantity discounts favor large producers, concentrating the market and increasing supply chain vulnerability. Discount effectiveness depends on market maturity rather than valency: pentavalent and hexavalent vaccines are already near their price floor, and among monovalent vaccines only rotavirus responds to discounts, whereas HPV and PCV remain too expensive to compete. Removing pentavalent and hexavalent vaccines from the procurement pool sharply increases missed doses. These results give planners practical guidance on objective selection, discount design, and protecting access to combination vaccines.

Moment-Based Anomaly Detection in High-Dimensional Mixed Data

Davide Ferrari, Andrea Nicolodi — 2026-10-12 — arxiv

DOI : —

We propose an anomaly detection method for high-dimensional mixed-type data that maps heterogeneous features to a common scale via standardized moment-based transformations, yielding interpretable feature-level anomaly scores. These scores are combined through a dependence-aware aggregation step to construct the Mixed-data Influence (MDI) score, accounting for correlations across features and providing a unified measure of how strongly an observation departs from the reference data distribution. By incorporating higher-order moments, the MDI can detect anomalies that arise not only from location shifts, but also from changes in variability, skewness, and other distributional characteristics. The aggregation mechanism allows our method to capture both sparse anomalies concentrated in a small number of features and weak, coordinated deviations distributed across many features. We empirically investigate the distribution and thresholding behavior of the MDI score across different sample sizes, dimensions, dependence structures, and data types. Simulation experiments and two real-data applications, to multi-omics and ESG data, evaluate finite-sample calibration, detection performance, and feature-level interpretability.

Comparative Analysis of Spatial Statistical Methods for Modelling Rice Yield Variability in Uniformity Trials

Adebayo O. P, Ahmed I, Garba I. M, Mustaph A. D — 2026-10-12 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.37394/232028.2026.6.9

Spatial heterogeneity is a fundamental characteristic of agricultural landscapes, and accurately modelling its impact on crop yield is critical for precision agriculture. This study presents a comparative analysis of spatial statistical and machine learning approaches to model yield variability in a field exhibiting strong spatial dependence. Initial geostatistical analysis confirmed significant positive spatial autocorrelation (Moran's I = 0.7355, p < 2.2e-16). A series of models were evaluated, progressing from linear and polynomial trend surfaces to spatial regression (Lag and Error), mixed effects, and a Random Forest ensemble. The results demonstrate that while traditional spatial models effectively captured and explained the spatial autocorrelation, a Random Forest model significantly outperformed all others in predictive accuracy, achieving a 31% lower Root Mean Square Error (36.05) than the best spatial regression model and explaining 71.02% of the variance. Among the spatial models, a quadratic trend surface with a row × column interaction provided the most parsimonious fit, revealing an accelerating yield increase along rows and a U-shaped pattern across columns. The Random Forest model's residuals showed no statistically significant remaining spatial autocorrelation (Moran's I p-value = 0.97); however, we caution that failure to reject spatial randomness does not prove its absence. Equivalence testing (TOST) and power analysis support the conclusion that spatial dependence was adequately captured. We conclude that machine learning ensembles like Random Forest offer superior predictive power for yield mapping, though spatial regression models remain valuable for providing parsimonious, interpretable insights into the underlying structural gradients driving yield variability.

Approximation, Consistency, and Prediction in Generalized Neural Network Mixed Models for Longitudinal and Clustered Data

Hasan Mahmud, Md Moinul Ahsan, Mathias Nthiani Muia, Mous-Abou Hamadou, Chathurika S. Abeykoon — 2026-10-11 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2610.15270

Generalized neural network mixed models (GNMMs) combine neural networks and mixed-effects models to analyze longitudinal and clustered data. Although GNMMs have shown good predictive performance in practice, their statistical properties are not well understood. Therefore, this paper studies several important properties of GNMM and provides theoretical support for its use. First, we show that the neural-network component can approximate an unknown nonlinear fixed-effect function arbitrarily well and establish consistency of the fitted neural- network function for its population target within a given network class. Next, we establish consistency of random-effect estimation under increasing within-subject information and show that the GNMM predictor converges to the true predictor. We then prove consistency of the conditional mean function and show that the excess mean squared prediction error (MSPE) relative to the oracle prediction error converges to zero. We conduct simulation studies to examine nonlinear fixed-effect recovery, random-effect estimation, predictor and conditional mean estimation, and excess prediction error under different numbers of subjects and repeated observations. We also apply GNMM to a longitudinal schizophrenia dataset to predict continuous and binary depression outcomes using smartphone- based behavioral measurements and compare its predictive performance with generalized linear mixed models and conventional artificial neural networks. Both the theoretical and empirical results demonstrate that GNMM provides a flexible framework for modeling nonlinear longitudinal and clustered data. These findings provide a statistical foundation for GNMM prediction and support future methodological developments.

Using forest structural complexity and topography metrics to characterise vertical microclimatic stratification

Sébastien Lhoumeau, Dominik Seidel, Rui B. Elias, Rosalina Gabriel, Paulo A. V. Borges — 2026-10-10 — openalex

DOI : https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ecoinf.2026.104087

Understanding how topography and forest structure drive the transformation of incoming solar energy into local microclimatic conditions is essential for predicting forest resilience under climate change. Quantifying the importance of such attributes on the stratification of microclimates requires the integration of heterogeneous ecological data streams. Here, we combined satellite-derived solar radiation and topographic data, LiDAR-derived forest structural metrics and in situ air temperature and relative humidity time series to quantify the interactions between incoming solar energy, forest structure and topography, ultimately driving microclimatic stratification. Focusing on native and exotic forest stands on Terceira Island, Azores, Portugal, we evaluated how forest structural complexity and topography interact with incoming solar energy to jointly shape daily air temperature and vapour pressure deficit dynamics. By explicitly linking terrain complexity and three-dimensional vegetation structure with external energy inputs and sensor-derived microclimatic responses, our approach provides a data-driven basis for identifying the structural attributes that drive microclimatic variability within forests. Our results show that forest structural complexity and topography shape the vertical gradient of air temperature and atmospheric dryness, but with responses differing among canopy, understory, and ground strata. These findings demonstrate the value of integrating remote sensing, environmental sensor networks, and statistical modelling to resolve biologically relevant microclimatic heterogeneity. More broadly, the workflow developed here offers a transferable framework for microclimate monitoring, forest restoration assessment, and the identification of microrefugia in structurally complex ecosystems.
